# Seven lines, one law: what Hardy–Littlewood's singular series predict

> **In short:** One object, the Hardy–Littlewood correlation functions S(B)/ln^k x, predicts the structure of the primes along seven lines (gap statistics, repeated numbers, residue classes, Ulam's diagonals, the gap sequence as a chain, its information content, reproducibility) with no fitted parameter. Tested in windows from 10⁷ to 10¹⁸, it matches the gap shares in every window and the number of primes in intervals of 30 and 60 to the third or fourth decimal (Poisson is off by up to 0.05); summed exactly, it reproduces the number of occurrences of every gap from 2 to 70 below 4·10¹⁸ (10¹⁴ to 10¹⁶ each, counted by Oliveira e Silva) with differences of the size of the square root of the count (largest 2.0√N), and it puts the change of the most common gap from 6 to 30 at 1.7427435·10³⁵ (the same Hardy–Littlewood computation in the literature gives 1.7427436·10³⁵). The same law predicts twin primes, Sophie Germain primes, prime values of n² + 1 and n² + n + 41, and the nearest neighbours of Gaussian and Eisenstein primes; and, as a check of the explicit formula (a theorem), R(x) and the first 2,419 zeros of ζ reproduce the swings of prime counts in blocks (all within 2σ). On top of it come constructions of ours or of ours in part (listed, with the literature, under *What is new here* below): a conditioned expansion (essentially Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's construction) that sums the law for long gaps, reproducing the counts of every tested gap from 40 to 700 below 4·10¹⁸ to within 0.065 % (0.001 % up to g = 100; the truncated law was 0.55–1.34 % off), accounting for the study's own large-gap finding of round 24 up to a 1.9σ remainder (our synthesis of rounds 51–55; round 51's own reading leaves it open), and giving the distribution of distances between consecutive twin primes up to 1,200 (χ² = 30.7 on 30 bands; the tail beyond misses at z = 3.7, 2.8 with a finer conditioning); a heuristic law for how predictable the next prime is from p's residues mod the primes up to y (roughly 1/(u·ω(u)), u = ln p/ln y; 50 % needs y ≈ p^0.28; out of sample to 0.0016); closed constants for the entropy of the next gap (K = 0.76275) and the information between consecutive gaps (0.26965 bits, and 0.15093 more from the gap before last), with a coupon-collector formula that makes the memory of the gap sequence unbounded at leading order (the past gaps reveal p mod 30030 after 34 gaps on average near 10⁹); a generating-function law for the k-th neighbour of a prime (the residue bias it describes is Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's) that gives the residue repeats k steps apart (116 values at 10⁹–10¹⁵, largest |z| 2.15, 48 at 10¹⁸ within 2.38σ and 20 at 10⁷–10⁸ within 1.98σ), the distance distributions and the last-digit table of consecutive primes (144 entries at 10⁹–10¹⁵ within 2.27σ, 96 of them beyond the gap law); the exact chain of gap laws on classes misses the repeats k ≥ 2 steps apart by up to 17σ at 10⁹ and 56σ at 10¹² and the digit table by up to 27σ; and the rigidity of gap-event counts per residue class. Of 716 pre-registered tests of the law with a pass rule 34 miss, against 10.5 expected by chance; counting the exact sums of round 21 in place of the expansion they correct and the repeated large-gap test once, 16 miss in 658, of which 5 have a found cause, 10 are at chance level and 1 is open: gap classes counted per residue class mod 210 scatter 10–30 % less than independent draws from the calibrated law; the long-range Hardy–Littlewood rigidity accounts for half of that at 10⁷–10⁸ (by round 39's own reading not enough: 0.847 predicted against 0.691 ± 0.06) and for about 70 % and 40 % of the smaller deficits at 10¹⁰ and 10¹⁵ (remainders within the noise), the remainder lies in the counts of each gap class per residue class (about 20 % of the predicted variance in both long windows, 1.6–1.7σ each), and the extra evenness of twin counts at coarse moduli is unresolved at about 2σ (rounds 13, 39–44).

**Code:** [writeup/code/study/](writeup/code/study/). **Data:** [writeup/data/study/](writeup/data/study/) (results, theory values, verdicts, and the pre-registrations written before each round).


**The formula in one place.** Write λ = ln x. For a pattern H = {h₁, …, h_k} (for example {0, 2} for twins):

  P(n + h₁, …, n + h_k all prime, n near x) ≈ S(H) / λ^k,  S(H) = ∏_p (1 − ν_p(H)/p) / (1 − 1/p)^k,

with ν_p(H) the number of different remainders of h₁, …, h_k mod p (Hardy–Littlewood; S({0, 2}) = 1.3203, and S = 0 when some p sees every remainder, as {0, 2, 4} mod 3). Everything in this study follows from it in these ways:
1. *Gaps*: the next prime after p is p + g when p + g is prime and nothing in between is: P(gap = g) = Σ_B (−1)^|B| S({0, g} ∪ B) / λ^(|B|+2), summed over the sets B of points strictly between 0 and g (exact, sections 6 and 10), or its Bahadur expansion (section 3), or, far more accurate for long gaps, the same expansion after averaging exactly over p mod 30030 (rounds 47–55).
2. *Counts in an interval of length H*: Var/mean = 1 + (2/(Hλ)) Σ_{h<H} (H − h)(S({0, h}) − 1), about 1 − (ln H − 1 + γ + ln 2π)/λ (section 4).
3. *Counts per residue class*: the same sum over points 210k apart, with S restricted to the primes p ∤ 210; it makes the counts per class far more even than chance (round 39).
4. *Predicting the next gap*: knowing p mod every prime q ≤ y, the best guess (the first y-rough number after p) is right with probability ⟨S_{>y}({0, h₁})⟩ / (ln p · ∏_{q≤y}(1 − 1/q) · e^γ · ω(u)), u = ln p / ln y, ω Buchstab's function (roughly 1/(u·ω(u)) for large y; a heuristic product of Hardy–Littlewood and Buchstab); about h₂(hit)/hit bits remain (section 15).
5. *The entropy of the next gap*: H = log₂(e·ln p) − K/ln 2 bits with K = ln(2C₂) + Σ_{p>2} ln((p − 1)/(p − 2))/(p − 1) = 0.76275 (round 52).
6. *The information between consecutive gaps*: I → Σ_{p≥3} I_p = 0.26965 bits, a closed sum over primes of local residue averages (round 56; the data approach it slowly, as the discrete leading-order law does, with λ × (I − I_∞) growing at least like ln λ by round 70's theory, so round 56's pre-registered 0.02-bit reading at 10¹² failed; round 68 passes with that comparator).
7. *What the gap before last adds*: I(g_{n+1}; g_{n−1} | g_n) → J_∞ = 0.15093 bits, the same kind of closed sum over primes (round 58).
8. *Memory of the gap sequence*: the last k gaps carry I_k = Σ_q [H_q(1) − E H_q(m_k)] about the next (a coupon-collector sum from a hidden forbidden residue mod each prime), 0.27, 0.42, 0.53, … bits, growing without bound like ln ln k at leading order (x → ∞ first; round 61, theory; on gap residues mod 30 the data follow its shape for k = 1–4 but exceed the registered q = 3, 5 comparator by up to 0.017 bits, so round 62's 0.01-bit reading failed; with q = 7, which the finite-λ law also needs, the differences at k = 1–3 are 0.0014–0.0076 bits). The hidden residues can be read off: a predictor that infers p mod 30030 from the past gaps gains 1.43 bits on the next gap, as the model's own information says (round 72); at finite λ the gaps reveal p sooner than the coupon collector (34.0 gaps instead of 39.7), because consecutive primes repel in residue, which the class-conditioned law reproduces for one step and, through a generating function for the number of primes between p and its k-th neighbour, at every lag tested (k ≤ 4, q ≤ 13 at 10⁹; k ≤ 6, q ≤ 23 at 10¹² and 10¹⁵; largest |z| 2.15; rounds 74, 79, 81, 82, 84), and chains on classes are slow by a lag that a post hoc line in Σ_{q>y} 1/q² fits for y = 7–17, its intercepts matching the data for q = 5–11 (the registered law of round 77 failed, the extrapolation to y = 3 failed; rounds 73–78).
9. *The k-th neighbour*: in each class a mod 30030, with p_i = ρ₀ S_{>13}({0, D, i})/S_{>13}({0, D}) for the rough points i between p and p + D and R_ij their pair ratios, the number of primes between has the generating function G_a(z) = exp(Σ_i ln(1 + p_i(z − 1)) + ½ Σ_{i≠j} R_ij w_i w_j), w_i = p_i(z − 1)/(1 + p_i(z − 1)), and P(p + D is the k-th prime after p) = ρ₀ S_{>13}({0, D}) [z^{k−1}] G_a(z), averaged over the classes (z = 0 is the gap law of item 1 in conditioned form; rounds 81–86). It gives the residue repeats k steps apart, the distribution of p_{n+k} − p_n and the last-digit table of consecutive primes to the counting noise at 10⁹–10¹⁵.

**What is new here**, and what is not (after a literature search on each item; it was not exhaustive):
- *A conditioned expansion of the gap law* (rounds 47–55, 63): average exactly over p mod 30030 (Chinese remainder theorem) and expand only the weak correlations of the larger primes. The construction itself is essentially known: Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan ([arXiv:1603.03720](https://arxiv.org/abs/1603.03720), 2016) condition on n mod q with the same ρ₀ = q/(φ(q) log n) and expand the avoidance of the interior points in the connected singular series of Montgomery and Soundararajan; Granville's model and Banks, Ford and Tao ([arXiv:1908.08613](https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.08613)) contain the averaging over residue classes. What is ours is its use as the expansion point for the whole gap law of long gaps (order 3 with the overlap correction) and the precision tests below. It reproduces the exact sums for the gaps 40–90, the counts of every tested gap 40–700 below 4·10¹⁸ to within 0.065 % (0.001 % up to g = 100; the truncated law: 0.55–1.34 %) and the counts of large gaps at 10⁸–10¹⁰ and, out of sample, at 10¹² (round 63: 4,341 against 4,296); with it the study's own large-gap finding (round 24) is accounted for up to a 1.9σ remainder in the first occurrences (a synthesis; round 51's pre-registered reading says open). Applied to twin events it gives the distribution of the distances between consecutive twin primes up to 1,200, χ² = 30.7 on 30 bands over 2.96 million gaps at 10⁹, against 2,294 for an exponential with the Hardy–Littlewood pair factor; the tail beyond 1,200 misses at z = 3.7 (2.8 with y = 17), the order-2 truncation; out of sample at 10¹¹, χ² = 29.7 on the same 30 bands over 20.5 million gaps, tail +0.24 % (z = 3.1), all of it beyond 2,400, at 10¹³ χ² = 19.3, where the cut at 1,200 is too early for the tail to show the truncation, and at 10¹⁵ χ² = 25.2 (rounds 59, 64, 67, 71; earlier work on twin gaps: Wolf, Kelly–Pilling).
- *A law for the k-th neighbour* (rounds 79, 81–90; the substance, including lag-k pairs and the failure of Markov chains, is Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan's Conjecture 1.4 and remark; what is ours is computational: the generating function per class, its finite-λ evaluation and the tests): with the conditioned expansion, the number of primes between p and p + D has in each class the generating function G_a(z) = exp(Σ ln(1 + p_i(z − 1)) + ½ Σ R_ij w_i w_j); its coefficients give the distribution of the k-th neighbour, and the repeat probabilities k steps apart agree with the primes for k ≤ 4, q ≤ 13 at 10⁹ and k ≤ 6, q ≤ 23 at 10¹² and 10¹⁵ (116 values, largest |z| 2.15; the values share primes and are not independent; rounds 81, 82, 84; 48 more at 10¹⁸ on a small sample, within 2.38σ, round 87; and 20 at 10⁷–10⁸ within 1.98σ, round 90); Hardy–Littlewood-based counts of consecutive primes go back to Gallagher (the Poisson limit) and Goldston–Ledoan (consecutive primes at distance d, arXiv:1111.3380). The Markov chains of gap laws tested miss the repeats k ≥ 2 steps apart by up to 17σ at 10⁹ (round 80) and, for q ≤ 13, 56σ at 10¹² and 34σ at 10¹⁵ (r82c.py); the law also gives the distance distributions (round 83) and the last-digit table of consecutive primes, 144 entries at 10⁹–10¹⁵ within 2.27σ (rounds 85–86), and, applied to twin events, the distance from a twin pair to its 2nd and 3rd next twin pair, where a chain on twin classes misses by χ² ~750 (round 89).
- *The predictability of the next prime* (rounds 45, 46, 50, 72; the ingredients, Buchstab's density of rough numbers and Granville's model, are classical, the predictor framing and the threshold were not found in the literature): knowing p mod every prime up to y, the best guess is right with probability ⟨S_{>y}⟩/(ln p ∏_{q≤y}(1 − 1/q) e^γ ω(u)), u = ln p/ln y (a heuristic product of Hardy–Littlewood and Buchstab; out of sample at 10¹⁵ to 0.0016), and about h₂(hit)/hit bits remain (for y ≳ 100; below that the gap is more predictable). And from the previous gaps alone: an online predictor that infers p mod 30030 from them gains 1.43 bits on the next gap after some 40 gaps, its learning curve within 0.025 of the coupon-collector formula and slightly ahead of it (round 72).
- *Three information constants of the gap sequence* (rounds 52, 56–58, 60–62, 66, 68–70; not found in the literature, the closest being Lacasa et al., Entropy 20 (2018) 131, [arXiv:1802.08349](https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.08349), who use Hardy–Littlewood for block entropies of gap residues without closed constants): the entropy of the next gap is log₂(e ln p) − K/ln 2 bits with K = ln(2C₂) + Σ_{p>2} ln((p − 1)/(p − 2))/(p − 1) = 0.762747, the information one gap carries about the next tends to Σ_{p≥3} I_p = 0.26965 bits, and the gap before last adds J_∞ = 0.15093 bits; all three are closed sums over primes, and one coupon-collector formula, from the random-sieve reading of the singular series, gives the information carried by the last k gaps for every k, unbounded as ln ln k at leading order (round 61), and the same construction gives the entropy of the gap between consecutive twin primes, with K_tw = 0.11434 (round 60). The data approach them slowly (the pre-registered readings of rounds 56 and 62 failed, the second mostly because its comparator left out the prime 7; with the right comparator fixed in advance, rounds 68 and 69 at 10¹² pass), following the discrete leading-order law at each λ; for K the full law was computed out to λ = 100 (0.004 bits from it there), and a fit puts its limit within 0.002 bits.
- *The rigidity of counts per residue class* (rounds 36, 39–44): the long-range Hardy–Littlewood sums predict, with no fitted parameter, how evenly gap events spread over the classes mod 210 (four-point sums; not found in the literature). For the primes themselves the mechanism is known: Friedlander and Goldston (Quart. J. Math. 47, 1996) derive the variance across classes from a prime-pair conjecture (for large q), and for small q it is the explicit formula with L-function zeros. A remainder of about 20 % of the variance of gap-event counts and the evenness of twin counts at coarse moduli stay open at about 2σ.

**Status:** computed. The Hardy–Littlewood and Bateman–Horn conjectures are conjectures; every prediction below assumes them. The Bahadur expansion is our own derivation and the conditioned expansion essentially Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's (section 6); both are heuristic (the series are asymptotic and truncated; section 3 gives the error of order 4 against the exact inclusion–exclusion sum for gaps up to 42; round 28 extends the comparison to gap 90). No parameter was fitted. The tables of the main rounds are printed by [tables.py](writeup/code/study/tables.py) from the verdict files and checked by validate.py; all other tables are typed from their json files and were checked by the reviews.

**Contents.** 1 method and windows · 2 the textbook formulas (round 1) · 3 the law: Hardy–Littlewood plus the Bahadur expansion (round 2) · 4 counts on a grid · 5 Ulam's diagonals (round 9) · 6 the law in all windows: gaps, classes, variance, information, tails, the fifth order, the exact gap law, every gap below 4·10¹⁸, large gaps (first occurrences, counts below 4·10¹⁸, far tails), 10¹⁸, jumping champions (rounds 3, 8, 22, 23, 24–30, 32, 33, 38, 47–49, 51, 53–55, 63, 17, 19) · 7 twin primes, and the gaps between consecutive twins and their entropy (rounds 4, 59, 60, 64, 65, 67, 71, 89) · 8 n² + 1 (round 5) · 9 second differences, bits per prime, the hidden residue chain (rounds 6–7) · 10 counts in intervals: third cumulant, the full distribution, exact sums, long intervals, the pair excess, the zeros of ζ (rounds 10, 12, 20, 21, 34, 35, 37) · 11 Gaussian and Eisenstein primes (rounds 11, 16) · 12 the residue chain cell by cell, rigidity of twin counts, the rigidity of the tables derived, three-gap law (rounds 13, 31, 36, 39–44, 15) · 13 Sophie Germain primes and n² + n + 41 (rounds 14, 18) · 14 synthesis and the scoreboard of all tests · 15 how predictable the next prime is, the entropy of the next gap, the information between consecutive gaps, how fast the past gaps reveal p, and the k-th neighbour law (rounds 45, 46, 50, 52, 56–58, 61, 62, 66, 68–70, 72–133) · Validation.

## 1. The question and the method

The protocol asked for seven independent lines (numerical, geometric, statistical, algebraic, dynamic, information-theoretic, computational) and for every result to be checked against known theory: the prime number theorem, residue classes, analytic number theory.

- **Windows.** train = [10⁷, 10⁸) (5,096,876 primes); A = [10¹⁰, 10¹⁰ + 5·10⁷) (2,171,134); B = [10¹², 10¹² + 5·10⁷) (1,808,833); C = [10¹⁵, 10¹⁵ + 2·10⁷) (578,837; opened only in round 3). Each window is split into 10 equal blocks.
- **The test.** For a statistic s with prediction T: r_b = s_b − T_b on the ten blocks, z = mean(r)/(sd(r)/√10). The theory value is recomputed per block (λ = ln x changes inside a window). The prediction fits when |z| ≤ 3. The noise comes from the primes themselves, not from a model.
- **No sieve models.** Fake primes built from a sieve explain nothing about why the primes behave as they do, so the only fakes are coin flips with chance 1/ln n (Cramér) and the window's own gaps in random order. They answer one question: is there structure at all.
- **Pre-registration.** Before each round a file in [preregister/](writeup/data/study/preregister/) fixed the formulas, the cut-offs and the pass rule; the commit that adds each file comes before the commit with its results (rounds 1–11: f802663, 3f6d791, ee457c0, f9d6fee, 4a15168, 6de4fb1, 204ca7e, 8cd9279, 5cbecb5, 520ab35, 92d808c). The clock times written inside the files are estimates and run 10–25 minutes later than these commits. The commit order shows the order of committing; since some result commits followed their pre-registration within a minute, the repository alone cannot prove that each plan was fixed before its computation, and the pre-registration should be read as stated, not as verified. Round 2 was built on the train window (single-gap shares only) and then run on A and B unchanged.
- **Added after seeing data** (marked where they appear): the grid term of the variance (section 4; round 2 had registered the unaligned pair sum, which missed H = 50 at train: 0.70049 against 0.70526), the variance law along quadratics (section 5; tested afterwards on fresh data in round 9), and the estimator-bias correction of the information statistics (section 6; the registered test missed at C before it).

## 2. Round 1: the textbook formulas ([round1.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round1.txt), [verdicts_r1.json](writeup/data/study/verdicts_r1.json))

The crude Gallagher–Hardy–Littlewood law says a prime near x is followed by the gap h with chance S(0, h)·e^(−h/ln x)/ln x. Montgomery and Soundararajan give Var/mean = 1 − (ln H − 1 + γ + ln 2π)/ln x for counts in [n, n + H). Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan-type class shares follow by summing the gap law over h ≡ 0 (mod q). Bateman–Horn gives the expected count on each of 198 irreducible quadratics 4n² + bn + c (b = −4 … 4 even, c odd ≤ 79; only 4n² ± 4n + 1 = (2n ± 1)² is left out), the curves Ulam's spiral draws as diagonals. (The pre-registration of round 1 says 195; that was a miscount in the text, the code used all 198 from the start.)

| statistic | primes (train) | round-1 formula | rel. diff | z train | z A | z B |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| two equal gaps in a row | 0.028487 | 0.019565 | +45.6 % | 56.5 | 47.6 | 48.6 |
| P(next gap larger) − P(smaller) | -4.5126e-05 | 0 |  | -0.1 | -1.8 | 1.8 |

- Every line shows structure far beyond coin flips: against the coins |z| > 8 for every statistic except the up/down symmetry, the races (too noisy, see below), the information between gap classes 5 and 10 steps apart (and 3 steps apart at B), which is zero for the primes too and, at B only, the scatter on the quadratics ([verdicts_r1.json](writeup/data/study/verdicts_r1.json)).
- What fits: the reflection symmetry of consecutive gaps (a theorem about the singular series: S is invariant under x → span − x), the Bateman–Horn means (slope 0.99985), and Montgomery–Soundararajan for H = 500 and 5000.
- What misses: every gap share (by 8–30 %), the equal gaps (46 %), the class shares (0.6–2.5 %), the variance at H = 50, and the scatter on the quadratics, which is 0.39 of Poisson instead of 1.
- The race statistics (slope of π(x;q,N) − π(x;q,R) on √x/ln x) came out 1.14, 1.32, 1.31 against the Rubinstein–Sarnak mean 1 at train; inside a window of 5·10⁷ at 10¹⁰ the race moves by about 11 primes against a noise of about 1,500, so the holdouts cannot test it. The race itself is explained by the zeros in [FINDINGS.txt](FINDINGS.txt) (line on E(x) up to 2·10⁹) and is not repeated here.

## 3. Round 2: one law for consecutive primes ([theory2.py](writeup/code/study/theory2.py), [round2.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round2.txt))

**Step 1 (conjecture: Hardy–Littlewood).** For a finite set B, the numbers n + b (b ∈ B) are all prime with density S(B)/λ^|B|, λ = ln n, S(B) = ∏_p (1 − ν_p(B)/p)/(1 − 1/p)^|B|, ν_p = number of residues of B mod p. Computation: for p larger than the span of B every residue is distinct, so S(B) = ∏_(p ≤ span) (…) · G_k(span) with G_k(P) = ∏_(p > P) (1 − k/p)/(1 − 1/p)^k computed once (primes to 2·10⁷ plus the tail k(k−1)/(2X ln X)). Check: S({0, 2}) = 1.3203236, twice the twin prime constant 0.6601618 (computed).

**Step 2 (identity).** B is a run of consecutive primes when its interior I = {0 < m < span, m ∉ B} has no prime. By inclusion–exclusion, P(B) = Σ_(A ⊆ I) (−1)^|A| S(B ∪ A)/λ^(|B|+|A|). Divided by S(B)/λ^|B|, this is the chance that a 0/1 process X_m on I with correlation functions E∏_(m∈A) X_m = S(B ∪ A)/(S(B) λ^|A|) avoids every point.

**Step 3 (a wrong turn, kept because it explains the size of the misses).** A first version exponentiated the Ursell functions of distinct interior points, exp(−Σκ₁ + Σκ₂ − …), with κ the connected parts of the correlation functions over distinct points. On the train window this converged, but to the wrong values: 1.6 times too high at gap 90. The exponential formula for set functions assumes the blocks of a partition can overlap; for 0/1 variables X² = X, and the diagonal terms are lost. (The true factorial-cumulant series of the number of interior primes, ln P(N = 0) = Σ(−1)^k κ_[k]/k!, is exact and contains these diagonal terms; version 1 was not that series.) At gap 6 the interior is {2, 4} and S(0, 2, 4, 6) = 0, so the exact chance is 1 − x − y (x = ρ₂/λ, y = ρ₄/λ), while the exponential gives e^(−x−y−xy).

**Step 4 (derived; heuristic truncation).** Expand around independent interior numbers instead. With p_m = ρ_m/λ, ρ_m = S(B ∪ {m})/S(B), and Z_m = (p_m − X_m)/(1 − p_m), which has mean 0, exactly

  E∏(1 − X_m) = ∏(1 − p_m) · E∏(1 + Z_m) = ∏(1 − p_m) · Σ_A E[∏_(m∈A) Z_m].

The single terms vanish. The connected parts of the pair, triple and quadruple terms are c_ab/((1 − p_a)(1 − p_b)), −κ₃/∏(1 − p) and +κ₄/∏(1 − p), where c, κ₃, κ₄ are the joint cumulants of the X's (set-partition formulas in `Pattern.log_avoid`). Exponentiating the connected parts:

  ln P(B) = ln S(B) − |B| ln λ + Σ ln(1 − p_m) + Σ c̃ − Σ κ̃₃ + Σ κ̃₄.

At gap 6 the exact value is (1 − x)(1 − y)(1 + t) with t = −xy/((1 − x)(1 − y)), while the exponentiated form gives (1 − x)(1 − y)e^t: a relative error of 9·10⁻⁶ at λ = 17.8, so the overlap problem is pushed to higher order, not removed. The share per prime is P(B)·λ, averaged over the window with weight 1/ln t (integrated in u = ln t).

**Step 5 (computed, train only, the development data).** The prediction divided by the actual share at train: gap 30 gives 1.165 / 1.017 / 1.005 / 1.003 at orders 1 to 4; gap 90 gives 2.06 / 1.09 / 1.01 / 0.99. Order 1 alone (independent interior numbers with their Hardy–Littlewood densities) is the form already used in [FINDINGS.txt](FINDINGS.txt) for Euler–Rodríguez chains; it is 1–16 % off at gaps 6–30. Order 4 was frozen for spans ≤ 120 and order 3 above, before the holdouts were opened.

**The truncation error (from the review; independent code in [review/c3_bahadur.py](writeup/code/study/review/c3_bahadur.py) → [c3_bahadur.out](writeup/data/study/review/c3_bahadur.out), [c3_bahadur_big.out](writeup/data/study/review/c3_bahadur_big.out)).** For gaps up to 42 the live interior has at most 20 points, so the exact Hardy–Littlewood inclusion–exclusion over all its subsets can be summed. Order 4 lies above the exact value by 0.017 % (gap 12), 0.062 % (18), 0.185 % (30), 0.254 % (36) and 0.322 % (42) at λ = 17.8, and by 0.012 % at gap 42 at λ = 34.5. So the agreement of 0.1–0.3 % with the primes at train is at the size of the truncation error itself, and the error grows with h/λ: it is the natural suspect for the tail miss of section 6. **The error is a bias, not only truncation** ([r22c.py](writeup/code/study/r22c.py) → [r22c.json](writeup/data/study/r22c.json)): against the exact sums of round 22, orders 3 and 4 at λ = 17.8 are +0.72 % and +0.69 % off at gap 52 and +1.41 % and +1.31 % at gap 70, and Aitken's acceleration of orders 2–4 changes little (+0.69 %, +1.31 %). At gaps 52 and 70 orders 3 and 4 agree with each other but not with the exact law, so there the series converges to a wrong value: exponentiating the connected parts adds products of overlapping clusters (κ(a, b)·κ(a, c)) that the true sum over disjoint blocks does not have (at other gaps the orders still move, and the sign of these terms is not general: the correction below is positive at g = 300). The Bahadur form makes these terms small (second order in the connected correlations) without removing them; the exact sums of rounds 21–23 have no such bias. The next term of the polymer (cluster) expansion of the true sum over disjoint blocks, −½ [Σ_B κ̃_B² + Σ_i Σ_(j≠k) κ̃_ij κ̃_ik] for pair clusters, removes most of it: at λ = 17.8 the bias at gap 70 falls from +1.3 % to +0.26 %, at λ = 23 from +0.37 % to +0.05 % (`overlap_correction` in theory2.py; checked against the exact sums, no data involved).

**Everything else is a sum over patterns.** The share of gap h is P({0, h})·λ; two equal gaps sum P({0, h, 2h}) over h; the class share mod q sums the gap law over q | h and divides by the total (the law sums to 1.0010, 1.0005, 1.0003 and 1.0002 at train, A, B and C); the joint law of two consecutive gaps is P({0, h₁, h₁ + h₂}); mutual information, entropies and the Markov gain are functions of the joint law.

## 4. Counts on a grid ([theory2.py](writeup/code/study/theory2.py), `var_ratio_aligned`)

The variance of the count in [n, n + H) needs only pairs. With the exact Hardy–Littlewood pair sum, Var/mean = 1 − 1/λ + (2/(Hλ)) Σ_(h<H) (H − h)(S(0, h) − 1). This still missed H = 50 (0.7005 against 0.7053). The reason is the grid: the intervals start at multiples of H, so position a inside every interval is the same residue mod 2 and mod 5, and only the pairs (a, a + h) inside one interval count. With u(a) = 1 when n + a is prime to 10, R(h) the pair factor without the primes 2 and 5 (R(h) = R₀ ∏_(p|h, p≠2,5) (p − 1)/(p − 2), R₀ = C₂/(0.6/0.64)) and c = 1/(½·⅘)²:

  Var/mean = 1 + (2c Σ_h R(h) N_h − H²)/(Hλ),  N_h = Σ_a u(a) u(a + h).

(Derivation: E c² = Σ_a p_a + 2 Σ_(a<b) E[X_a X_b] with p_a = c^(1/2) u(a)/λ and E[X_a X_b] = c u(a) u(b) R(b − a)/λ²; Σ_a p_a = H/λ.) For H = 50 this gives 0.70556 / 0.77451 / 0.81207 at train / A / B against 0.70526 / 0.77551 / 0.81187. The H = 50 values of A and B had been seen in round 1 when this was derived; the formula has nothing to tune, and C (round 3) was not seen.

## 5. Ulam's diagonals scatter less than Poisson ([theory_geo.py](writeup/code/study/theory_geo.py))

On each quadratic f the count of prime values has mean Σ_n C(f)/ln f(n) (Bateman–Horn), and round 1 found that the counts scatter with χ²/curve = 0.386 instead of 1. The same pair argument as in section 4, with polynomials:

  Var/E = 1 − C/λ + (2/(C·N·λ)) Σ_d (N − d)(C_d − C²)  (λ-weights done exactly per n in the code),

where C_d is the Bateman–Horn constant of the pair {f(x), f(x + d)}: C_d/C² = ∏_p (1 − ω_d(p)/p)/(1 − w(p)/p)², w(p) the number of roots of f mod p and ω_d(p) = |R ∪ (R − d)|. For large p the factor is generic (ω = 2w) except at p | d (ω = w: the shifted curve has the same roots) and at p | 16d² − D, D = b² − 16c (ω = 3: one root of f(x + d) is a root of f(x)). For 4n² + 1 the neighbours repel: C₁/C² = 0.5175 (brute-force product to 3,000: 0.51751). The predicted χ²/curve is 0.406 at train, 0.694 at A and 0.831 at B, against 0.386, 0.715 and 0.721 (block z −0.0, 1.1, −1.0). This is a Montgomery–Soundararajan-type law for a curve instead of an interval: the counts are rigid because values a short step apart along the curve share prime factors in a structured way. The law was derived after round 1 had shown the low scatter, so the three windows above are not an independent test; round 9 below is. The collision primes p | 16d² − D are included up to 3·10⁴; including all of them moves the train value by about +0.001 (review, c5_ulam.out).

**Round 9: fresh data and a second family** ([r9.py](writeup/code/study/r9.py), [round9.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round9.txt), [r9.json](writeup/data/study/r9.json)). For a general f = ax² + bx + c the coincidence rule reads a²d² ≡ D (mod p). On n ∈ [10⁶, 1.05·10⁶), every value tested by gmpy2 (no sieve), the pre-registered test compares the mean of (obs − E)²/E over the curves with the mean predicted Var/E, the noise taken from the scatter over the curves:

| family | curves | prime values | χ²/curve | law | z | Bateman–Horn: mean (obs − E)/E, z |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ulam diagonals 4n² + bn + c (all 198) | 198 | 681,550 | 0.499 | 0.542 | -0.9 | -0.00015, -0.2 |
| Ulam diagonals, the 159 distinct curves | 159 | 547,321 | 0.531 | 0.542 | -0.2 | +0.00039, 0.3 |
| Euler's n² + n + c, c odd ≤ 199 | 100 | 361,921 | 0.494 | 0.522 | -0.4 | +0.00074, 0.5 |

Both fit. Since 4n² + 4n + c = f₋₄,c(n + 1), 39 of the 198 Ulam curves are shifted copies of others; the middle row keeps only the 159 distinct curves, so that the scatter over curves is not counted twice (review, point 10). The counts along a quadratic scatter at about half the Poisson value at this size, as the pair constants predict. The Bateman–Horn constants run from 0.62 to 6.64 (n² + n + 41) in both families; the test is on the mean over the curves, not curve by curve.

- Gap classes are c = min(g/2, 20). The information quantities are estimators; their z is computed after subtracting the estimator bias measured by drawing samples of the block size from the law's own joint distribution ([bias_r3.py](writeup/code/study/bias_r3.py)). Before that correction they gave z = −3.9 (mutual information) and −6.0 (conditional entropy) at C, where a block holds only 58,000 primes for 400 cells.
- The Markov gain of the dynamic line (a cross-validated log-loss gain) was compared with the mutual information in round 2 and missed in all three windows (z = −3.2, −5.4, −8.7, [verdicts_theory_r2.json](writeup/data/study/verdicts_theory_r2.json)). It is not an estimate of the mutual information: it equals the mutual information minus a finite-sample penalty of about K(K − 1)/(2N ln 2) bits (3.4·10⁻⁴ at B, where the shortfall is 3.9·10⁻⁴) and minus the drift between the two halves of a window, and it is not used further.
- The bias correction of the information statistics was added after the registered test had missed at C (t₉ = −3.9 and −6.0) and at B (conditional entropy, −3.0); it uses only the law, not the data.

**The tail** (gaps of at least 3, 4 and 5 times ln x):

| window | ln x | gaps ≥ 3 ln x: primes / law, z | ≥ 4 ln x | ≥ 5 ln x |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| train | 17.8 | h ≥ 54: 0.03549 / 0.03583, -4.1 | h ≥ 72: 0.009629 / 0.009754, -2.3 | h ≥ 90: 0.002803 / 0.002843, -1.4 |
| A | 23.0 | h ≥ 70: 0.03806 / 0.03809, -0.3 | h ≥ 92: 0.0117 / 0.01166, 0.6 | h ≥ 116: 0.00347 / 0.003502, -0.8 |
| B | 27.6 | h ≥ 82: 0.04254 / 0.0425, 0.3 | h ≥ 110: 0.01345 / 0.01334, 1.4 | h ≥ 138: 0.004216 / 0.004241, -0.7 |
| C | 34.5 | h ≥ 104: 0.04137 / 0.04138, -0.1 | h ≥ 138: 0.01405 / 0.01427, -1.4 | h ≥ 172: 0.004492 / 0.004612, -1.3 |

The one miss is the tail at train beyond 3 ln x: the law is 0.9–1.4 % too high for gaps of 54 and more (the tails beyond 4 and 5 ln x pass the test there). There λ ≈ 17.8 and h/λ ≥ 3, where the series is clearly asymptotic: at gap 90 the step from order 3 to order 4 is still 2.4 %. At 10¹⁰ and above, the same h/λ fits.

**Round 8: the next order** ([r8.py](writeup/code/study/r8.py), [round8.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round8.txt), [r8_tail.json](writeup/data/study/r8_tail.json), [r8_blocks.json](writeup/data/study/r8_blocks.json)). The fifth cumulant, κ₅ = Σ_π (−1)^(|π|−1) (|π|−1)! ∏_(blocks) μ(block) over the 52 set partitions of five interior points, enters with the sign (−1)⁵; every partition carries λ⁻⁵, so κ₅ = K₅/λ⁵ with K₅ built from singular series alone. Added for spans 50–120 (and order 4 in place of order 3 for spans 122–200), it is about half the order-4 term at every gap (gap 90 at λ = 17.8: −0.037 at order 4, −0.018 at order 5, in ln P), and it lowers the train tail by 0.29 % (gaps ≥ 54: 0.035827 → 0.035722; primes 0.035494). The block t₉ goes from −4.09 to −2.80 (≥ 54), −2.30 to −1.19 (≥ 72) and −1.41 to −0.37 (≥ 90). The pre-registration expected a shift of about 1 %; it is 0.29 %, enough to bring the test inside |t₉| ≤ 3 but leaving 0.64 % (2.8 binomial standard errors) to the higher orders, which keep the same sign, or to chance. With the exact-sum comparison of section 3 (order 4 is 0.32 % above the exact law already at gap 42 and λ = 17.8, growing with h), the approximation of the expansion was the plausible cause of the rest; round 22 shows it (it is a bias of the expansion rather than plain truncation, section 3).

**Round 22: the exact gap law** ([r22.py](writeup/code/study/r22.py) → [r22_HM52.json](writeup/data/study/r22_HM52.json), [r22_HM70.json](writeup/data/study/r22_HM70.json), [round22.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round22.txt)). The enumeration of round 21 also gives the gap law without truncation: for B = {0, h} the admissible interior subsets (no prime's residues all covered by B ∪ A) are listed depth-first, 1,771,344 of them for all h ≤ 70, and with U_j(h) = Σ_{|A|=j} S(B ∪ A) the share of gap h is Σ_j (−1)^j U_j(h)/λ^(j+1), exact. The tail beyond 70 is one minus their sum: splitting the inclusion–exclusion for "no prime in (0, 72)" by the largest point is a finite telescoping identity (checked to 10⁻¹⁶ in the third review, [review3/hl_enum.py](writeup/code/study/review3/hl_enum.py)). At train every gap share from 2 to 70 fits (35 gap shares and the tail beyond 70: 36 block tests, none above 3), and the tails become: gaps ≥ 54, 0.035653 in the data against 0.035704 (t₉ = -0.63, where order 4 gave −4.1 and order 5 −2.8); gaps ≥ 72, 0.0096909 against 0.0096907 (t₉ = 0.004). The train-tail miss was the bias of the Bahadur expansion; the exact Hardy–Littlewood law has no miss at train. Since the sums U_j(h) do not depend on λ, the same exact law was then run on the other windows (pre-registered addendum): 2 misses in 108 tests (gap 44 at A, t₉ = 4.6, gap 34 at B, t₉ = −3.1; 1.6 expected by chance), and the share of gaps ≥ 72 is 0.0341 against 0.0340 at A, 0.0644 against 0.0643 at B, 0.1175 against 0.1175 at C. (The block means here are unweighted means over the ten blocks, for the data and the law alike, so they differ slightly from the pooled values of the table above.)

**Round 23: the exact law against every gap below 4·10¹⁸** ([r23.py](writeup/code/study/r23.py) → [r23.json](writeup/data/study/r23.json), [round23.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round23.txt); the counts in [oes_gap_counts_4e18.json](writeup/data/study/oes_gap_counts_4e18.json)). Tomás Oliveira e Silva's table (linked from the page [sweet.ua.pt/tos/gaps.html](http://sweet.ua.pt/tos/gaps.html); a local copy is in references/web/, not in git) gives N(g), the number of times each gap g occurs between consecutive primes below 4·10¹⁸. Since the sums U_j(g) of round 22 do not depend on λ, the exact law gives N(4·10¹⁸; g) = N(10⁶; g) + ∫ Σ_j (−1)^j U_j(g)/ln^(j+2) t dt over [10⁶, 4·10¹⁸] (the counts below 10⁶ directly, since the law is asymptotic; the integrals with mpmath to 30 digits, the singular-series constants good to about 2·10⁻¹⁰, which is below 0.01√N here). The counts are between 10¹⁴ and 10¹⁶, and the exact Hardy–Littlewood law reproduces every one of them to a relative 5.44·10⁻⁸ at most (gap 68); the differences lie within 2.0√N (the mean square of (count − law)/√count is 0.76). That is the size of the counts' own scatter if that scatter is √N-like (an empirical assumption: π(x) itself deviates from li(x) by far less than √π(x)); gaps 2 and 4 have the same law, so they are not independent tests. The page notes that Hardy–Littlewood estimates of N(x; g) stay within √(2N ln ln N) of the counts; the law here does so for every gap (largest ratio 0.74). This is an agreement of two Hardy–Littlewood computations with the same data, so it confirms the enumeration and the integrals rather than anything new about the conjecture.

**Round 24: first occurrences of large gaps** ([r24.py](writeup/code/study/r24.py) → [r24.json](writeup/data/study/r24.json), [round24.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round24.txt); data [oes_first_occurrence.json](writeup/data/study/oes_first_occurrence.json)). The same table lists P(g), the first prime followed by a gap of g. If gaps g arrive as a Poisson process with the law's rate, U_g = N_law(P(g); g), the expected number of such gaps before the first one, is exponential with mean 1. For the 138 even gaps from 180 to 454 (first occurring between 1.7·10⁷ and 4.7·10¹¹, at 9.9 to 18.3 times ln P(g); the law's cache ends at 454, so 456 was left out), the cluster law gives a mean of 1.27 ± 0.11 (pass: within 3 standard errors of 1), but the Kolmogorov–Smirnov test fails (p = 0.002; median 0.98 against ln 2 = 0.69). The fourth review showed what this means ([review4/fo_stats.py](writeup/code/study/review4/fo_stats.py) → [fo_stats.out](writeup/data/study/review4/fo_stats.out)): dividing every U by one common factor 1.27 (the maximum-likelihood scale) gives a KS p of 0.47, so the shape of the distribution is exponential and only the scale is off: the law's rate for these gaps is about 27 % too high (95 % interval 8–52 %). Dependence between the first occurrences is not the cause: they lie at least 7·10⁵ apart, the rank correlation of U_g and U_(g+2) is 0.05, and a long gap contains no smaller gap. The crude law S(0, g)·e^(−g/ln x)/ln x is off by a factor of about 15 (mean 15.3). **Round 25** ([r25.py](writeup/code/study/r25.py) → [r25.json](writeup/data/study/r25.json), [round25.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round25.txt)) added the second-order polymer correction of section 3 to the law for every gap. It moves the mean of U by 0.6 % (single values by up to 10 % in both directions; mean 1.28 ± 0.11, KS p = 0.003), so this term is not the cause; for g = 300 it even raises the rate. The higher polymer terms and the order-3 truncation at these spans (66 to 209 live interior points) were not tested, and a geometric extrapolation of the order-2 and order-3 terms suggests the truncation leaves only a few per cent. The cause of the 27 % is open.

**Round 26: the law for large gaps, measured directly** ([r26.py](writeup/code/study/r26.py) → [r26.json](writeup/data/study/r26.json), [round26.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round26.txt)). The same table gives N(4·10¹⁸; g) for every gap. With the law of round 3 plus the overlap correction on a λ grid reaching ln(4·10¹⁸), the counts of all 192 even gaps from 72 to 454 (from 1.1·10¹⁵ down to 2.5·10¹⁰ occurrences) come out within −1.9 % to +0.01 % of the counts (the law is high, by 0.38 % on average; the largest deviation, −1.9 %, at g = 444). At these sizes the law's rate for large gaps is right to within 2 % (0.4 % on average); in units of the counts' own scatter that is still far off (hundreds to tens of thousands of √N), so beyond gap 70 the expansion is a 1 %-level approximation, not the exact law. The deviation is larger for gaps divisible by 6 (-0.73 % on average against -0.21 % for the others), whose interiors have more live points, which points to the expansion. Round 26 cannot separate the smaller x of the first occurrences from their larger g/ln x (here g/ln x runs only to 10.6), so the 27 % of round 24 stays open.

**Round 27: the far tail at small λ** ([r27.py](writeup/code/study/r27.py) → [r27.json](writeup/data/study/r27.json), [round27.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round27.txt)). Counting the gaps of at least 6, 8 and 10 times ln x in the four windows, against the law with the overlap correction (count / expected, ratio):

| window | ≥ 6 ln x: gaps / law | ≥ 8 ln x | ≥ 10 ln x |
|---|---|---|---|
| train | 4373 / 4473.2 (0.98) | 298 / 336.7 (0.88) | 15 / 26.4 (0.57) |
| A | 2473 / 2540.7 (0.97) | 217 / 219.1 (0.99) | 18 / 19.3 (0.93) |
| B | 2393 / 2333.8 (1.03) | 228 / 218.3 (1.04) | 19 / 22.2 (0.86) |
| C | 809 / 841.6 (0.96) | 91 / 90.2 (1.01) | 5 / 8.6 (0.58) |

Pooled over the windows, the 10 ln x tail has 57 gaps against 76.6 expected (ratio 0.74, z = −2.2), but the deficit comes from train (15 against 26.4) and C (5 against 8.6); A and B, where λ is 23 to 27.6 like most of the first occurrences, give 37 against 41.5 at 10 ln x and 445 against 437.3 at 8 ln x. With 12 cells, a smallest p of 0.012 has a chance of about 0.13. So this round does not locate the large-gap discrepancy; it stays open.

**Round 28: how large is the expansion's bias for longer gaps?** (diagnostics after round 27; [r28.py](writeup/code/study/r28.py) → [r28.json](writeup/data/study/r28.json), [r28b.py](writeup/code/study/r28b.py) → [r28b.json](writeup/data/study/r28b.json)). The exact sums can be pushed to single gaps of 90 by streaming the enumeration (up to 27 million admissible interior sets per gap); the error of the expansion against them:

| gap h | admissible interior sets | order 4, λ = 17.8 | order 4 + overlap, λ = 17.8 | order 4, λ = 23 | order 4 + overlap, λ = 23 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 74 | 733,900 | +1.30 % | +0.31 % | +0.36 % | +0.06 % |
| 78 | 2,581,763 | +0.73 % | +0.69 % | +0.15 % | +0.15 % |
| 82 | 2,333,308 | +1.62 % | +0.37 % | +0.45 % | +0.07 % |
| 86 | 5,516,720 | +1.71 % | +0.47 % | +0.46 % | +0.09 % |
| 90 | 27,218,840 | +1.12 % | +0.94 % | +0.24 % | +0.20 % |

Up to gap 90 the bias stays below 2 % at train, far too small for the far-tail shortfall. But the round-3 law used order 3 for spans above 120, and round 8's order-4 terms for spans 122–200 are large at train; with them the train tails change from ratios 0.978 / 0.885 / 0.567 to 0.988 / 0.930 / 0.603 (6, 8, 10 ln x). So truncation at long spans explains part of the train-tail deficit of round 27 (its effect on the first occurrences is computed in round 32); the 10 ln x tail (gaps ≥ 178, only 15 of them, mostly beyond span 200 where order 3 remains) is still low, and the first-occurrence result stays open.

**Round 29: does the error grow with g/ln x?** ([r29.py](writeup/code/study/r29.py) → [r29.json](writeup/data/study/r29.json), [round29.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round29.txt)). The 4·10¹⁸ table also counts gaps far beyond 454. For a sample of 11 gaps from 460 to 700 (g/ln x from 10.7 to 16.3, the range of the first occurrences), the same law as in round 26:

| gap g | g/ln x at 4·10¹⁸ | counted | law | (count − law)/count |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 460 | 10.7 | 29,560,946,024 | 29,723,543,696 | -0.6 % |
| 482 | 11.3 | 10,867,009,498 | 10,941,285,988 | -0.7 % |
| 500 | 11.7 | 9,490,713,172 | 9,554,914,676 | -0.7 % |
| 520 | 12.1 | 6,254,193,891 | 6,298,956,589 | -0.7 % |
| 538 | 12.6 | 2,699,307,834 | 2,723,249,176 | -0.9 % |
| 556 | 13.0 | 1,584,066,731 | 1,598,792,665 | -0.9 % |
| 580 | 13.5 | 1,186,349,518 | 1,197,249,566 | -0.9 % |
| 610 | 14.2 | 532,420,767 | 537,943,397 | -1.0 % |
| 640 | 14.9 | 229,602,329 | 232,188,422 | -1.1 % |
| 670 | 15.6 | 107,448,341 | 108,824,613 | -1.3 % |
| 700 | 16.3 | 55,549,826 | 56,294,110 | -1.3 % |

The pre-registered prediction (an error growing to 20–30 % near g/ln x = 16) failed: at 4·10¹⁸ the law is too high by only 0.6 % to 1.3 %, growing slowly with g/ln x. So the 27 % of rounds 24–25 is not a property of large g/ln x as such; it belongs to the sizes where the first occurrences sit (ln x from 16 to 27), where the expansion's parameters ρ/λ are larger. The exact sums of round 28 measure the expansion's bias at ln x = 17.8 only up to gap 90 (below 2 %), so whether the expansion or the primes are responsible at gaps of 180 to 454 near 10⁷–10¹¹ is not settled.

**Round 30: large gaps counted at 10⁹** ([r30.py](writeup/code/study/r30.py) → [r30.json](writeup/data/study/r30.json), [round30.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round30.txt)). In [10⁹, 2·10⁹) (47,374,752 gaps, ln x ≈ 21), the same law against the counts by band:

| band of g | g/ln x | gaps counted | law | ratio | z |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 120–178 | 5.7–8.4 | 72,370 | 73949.8 | 0.979 | −5.8 |
| 180–238 | 8.5–11.3 | 2,234 | 2287.8 | 0.976 | −1.1 |
| 240–298 | 11.4–14.1 | 58 | 66.6 | 0.871 | −1.1 |
| 300–358 | 14.2–16.9 | 0 | 2.0 | 0 | −1.4 |
| 360–454 | 17.0–21.5 | 0 | 0.1 | 0 | −0.2 |

The data show an error larger at this size than at 4·10¹⁸: about 2 % at 6 to 8 times ln x (0.979 ± 0.004, significant with 72,000 gaps), against about 1 % or less there. They do not show growth with g/ln x (0.976 ± 0.021 at 8–11 ln x, 0.87 ± 0.12 at 11–14 ln x), so the pre-registered test (ratios near 0.8 from gap 240 up) is inconclusive. Part of the 2 % may be the law's own truncation: the law here uses order 3 beyond span 120, and the order-4 terms that lowered the train tails by 1–6 % (round 28) exist only on the train grid, so they could not be applied at ln x ≈ 21. Whether the expansion or the primes carry the remaining error at these sizes is still open: the exact sums reach only gap 90.

**Round 32: order 4 for the first occurrences** ([r32.py](writeup/code/study/r32.py) → [r32.json](writeup/data/study/r32.json), [r32b.py](writeup/code/study/r32b.py) → [r32b.json](writeup/data/study/r32b.json), [round32.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round32.txt)). The order-4 term is not small for long gaps at small λ: in ln P it is -0.18, -0.11 and -0.05 for g = 240 and -0.28, -0.18 and -0.08 for g = 300 at λ = 18, 20 and 24 (about a third of that for g = 242 and 302, whose interiors have fewer live points). The excess of rounds 24–25 sits mostly in the gaps 180–300 (61 gaps: mean U 1.43 ± 0.21 with the order-3 law and the overlap correction, KS p = 0.020). Adding the order-4 term for all 61 gaps moves the mean to 1.32 ± 0.19, the median from 1.03 to 0.90 and the KS p to 0.040: order 4 removes about a quarter of the excess. (Both pre-registered tests pass, but they passed with order 3 on these 61 gaps too, a subset chosen after rounds 24–25; only the shifts are informative.) Order 5 has the same sign for g = 180 (round 33) and for every gap computed in round 38, which brings the excess down to borderline (p ≈ 0.04); rounds 47–55 below account for it. **Round 33** ([r33.py](writeup/code/study/r33.py) → [r33.json](writeup/data/study/r33.json), no data) checks how fast the series falls for a long gap: for g = 180 the order-5 term is -0.056, -0.032 and -0.012 at λ = 18, 20 and 24, against -0.100, -0.063 and -0.028 for order 4 (ratios 0.56, 0.50, 0.42). If the series keeps falling at about that rate, the orders beyond 4 together add about as much again as order 4, which would take the mean of U for the gaps 180–300 to about 1.23 (1.19 to 1.26 for ratios from 0.42 to 0.64), 1.1 to 1.45 standard errors from 1. That is an extrapolation, not a computation; the ratio rises as λ falls (0.64 at λ = 16, sixth review, [review6/bahadur.py](writeup/code/study/review6/bahadur.py)), so for g ≈ 300 near λ = 16 the series may converge slowly or not at all.

**Round 38: order 5 for the long gaps** ([r38.py](writeup/code/study/r38.py) → [r38.json](writeup/data/study/r38.json), [r38_u.json](writeup/data/study/r38_u.json); a diagnostic, [round38.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round38.txt)) replaces that extrapolation by a computation. Order 5 sums the 52-partition cumulant over all 5-subsets of the live interior points; keeping the order-4 values in a combinatorially indexed vector instead of a dense n⁴ table makes it fit in memory for up to about 100 live points, which would cover every gap from 180 to 300 not divisible by 6 and the multiples of 6 up to 210; it was run for 24 of them (19 of the 40 gaps not divisible by 6, 5 of the 21 multiples of 6: 180–198 and 210). It reproduces r32.json (order 4) and r33.json (order 5 for g = 180) exactly, and the sixth review's independent code gives the same order-5 terms for g = 186 and 190 ([review6/bahadur.py](writeup/code/study/review6/bahadur.py) → [o5_186.out](writeup/data/study/review6/o5_186.out), [o5_190.out](writeup/data/study/review6/o5_190.out)). Computed for 24 of the 61 gaps, the ratio of order 5 to order 4 at λ = 16 depends on the residue class of g: 0.64–0.69 for the multiples of 6 (5 gaps, rising with g), 0.07–0.22 for the multiples of 10 not divisible by 6 (5), 0.25–0.39 for the others (14); at λ = 27 each is about 0.55–0.6 times that. Taking order 5 as computed where available and from a linear trend in g within the class elsewhere, the mean of U for the 61 gaps falls from 1.427 (order 3) and 1.324 (order 4) to **1.277**: 1.5 standard errors from 1 with the sample spread (± 0.179), 2.2 with the spread the null itself implies (U exponential: ± 1/√61 = 0.128; exact Gamma test p = 0.04 two-sided, against 0.003 at order 3), KS p = 0.069. For the 16 multiples of 6 above 210 the trend is extrapolated far beyond the computed range (ratio 0.76 at g = 250 and 0.85 at g = 300 for λ = 16); holding the ratio at the last computed value instead (g = 210, 0.69) gives 1.280. If the series continued geometrically with the order-5 ratio (a scenario, not computed, and for the large multiples of 6 mostly a product of the extrapolated ratio), the mean would be 1.221 (1.7 null standard errors). Split by class after seeing the ratios (post hoc): the gaps not divisible by 6 have mean U 1.17 ± 0.17 at order 3 and 1.12 ± 0.17 at order 5, consistent with the law at every order; the excess sits in the multiples of 6 (1.91 ± 0.49 at order 3, 1.57 ± 0.41 at order 5, 1.41 in the geometric scenario), the class where the series converges slowest (when 3 | g the two ends share their residue mod 3 and every even interior point stays live, about g/2 of them; otherwise the ends occupy two residues and the interior points in the third are excluded, leaving about g/3). So with order 5 the large-gap result is borderline rather than clear (mean test p ≈ 0.04, KS p = 0.07; at order 3 p ≈ 0.003; the 61 gaps are the subset of round 32, chosen after rounds 24–25; checked by the seventh review, [review7/r38chk.py](writeup/code/study/review7/r38chk.py) → [r38chk.out](writeup/data/study/review7/r38chk.out)). Its most plausible source is the truncation: every added order moved the mean towards 1, and the excess sits in the class where the series converges slowest. That stays a reading, since the orders beyond 5 were not computed. (See rounds 47–51 below.)

**Rounds 47–55 and 63: a conditioned expansion and the large gaps** (the Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan conditioning, used as the expansion point for long gaps; [r47.py](writeup/code/study/r47.py), [r47_check3.py](writeup/code/study/r47_check3.py), [r48.py](writeup/code/study/r48.py) → [r47_check_y13.json](writeup/data/study/r47_check_y13.json), [r47_check3_y13.json](writeup/data/study/r47_check3_y13.json), [r47_u_y13.json](writeup/data/study/r47_u_y13.json), [r47_u_y17.json](writeup/data/study/r47_u_y17.json), [r47_u3_y13.json](writeup/data/study/r47_u3_y13.json), [r48.json](writeup/data/study/r48.json); [round47.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round47.txt), [round48.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round48.txt)). The Bahadur series of section 3 expands around independent interior points, but the correlations of the small primes are strong, and that is why it converges slowly for long gaps, worst for multiples of 6. The singular series factorises exactly, S(B) = S_{≤y}(B)·S_{>y}(B), and by the Chinese remainder theorem the small-prime part is exactly an average over the classes a of p mod ∏_{q≤y} q: in each class an interior point is either impossible or "rough", and a rough point is prime with chance ρ₀ = 1/(λ ∏_{q≤y}(1 − 1/q)) times a weak factor. So

  P(gap = g | p prime) = E_a[ 1(a + g rough) · ρ₀ S_{>y}({0, g}) · Π_a ],

with Π_a the chance that none of the rough interior points of class a is prime, a measure whose only correlations come from the primes q > y (at most about 1/(q − 2) ≤ 1/15 for y = 13). The classes are averaged exactly (y = 13: 5,760 classes) and only Π_a is expanded, in Bahadur form with the overlap (polymer) correction. Against the exact sums for g = 74–90 (r28.json) the error is at most 0.27 % at second order and 0.04 % at third order with the overlap correction (λ = 17.8; 0.011 % at λ = 23), against 0.7–1.7 % for the unconditioned order 4 (0.3–0.9 % with its overlap correction). Order 3 alone is worse than order 2; the agreement comes from order 3 and the overlap term together, which partly cancel (+0.66 % and −1.26 % at g = 150), and it is validated only for g = 74–90; for g = 240–300 at λ = 16 the step from order 2 is 11–19 % in the share, so the method is least certain there, and the next Mayer term was not computed. The construction is essentially that of Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan ([arXiv:1603.03720](https://arxiv.org/abs/1603.03720)), who condition on n mod q with ρ₀ = q/(φ(q) log n) and expand the avoidance of the interior points in the connected singular series of Montgomery and Soundararajan, for biases between consecutive residues; the averaging over residue classes is also in Granville's model and in Banks, Ford and Tao ([arXiv:1908.08613](https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.08613)). What is ours is its use as the expansion point for the whole law of long gaps and the tests below.

*Round 47* (pre-registered): the first occurrences of the 61 gaps 180–300. Second order gives mean U = 1.315 (y = 13) and 1.301 (y = 17, converged to 0.014), third order with the overlap correction 1.274: 2.1 null standard errors above 1 (KS p = 0.064; multiples of 6 1.61, the others 1.10; on the fine λ grid of round 53 about 1.286, 2.2σ). Round 38's order 5 (1.277) had been close; only its geometric scenario (1.221) over-corrected. By round 47's pre-registered reading at its registered order 2 (z = 2.46) this reads as a deviation, but the 61 gaps were chosen after rounds 24–25 had shown the excess.

*Round 48* (pre-registered) tests the method with counts instead of single events: every gap of 120–298 between consecutive primes in [10⁹, 2·10⁹) against the conditioned law (third order with overlap correction):

| band | g/ln x | counted | old law (round 30) | conditioned law | ratio | z |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 120–178 | 5.7–8.4 | 72,370 | 73,950 (z −5.8) | 72,432 | 0.9991 | -0.2 |
| 180–238 | 8.5–11.3 | 2,234 | 2,288 | 2,167 | 1.031 | +1.4 |
| 240–298 | 11.4–14.1 | 58 | 66.6 | 60.5 | 0.96 | -0.3 |

The multiples of 6 fit as well (40,547 against 40,560 in the first band, 1,231 against 1,184 in the second). By the pre-registered reading the old 2 % shortfall at z = −5.8 (gaps of 6–8 ln x) was the truncated expansion: that part is **closed** (ratio 0.999 ± 0.004; the counts are round 30's, known before the method was built, only the law values are new). For larger g/ln x the counts at 10⁹ are few and cannot exclude a deficit of the size of the first-occurrence excess (240–298: 58 against 60.5 ± 7.8).

*Round 49* ([r49.py](writeup/code/study/r49.py) → [r49.json](writeup/data/study/r49.json), [round49.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round49.txt), pre-registered) stresses the method where it is hardest, at small x: the gaps of 92–220 in [10⁷, 10⁸) (λ = 16–18):

| band | g/ln x | counted | order 2 | order 3 + overlap | ratio | z |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 92–118 | 5.0–7.3 | 9,392 | 9369.0 | 9323.5 | 1.007 | +0.7 |
| 120–148 | 6.5–9.2 | 1,533 | 1495.2 | 1479.7 | 1.036 | +1.4 |
| 150–178 | 8.1–11.0 | 153 | 170.9 | 167.5 | 0.913 | −1.1 |
| 180–220 | 9.8–13.6 | 11 | 20.6 | 20.0 | 0.551 | −2.0 |

By the pre-registered reading the method holds at small x: every band within 3σ, and the step from order 2 to order 3 smaller than each band's noise. (Three λ points over the window bias the law low by up to 1.7 %; with direct quadrature, [r49g.py](writeup/code/study/r49g.py) → [r49g.json](writeup/data/study/r49g.json), the ratios are 1.000, 1.026, 0.90 and 0.54, z = 0.0, +1.0, −1.3 and −2.1: the verdict is unchanged.) The largest band is 2σ low (11 against 20, Poisson p ≈ 0.02), in the same direction as the first-occurrence excess of round 47, so a small shortfall of the very largest gaps at small x (g/ln x above about 10) remains possible; at 10⁹ the corresponding band (240–298) fits.

*Round 51* ([r51.py](writeup/code/study/r51.py) → [r51.json](writeup/data/study/r51.json), [round51.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round51.txt), pre-registered after the eleventh review pointed out that rounds 47–48 had not re-run the registered test) re-runs rounds 24–25 on all 138 gaps 180–454 with the conditioned law. Mean U is **1.160** with order 3 and the overlap correction (1.9 null standard errors of 0.085; KS p = 0.031, now passing round 24's rule p > 0.01), 1.201 at order 2 (2.4σ, KS p = 0.011); the gaps 302–454 fit (1.06), the gaps 180–300 do not (1.29). (These use the fine λ grid of round 53, [r51f.py](writeup/code/study/r51f.py) → [r51f.json](writeup/data/study/r51f.json); the first run on steps of 1 gave 1.151.) The original law gave 1.27. By the pre-registered reading the large-gap finding stays **open**: the conditioned law explains about 40 % of it, and an excess of about 16 % at 1.9σ remains, together with round 49's 2σ shortfall of the largest gaps at small x (not independent: they share 7 of 11 gaps).

*Round 53* ([r53.py](writeup/code/study/r53.py), [r53g.py](writeup/code/study/r53g.py) → [r53.json](writeup/data/study/r53.json), [r53g.json](writeup/data/study/r53g.json), [round53.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round53.txt), pre-registered) is a precision test: the counts N(g) of gaps below 4·10¹⁸ (Oliveira e Silva, counts over [2, 4·10¹⁸]; statistical noise 10⁻⁸–10⁻⁴), where the old law was 0.55–1.34 % too high for the gaps 460–700 (round 29), integrated as in round 29 (from 10⁷, plus the actual counts below 10⁷). The registered run (r53.py) passed 18 of the 20 gaps and left a residual growing linearly with g (0.03 % at g = 72, 0.41 % at 700), the same with y = 17 ([r53b.py](writeup/code/study/r53b.py) → [r53b_y17.json](writeup/data/study/r53b_y17.json)) and present at the controls g = 40–70, where the exact law fits the counts to about 10⁻⁸ ([r53c.py](writeup/code/study/r53c.py) → [r53c_y13.json](writeup/data/study/r53c_y13.json)). It was numerical: ln share contains −g/λ, and its linear interpolation on steps of 2 in λ lies below the true curve by up to (step²/8)·2g/λ³ (about (step²/6)·g/λ³ on average over a step); a grid of 0.25 near the top moved the result by −0.028 %, −0.159 % and −0.334 % at g = 70, 300, 700 ([r53e.py](writeup/code/study/r53e.py) → [r53e.json](writeup/data/study/r53e.json), [r53f.py](writeup/code/study/r53f.py) → [r53f.json](writeup/data/study/r53f.json)), and the twelfth review showed that even that grid and the linear interpolation of the order-3 term left a bias of up to 0.035 % of the same sign. The final numbers (r53g.py) evaluate order 2 directly at 160 Gauss–Legendre nodes and interpolate the order-3 + overlap correction cubically from five values of λ; they agree with the twelfth review's own implementation:

| g | count below 4·10¹⁸ | old law (r29) | order 2 | order 3 + overlap | step | remainder / step |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 40 | 1,735,952,776,259,227 | – | +0.0025 % | +0.0000 % | +0.0025 % | 0.01 |
| 70 | 962,217,154,127,146 | – | +0.0113 % | +0.0002 % | +0.0111 % | 0.02 |
| 100 | 370,746,210,140,783 | – | +0.0188 % | +0.0008 % | +0.0179 % | 0.05 |
| 150 | 199,709,892,313,088 | – | +0.0127 % | +0.0031 % | +0.0096 % | 0.32 |
| 200 | 26,410,124,134,031 | – | +0.0003 % | +0.0060 % | −0.0057 % | 1.06 |
| 300 | 3,826,083,078,992 | – | −0.0955 % | +0.0154 % | −0.1109 % | 0.14 |
| 400 | 139,516,829,742 | – | −0.2516 % | +0.0256 % | −0.2772 % | 0.09 |
| 454 | 24,846,683,496 | – | −0.3797 % | +0.0327 % | −0.4124 % | 0.08 |
| 460 | 29,560,946,024 | −0.55 % | −0.3925 % | +0.0332 % | −0.4257 % | 0.08 |
| 556 | 1,584,066,731 | −0.93 % | −0.6543 % | +0.0541 % | −0.7084 % | 0.08 |
| 610 | 532,420,767 | −1.04 % | −0.8279 % | +0.0528 % | −0.8807 % | 0.06 |
| 640 | 229,602,329 | −1.13 % | −0.9216 % | +0.0627 % | −0.9843 % | 0.06 |
| 700 | 55,549,826 | −1.34 % | −1.1623 % | +0.0499 % | −1.2122 % | 0.04 |

The conditioned law (order 3 with the overlap correction) is within 0.001 % of the counts for g ≤ 100, 0.016 % up to g = 300 and 0.065 % up to g = 700 (largest 0.063 % at g = 640) (the old law: 0.55–1.34 %, itself about 0.05–0.07 % too low from its own 40-point grid). All residuals are positive and far beyond the counting noise (about 4σ at g = 700, hundreds of σ at g = 300), so they are not noise; at large g (g ≥ 454) they are 4–8 % of the step from order 2 to order 3, which is the size one expects from the next order, but no higher order was computed and orders 3 and the overlap term partly cancel, so that remains a reading. All 20 gaps pass the pre-registered rule with the corrected numerics, but at large g the rule's threshold (twice the step, up to 2.4 %) is loose. So the Hardy–Littlewood law, summed this way, reproduces the counts of every tested gap up to 700 below 4·10¹⁸ to 0.065 %. (A bias fitted to g = 40–70 and extrapolated, [r53d.json](writeup/data/study/r53d.json), had mimicked the numerical error before its cause was found.)

*Round 54* ([r54.py](writeup/code/study/r54.py) → [r54.json](writeup/data/study/r54.json), [round54.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round54.txt), pre-registered) asks whether the remaining first-occurrence excess is a property of the rate: every gap of 230–420 (about 10–18 ln x) between consecutive primes in the new window [10¹⁰, 2·10¹⁰) (427,154,204 gaps, π(2·10¹⁰) − π(10¹⁰) − 1 exactly), against the conditioned law:

| band | g/ln x | counted | conditioned law | ratio | z | z against 0.84 × law |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 230–278 | 9.7–12.1 | 4,095 | 4,203 | 0.974 | −1.7 | +9.5 |
| 280–328 | 11.8–14.2 | 320 | 318 | 1.005 | +0.1 | +3.2 |
| 330–420 | 13.9–18.2 | 31 | 25 | 1.225 | +1.1 | +2.1 |
| **pooled** | 9.7–18.2 | 4,446 | 4,547 | 0.978 | −1.5 | +10.1 |

The pooled ratio is 0.978 ± 0.015 (−1.5σ), and a rate 16 % below the law is excluded at 10σ. By the pre-registered reading the first-occurrence excess is not a property of the rate at these sizes. With the conditioned law, round 51 also passes round 24's own registered rules (mean of U within 3 standard errors: 1.9; KS p = 0.031 > 0.01). So about 40 % of the large-gap finding of rounds 24–25 was the truncated law (shown by the counts, round 48); the remaining 16 % (1.9σ) is not in the rate (round 54; even at 12–18 ln x alone, 351 counted against 343.7 expected, 0.84 × law excluded at 3.7σ; the multiples of 6 at 0.968 ± 0.022) and passes the registered rules: **explained**, with a 1.9σ remainder in the first occurrences, which by themselves cannot tell the old law from the new (1.16 is 1.3σ from the old 1.27). Round 49's shortfall of the very largest gaps at 10⁷–10⁸ did not replicate (round 55 below).

*Round 55* ([r55.py](writeup/code/study/r55.py) → [r55.json](writeup/data/study/r55.json), [round55.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round55.txt), pre-registered) replicates round 49's small-x shortfall with more data: the gaps of g/ln x ≥ 10 in [10⁸, 10⁹) (45,086,078 gaps):

| band | g/ln x | counted | conditioned law | ratio | z |

| 208–238 | 10.0–12.9 | 168 | 157.9 | 1.06 | +0.8 |
| 240–268 | 11.6–14.5 | 25 | 20.8 | 1.20 | +0.9 |
| 270–300 | 13.0–16.3 | 2 | 3.2 | 0.62 | −0.7 |
| **pooled** | 10.0–16.3 | 195 | 182.0 | 1.07 | +1.0 |

The pooled ratio is 1.07 (+1.0σ), and round 49's ratio of 0.54 is excluded at 9.8σ: by the pre-registered reading, round 49's shortfall was chance.

*Round 63* ([r63.py](writeup/code/study/r63.py) → [r63.json](writeup/data/study/r63.json), [round63.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round63.txt), pre-registered) replicates round 54 out of sample at a new scale: every gap of 276–500 (about 10–18 ln x) in [10¹², 10¹² + 10¹⁰) (361,840,207 gaps; apart from its first 5·10⁷ numbers, window B, never counted at these sizes):

| 276–330 | 10.0–11.9 | 3,994 | 3,931 | 1.016 | +1.0 |
| 332–390 | 12.0–14.1 | 322 | 338 | 0.953 | −0.9 |
| 392–500 | 14.2–18.1 | 25 | 27 | 0.923 | −0.4 |

The pooled ratio is 1.010 (+0.7σ) and a rate 16 % below the law is excluded at 12σ: by the pre-registered reading the conditioned law holds for large gaps at 10¹² as well.
