round 19, jumping champions within 2 %: passes), round 39's reading fails at train (section 12), and rounds 59, 64, 67 and 71 (twin gaps) pass their χ² readings, the first two missing the tail at z = 3.7 and 3.1 (section 7); a miss is |t₉| > 3 for block tests, |z| > 3 for χ² tests and means, and p < 0.01 for the Kolmogorov–Smirnov tests of rounds 24–25; the chance of a miss per test is about 0.015, 0.0027 and 0.01; the tests are not all independent: round 22 re-tests round 3's gap shares on the same primes, and nested tails overlap):

| round | what | tests | misses | expected by chance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2-3 | gap law, classes, variance, information, tails, CPAP-4 (4 windows; registered test) | 96 | 4 | 1.4 |
| 4 | consecutive twin primes | 68 | 0 | 1.0 |
| 5 | consecutive n with n² + 1 prime | 45 | 3 | 0.7 |
| 6 | second differences | 20 | 0 | 0.3 |
| 9 | variance and means along quadratics (fresh data) | 4 | 0 | 0.0 |
| 10 | third cumulant of interval counts | 8 | 0 | 0.1 |
| 10, 34 | variance in long intervals (3 windows) | 9 | 1 | 0.1 |
| 11 | Gaussian primes, nearest neighbours (4 boxes) | 36 | 0 | 0.5 |
| 13 | residue chain mod 30 and 210, cell by cell (χ² too low counts as a miss) | 8 | 1 | 0.0 |
| 14 | consecutive Sophie Germain primes | 56 | 1 | 0.8 |
| 15 | joint law of three gaps, 729 cells (4 windows) | 4 | 0 | 0.0 |
| 16 | Eisenstein primes, nearest neighbours (4 boxes) | 36 | 1 | 0.5 |
| 17 | the law at 10¹⁸ | 18 | 1 | 0.3 |
| 18 | consecutive n with n² + n + 41 prime (37 of 45 registered tests; 8 with expected count below 5 excluded after the run, 3 of which miss formally) | 37 | 0 | 0.6 |
| 20 | count distribution in short intervals (H = 30, 60; cells with P ≥ 10⁻³) | 56 | 17 | 0.8 |
| 21 | the same with the exact Hardy–Littlewood sums (H = 30 and 60) | 55 | 1 | 0.8 |
| 22 | exact gap law: gaps 2–70 and the tail ≥ 72 in four windows (and ≥ 54 at train) | 145 | 2 | 2.2 |
| 24 | first occurrences of the gaps 180–454 (mean of U, KS test against Exp(1)) | 2 | 1 | 0.0 |
| 25 | the same with the polymer overlap correction | 2 | 1 | 0.0 |
| 31 | variance of twin counts in intervals (H = 10³ to 10⁷; the invalid sliding H = 10⁷ estimate left out) | 9 | 0 | 0.1 |
| 32 | first occurrences of the gaps 180–300 with the order-4 term (mean of U, KS; order 3 passed these too, so they do not separate the orders) | 2 | 0 | 0.0 |
| | **all** | **716** | **34** | **10.5** |

34 misses in 716 tests against 10.5 expected (the rows are rounded; round 35, a check of the explicit formula, is not counted). Read as a ledger, the 56 cells of round 20 are replaced by round 21 (the exact sums for the same question): 17 misses in 660 tests against 9.7 expected. Four of these have a found cause: three are the information estimators before their bias was measured from the law (round 3), one is the gap tail at train, which the exact law of round 22 removes. Two are one finding counted twice, the large-gap first occurrences of rounds 24–25 (same data; counted once, the ledger is 16 misses in 658 tests), explained as well (counted with the 5 found causes, since the registered rules now pass): with the conditioned expansion the registered test passes its own rules (round 51: mean U 1.16, a 1.9σ remainder; KS p = 0.03), about 40 % of the excess was the truncation, and the counts of the same g/ln x at 10¹⁰ and 10⁸–10⁹ match the law (rounds 54–55 and, out of sample at 10¹², 63; a 16 % rate deficit excluded at 10σ and 12σ, and at 3.7σ even at 12–18 ln x at 10¹⁰). One more is an open point, the low χ² of the residue tables (round 13), half explained at train by the Hardy–Littlewood rigidity derived in round 39 (predicted 0.85 against 0.69 measured, against 1 for independent draws) and within the noise at 10¹⁰ and 10¹⁵ (at 10¹² the table shows no deficit). The variance miss at H = 10⁴ (round 10) did not replicate in a third window (round 34, and neither did the sign pattern for shorter H, round 37) and is counted with chance. The other 10 against about 9.6 expected are at chance level: the law is right to about 0.1 % where the counts are in the millions and to a few per cent in the smallest samples; with the exact sums (gaps up to 70) it matches the counts below 4·10¹⁸ to their own scatter (round 23, which like rounds 26–28 has no pass rule and is not in the table). (Before the second review the count was 15 of 405, with 4 misses in the two-dimensional boxes; 3 of those came from counting every point twice in boxes symmetric about a mirror line, see section 11.)


- **Numerical, statistical, algebraic, dynamic, information-theoretic**: five views of one object, the law of runs of consecutive primes. Its only input is the singular series; its only scale is ln x. The textbook versions miss because they truncate it: the Poisson factor e^(−h/ln x) ignores that the numbers between two primes are correlated with them (round 1: up to 30 %), exponentiating the Ursell functions of distinct points mistreats X² = X (step 3), and the Montgomery–Soundararajan asymptotics ignore the grid (H = 50).
- **Geometric**: Ulam's diagonals are the same law for polynomials: the means are Bateman–Horn and the scatter is set by the Bateman–Horn constants of polynomial pairs. In the plane, the Gaussian primes follow the same expansion with the singular series of ℤ[i] (section 11).
- **Computational**: the pre-registered windows and the block test. Windows from 10⁷ to 10¹⁸ (short windows at 10¹⁰, 10¹², 10¹⁵ and 10¹⁸, and the long windows [10⁷, 10⁸), [10⁸, 10⁹), [10⁹, 2·10⁹), [10⁹, 10¹⁰), [10¹⁰, 2·10¹⁰), [10¹¹, +10¹⁰), [10¹², +10¹⁰) and [10¹³, +10¹⁰)), several opened only after the law was frozen.
- **Open**: the tail of the twin-gap law beyond 1,200 at 10⁹ and 10¹¹ (z = 3.7 and 3.1; attributed to the order-2 truncation, measured at 10⁹, not yet removed); a remainder of the rigidity of gap-event counts per residue class (rounds 39–41); the evenness of twin counts per class at the coarse moduli (rounds 42–44).
- **What is not new** (for what is, see *What is new here* at the top): the Hardy–Littlewood and Bateman–Horn conjectures, Gallagher's Poisson law, the Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan bias and the Montgomery–Soundararajan variance are literature; that a parameter-free Hardy–Littlewood count fits gap counts has been used before (Brent 1974 for small gaps; Odlyzko, Rubinstein and Wolf 1999 for jumping champions). Hardy–Littlewood inclusion–exclusion for differences of consecutive primes, with lower-order terms, has been studied (for example by Goldston and Ledoan, and by Funkhouser, Goldston and Ledoan for large gaps); those papers were not read for this study. What this study adds, as far as the files in this archive show, is the Bahadur form and its accuracy (0.1–0.3 % for gaps up to 30 at four scales, which is the size of its own bias at train), the grid term in the variance, the variance law along quadratics, and the same expansion for twin primes, for n² + 1 and for Gaussian primes; the literature for these was not searched beyond this archive.

## 15. How predictable is the next prime? (rounds 45, 46, 50, 52, 56–58, 61, 62, 66, 68–70, 72–86)

**The question.** Knowing p, can one predict the next gap? Guessing the commonest gap per class is right 13 %, 17 % and 20 % of the time if p mod 6, 30 or 210 is known (near 10⁹). The gap is fixed once p mod q is known for every prime q ≤ √p, since that decides which of p + 2, p + 4, … are prime. So the real question is how the predictability grows with y, the largest prime whose residue is known.

**The law (own construction from classical parts: Buchstab's density of rough numbers, Granville's model, the Hardy–Littlewood pair factor; a literature search found the parts but not this predictor).** With p mod q known for all q ≤ y, the best guess is the first y-rough number after p (no prime factor ≤ y); it is right exactly when that number is prime. So hit(y) is the share of primes among the y-rough numbers that follow a prime:

  **hit(y) = ⟨S_{>y}({0, h₁})⟩ / ( ln p · ∏_{q≤y} (1 − 1/q) · e^γ · ω(u) ),  u = ln p / ln y,**

where ω is Buchstab's function (it counts rough numbers correctly up to √p, where Mertens' product would give e^γ/2 = 0.89 instead of 1), and ⟨S_{>y}({0, h₁})⟩ is the Hardy–Littlewood pair factor of the primes above y, averaged over the first rough distance h₁ (it carries the fact that p itself is prime: for y = 2 the guess is p + 2, right with chance S({0, 2})/ln p = 1.32/ln p, not 2/ln p). (This is a heuristic of Granville's type: Maier's theorem shows that prime counts in short intervals follow the local density of rough numbers rather than the Cramér density, which is the Buchstab factor's role here; that the first rough number after a prime is prime with the average chance times the pair factor is assumed, and the data at 10⁹–10¹⁵ agree with it at this precision.) Simply: **hit ≈ 1/(u·ω(u))**, a function of u alone; to guess right with probability ρ one needs the residues mod all primes up to about p^(ρ·e^(−γ)) ≈ p^(0.56ρ) (for ρ not close to 1), so 50 % needs y ≈ p^0.28.

**Round 45** ([r45.py](writeup/code/study/r45.py) → [r45.json](writeup/data/study/r45.json), [r45b.py](writeup/code/study/r45b.py) → [r45b.json](writeup/data/study/r45b.json), [round45.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round45.txt)). With M(p) the largest smallest-prime-factor among the composites in the gap, hit(y) = P(M(p) ≤ y), measured over every prime of [10⁹, 10⁹ + 5·10⁷) and [10¹², 10¹² + 10⁷) on a grid of 44 values of y up to √x. The pre-registered law had the Buchstab factor but not the pair factor; it misses at y = 2, 3, 5 (10⁹) and y = 2, 3 (10¹²) and holds everywhere else (to the tolerance 0.01 plus 3 binomial sd). With the pair factor, derived after the misses, every one of the 88 points agrees to within 0.0009 (binomial sd about 0.0003–0.0004 near 10⁹ and 0.0004–0.0008 near 10¹²). Near 10⁹:

| y | u | measured hit | pre-registered law | with the pair factor | Mertens only |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 29.93 | 0.0636 | 0.0964 | 0.0636 | 0.0964 |
| 3 | 18.89 | 0.1272 | 0.1446 | 0.1273 | 0.1446 |
| 7 | 10.66 | 0.2033 | 0.2109 | 0.2036 | 0.2109 |
| 97 | 4.54 | 0.3995 | 0.4006 | 0.3998 | 0.4006 |
| 293 | 3.65 | 0.4945 | 0.4950 | 0.4947 | 0.4945 |
| 1,777 | 2.77 | 0.6381 | 0.6381 | 0.6381 | 0.6446 |
| 10,607 | 2.24 | 0.8249 | 0.8248 | 0.8248 | 0.7966 |
| 20,731 | 2.09 | 0.9229 | 0.9231 | 0.9231 | 0.8536 |
| 32,401 | 2.00 | 1.0000 | 1.0000 | 1.0000 | 0.8919 |

An independent walk over 200,000 consecutive primes near 10¹² (gmpy2, gcd with primorials, no sieve; [r45_check.py](writeup/code/study/r45_check.py) → [r45_check.out](writeup/data/study/r45_check.out)) gives 0.0477, 0.1526 and 0.2992 at y = 2, 7, 97, against 0.0478, 0.1531, 0.3002.

**Round 46: how many bits remain** ([r46.py](writeup/code/study/r46.py) → [r46.json](writeup/data/study/r46.json), [round46.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round46.txt); own derivation, pre-registered). Given the residues mod q ≤ y, the positions of the rough numbers after p are fixed and only K, the index of the next prime among them, is unknown. If each is prime with chance ρ = hit(y) independently, K is geometric and the remaining uncertainty is **h₂(ρ)/ρ bits** (h₂ the binary entropy): 4.7 bits measured when only p mod 2 is known (near 10⁹), 0 at y = √p. Measured H(K) (plug-in, Miller–Madow; pooled over the classes it is an upper bound on the conditional entropy E_a H(K | a), larger by 0.012 bits at y = 13 near 10⁹) agrees to about 0.003 bits from y ≈ 100 up to √x in both windows (for example 2.020 against 2.021 bits at y = 293 near 10⁹, 0.424 against 0.424 at y = 20,731), and the ratio P(K = 2)/P(K = 1) equals 1 − ρ to about 0.003. By the pre-registered rule (0.05 bits at every point with u ≥ 2.5) it misses at y = 2, 3, 5, 7 near 10⁹ and y = 2, 3, 5, 7 near 10¹² (8 of 69 points), always on the low side: with only the smallest primes known, successive rough numbers are correlated (the Hardy–Littlewood structure the geometric law leaves out) and the gap is more predictable than independence says (4.66 against 5.37 bits at y = 2 near 10⁹).

**Round 50: out of sample at 10¹⁵** ([r50.py](writeup/code/study/r50.py) → [r50.json](writeup/data/study/r50.json), [round50.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round50.txt), pre-registered with the corrected formula fixed in advance). In the fresh window [10¹⁵, 10¹⁵ + 10⁷) (289,393 primes) all 44 points of the corrected law agree to within 0.0016 (pass rule 0.002 + 3 binomial sd; for example y = 2: 0.0379 against 0.0382; y = 12,569: 0.4875 against 0.4876; y = √x: 1 against 1), with a slight systematic offset of about −0.001 for y below 5,000; the information law agrees to within 0.0034 bits at every y ≥ 100. Both pass.


**Round 52: the entropy of the next gap** ([r52.py](writeup/code/study/r52.py) → [r52.json](writeup/data/study/r52.json), [round52.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round52.txt); own derivation, pre-registered). To leading order the Hardy–Littlewood gap law is P(g) = (S(0, g)/λ) e^(−g/λ) for even g, whose entropy is ln(eλ) − ½·mean_{g even}[S ln S]. Since S(0, g) = 2C₂ ∏_{p | g, p > 2} (p − 1)/(p − 2) is multiplicative and the mean of S·1(p | g) over even g is 2/(p − 1), the mean is a closed sum:

  **H(next gap) = log₂(e·ln p) − K/ln 2 bits,  K = ln(2C₂) + Σ_{p>2} ln((p − 1)/(p − 2))/(p − 1) = 0.7627474**

(brute force over the even g up to 4·10⁶: 0.762742). For gaps that were only even and otherwise random K would be ln 2, so the singular series takes away (K − ln 2)/ln 2 = 0.1004 bits, almost exactly a tenth of a bit. (A quick literature search found entropy studies of prime gaps, for example [arXiv:1606.08293](https://arxiv.org/abs/1606.08293), and a conditional lower bound of order log log X, [arXiv:2605.12504](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12504), but no closed formula of this kind.) Measured over every gap of five windows (plug-in with the Miller–Madow correction):

| window | gaps | mean λ | measured H (bits) | log₂(eλ) − K/ln 2 | difference | λ × difference | discrete leading-order law |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [10⁷, 10⁸) | 5,096,875 | 17.7 | 4.4169 | 4.4842 | −0.0673 | −1.19 | 4.5950 |
| [10⁹, 2·10⁹) | 47,374,752 | 21.1 | 4.6891 | 4.7420 | −0.0529 | −1.12 | 4.8416 |
| A (10¹⁰) | 2,171,133 | 23.0 | 4.8211 | 4.8676 | −0.0465 | −1.07 | 4.9622 |
| B (10¹²) | 1,808,832 | 27.6 | 5.0952 | 5.1305 | −0.0353 | −0.97 | 5.2152 |
| C (10¹⁵) | 578,836 | 34.5 | 5.4251 | 5.4524 | −0.0274 | −0.95 | 5.5261 |

The difference shrinks steadily and is below 0.05 bits at 10¹⁵: by the pre-registered reading, applied to the formula as written (the analytic asymptote at the window's mean λ; widening to the window changes it by under 0.001 bits), it is the right asymptote. (r52.py's `diff` field compares with the discrete leading-order law instead, −0.101 bits at C, which would fail the 0.05 rule; the reading here uses the formula.) The data pin K only to about ±0.04 bits: fitting the five differences as a + b/λ gives an intercept of +0.016 bits, with a + b ln λ/λ +0.043 bits, against the 0.100-bit effect of the singular series. λ times the difference stays near −1 (−1.2 to −0.9), so empirically H ≈ log₂(eλ) − K/ln 2 − c/λ with c ≈ 1 bit; that next term is read off five points, not derived. The discrete leading-order law at finite λ (last column) lies about 0.1 bits higher than the asymptote, because averages of the singular series over short ranges converge slowly (the −½ ln H of section 4), and that offset partly cancels against the next-order terms of the law.

**Round 57** (theory, no data; [r57.py](writeup/code/study/r57.py) → [r57.json](writeup/data/study/r57.json)) checks the asymptote where data cannot go: the entropy of the full Hardy–Littlewood gap law (conditioned expansion, order 2) at fixed λ. At the data's λ it reproduces the measured entropies (4.429 at λ = 17.8, about 4.418 at the window's prime-weighted mean λ = 17.66, and 4.819, 5.424, against 4.417, 4.821, 5.425 bits), and beyond them it keeps closing in on log₂(eλ) − K/ln 2:

| λ | x | entropy of the full law (bits) | asymptote | difference | λ × difference |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 17.8 | 5·10⁷ | 4.4291 | 4.4961 | −0.0670 | −1.19 |
| 23.0 | 10¹⁰ | 4.8188 | 4.8658 | −0.0470 | −1.08 |
| 34.5 | 10¹⁵ | 5.4245 | 5.4508 | −0.0264 | −0.91 |
| 50.0 | 5·10²¹ | 5.9712 | 5.9861 | −0.0149 | −0.75 |
| 70.0 | 2.5·10³⁰ | 6.4631 | 6.4716 | −0.0084 | −0.59 |
| 100.0 | 2.7·10⁴³ | 6.9820 | 6.9861 | −0.0041 | −0.41 |

|λ × difference| shrinks roughly linearly in ln λ, so the law crosses the asymptote near λ ≈ 270 and then approaches it like ln λ/λ (consistent with round 52's discrete law lying about 0.1 bits above the asymptote); a fit with a free constant puts the law's limit within about 0.002 bits of K ([review14/r57fit.py](writeup/code/study/review14/r57fit.py) → [r57fit.out](writeup/data/study/review14/r57fit.out)). This uses the order-2 conditioned law, whose own truncation could matter at the 10⁻³-bit level. So K is the law's own limit to that precision, not only an extrapolation of the data.

**Round 66: the gap entropy at 10¹⁸** ([r66.py](writeup/code/study/r66.py) → [r66.json](writeup/data/study/r66.json), [round66.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round66.txt), pre-registered; out of sample). The 48,427 gaps of round 17's window [10¹⁸, 10¹⁸ + 2·10⁶) (its 48,428 primes, listed by gmpy2.next_prime, are in [r17_primes.txt](writeup/data/study/r17_primes.txt); the last prime lies just beyond the window, so the last gap straddles its end) have entropy 5.6914 ± 0.0066 bits; the full law at the window's λ = 41.4465 (round 57's computation) gives 5.6954 (data −0.0040, z = −0.6; the registered run took the law at λ = 41.4, 5.6938, z = −0.4), and the asymptote log₂(eλ) − K/ln 2 lies 0.024 bits above the data, as round 57's trend predicts (−0.02 at this λ). By the pre-registered reading (within 2 standard errors of the full law) the entropy law holds at 10¹⁸.

**Round 56: the information one gap carries about the next** ([r56.py](writeup/code/study/r56.py) → [r56.json](writeup/data/study/r56.json), [round56.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round56.txt); own derivation, pre-registered). To leading order the joint law of two consecutive gaps is proportional to S({0, g₁, g₁ + g₂}) e^(−(g₁+g₂)/λ) and each marginal to S({0, g}) e^(−g/λ); the exponentials and λ cancel in the log-ratio, and the average factorises over the primes by the Chinese remainder theorem:

  **I(g_n; g_{n+1}) → I_∞ = Σ_{p≥3} I_p,  I_p = (p−1)/p² · f_A ln((1−1/p)/(1−2/p)) + (p−1)(p−2)/p² · f_B ln((1−3/p)(1−1/p)/(1−2/p)²),**

with f_A = (1−2/p)/(1−1/p)³ and f_B = (1−3/p)/(1−1/p)³ (the two residue patterns of {0, g₁, g₁ + g₂} mod p that change the ratio; p = 2 contributes nothing, since all gaps are even). I_∞ = 0.186908 nats = **0.26965 bits**, of which the prime 3 alone gives 0.25 bits; the closed sum agrees with direct enumeration of the local averages for p ≤ 61. Measured (plug-in mutual information of consecutive gaps with the Miller–Madow correction):

| window | consecutive pairs | mean λ | measured I (bits) | difference from I_∞ | λ × difference |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [10⁷, 10⁸) | 5,096,874 | 17.8 | 0.3222 | +0.0526 | 0.94 |
| [10⁹, 2·10⁹) | 47,374,751 | 21.1 | 0.3143 | +0.0447 | 0.94 |
| A (10¹⁰) | 2,171,132 | 23.0 | 0.3115 | +0.0418 | 0.96 |
| B (10¹²) | 1,808,831 | 27.6 | 0.3056 | +0.0359 | 0.99 |
| C (10¹⁵) | 578,835 | 34.5 | 0.3008 | +0.0311 | 1.08 |

By the pre-registered rule (difference below 0.02 bits at B) the reading **fails**: the difference is 0.036 bits there. What the thirteenth review found after the fact ([review13/mi.py](writeup/code/study/review13/mi.py) → [mi_A.out](writeup/data/study/review13/mi_A.out), [mi_B.out](writeup/data/study/review13/mi_B.out), [mi_C.out](writeup/data/study/review13/mi_C.out); [i_inf.py](writeup/code/study/review13/i_inf.py) → [i_inf.out](writeup/data/study/review13/i_inf.out)): a shuffle control (true information 0) leaves +0.0014 bits at B and +0.0062 at C after the Miller–Madow correction, so C is biased high; with C corrected, a fit a + b/λ gives a = +0.001, and a pure b/λ with b = 0.95 bits matches all five windows to 0.0006 bits. And the 1/λ term is the leading-order law's own: the mutual information of the discrete law ∝ S({0, g₁, g₁ + g₂}) e^(−(g₁+g₂)/λ), with exact marginals, is 0.3173, 0.3109, 0.3080, 0.3025 and 0.2967 bits at the five windows, 0.003–0.005 bits below the data. So the slow approach comes from the slow convergence of singular-series averages that the law itself predicts; I_∞ is its limit.

**Round 68: the same, out of sample with the right comparator** ([r68.py](writeup/code/study/r68.py) → [r68.json](writeup/data/study/r68.json), [round68.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round68.txt), pre-registered). Over the 361,840,206 consecutive gap pairs of [10¹², 10¹² + 10¹⁰) (estimator bias negligible): measured 0.3046 bits, the discrete leading-order law 0.3024 (data +0.0022; rule ≤ 0.006), and λ × (data − I_∞) = 0.966 (rule 0.95 ± 0.15). Both conditions hold: at λ = 27.6 the data are consistent with the coefficient 0.95 bits/λ of the approach to I_∞ = 0.26965 bits that the leading-order law predicts (the discrete law's own coefficient there is 0.91). This is one λ, and round 56's window B, the first 5·10⁷ numbers of this window, had already sampled it; 99.5 % of the data are new.

**Round 70: how the limit is approached** (theory, no data; [r70.py](writeup/code/study/r70.py) → [r70.json](writeup/data/study/r70.json)). The discrete leading-order law carries the slow approach of rounds 56 and 68, so it can be followed beyond any data (λ = 300 is x ≈ 10¹³⁰):

| λ | 21.1 | 27.6 | 40 | 60 | 100 | 150 | 200 | 300 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| I − I_∞ (bits) | 0.0413 | 0.0328 | 0.0238 | 0.0167 | 0.0107 | 0.0076 | 0.0059 | 0.0042 |
| λ × (I − I_∞) | 0.871 | 0.906 | 0.952 | 1.003 | 1.074 | 1.137 | 1.184 | 1.254 |

λ × (I − I_∞) keeps growing over λ = 21–300, at least like ln λ, so the information between consecutive gaps approaches I_∞ = 0.26965 bits more slowly than the 1/λ read off the data (the measured coefficient 0.95–0.97 at λ = 27.6 is this law's 0.91 plus the data's small positive remainder). As a description of λ = 21–300 only, (0.43 + 0.142·ln λ)/λ fits to 0.013 in λ × (I − I_∞) but with systematic residuals (signs + + − − − − + +), and a further c·ln²λ term cuts them to 0.0026 ([r70.py](writeup/code/study/r70.py) `fit`); the twenty-first review's own computation at λ = 600 gives λ × (I − I_∞) = 1.376, above the linear fit's 1.340 ([review21/mi70.py](writeup/code/study/review21/mi70.py)). So the form of the approach is not settled, only that it is slow and that I tends to I_∞; where the growth comes from (presumably the −½ ln H of singular-series averages) is not derived.


**Round 58: what the gap before last adds** ([r58_theory.py](writeup/code/study/r58_theory.py) → [r58_theory.json](writeup/data/study/r58_theory.json), [r58.py](writeup/code/study/r58.py) → [r58.json](writeup/data/study/r58.json), [round58.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round58.txt); own derivation, pre-registered). Three consecutive gaps (a, b, c) have, to leading order, the law ∝ S({0, a, a + b, a + b + c}) e^(−(a+b+c)/λ); the exponentials cancel again, and the conditional information of the gap before last, given the last, factorises over the primes:

  **I(g_{n+1}; g_{n−1} | g_n) → J_∞ = Σ_{p odd} E_{a,b,c mod p}[ f₄ ln( f₄ f₂(b) / (f₃(a, b) f₃(b, c)) ) ],**

with f_k the local factor (1 − ν/p)/(1 − 1/p)^k of the residue pattern. By exact enumeration for p ≤ 400 (the rest, which falls like 0.5/p³, is below 3·10⁻⁷) J_∞ = 0.104613 nats = **0.15093 bits** (p = 3: 0.0866 nats). Measured over long windows (plug-in with the Miller–Madow correction), with the discrete leading-order law at the same λ:

| window | consecutive triples | mean λ | measured (bits) | discrete leading-order law | data − law | data − J_∞ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [10⁷, 10⁸) | 5,096,873 | 17.8 | 0.1781 | 0.1704 | +0.0077 | +0.0272 |
| [10⁹, 2·10⁹) | 47,374,750 | 21.1 | 0.1710 | 0.1675 | +0.0035 | +0.0200 |
| [10¹⁰, 2·10¹⁰) | 427,154,202 | 23.4 | 0.1681 | 0.1659 | +0.0022 | +0.0172 |

The data follow the discrete leading-order law (summed to a + b + c ≤ 20λ; the first run's 12λ left it about 0.0007 bits high) to within 0.008, 0.004 and 0.002 bits, and their distance to J_∞ shrinks: by the pre-registered reading J_∞ is supported as the limit. Part of the data − law difference is estimator bias: a conditional shuffle control, whose true value is 0, still gives +0.0046 and +0.0012 bits at the first two windows after the Miller–Madow correction ([review14/cmi.py](writeup/code/study/review14/cmi.py) → [cmi_1e7_9e7.out](writeup/data/study/review14/cmi_1e7_9e7.out), [cmi_1e9_1e9.out](writeup/data/study/review14/cmi_1e9_1e9.out)); with it removed, a positive remainder of about 0.002–0.003 bits stays, like round 56's, and the bias-corrected λ × (data − J_∞) is about 0.40 in all three windows. (The pre-registration was committed 113 s before the data commit, without r58.py, so the commit order does not by itself show that the data were not looked at first.) So one gap tells about 0.27 bits about the next, and the gap before it about 0.15 bits more, in the limit of large x.

**Round 69: out of sample at 10¹²** ([r69.py](writeup/code/study/r69.py) → [r69.json](writeup/data/study/r69.json), [round69.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round69.txt), pre-registered with the discrete law as comparator). Over 361,840,205 consecutive gap triples of [10¹², 10¹² + 10¹⁰): measured 0.1655 bits; a conditional shuffle control (true value 0; g₃ permuted among triples with the same g₂, which keeps both pair tables) gives a bias of 0.0091 bits on a sub-window; the registered run scaled that as 1/N to 0.0003, but the bias falls more slowly because the occupied cells grow with the sample (the twentieth review ran the control at full size: 0.00047 bits, [review20 notes](writeup/data/study/review20/NOTES.txt)), so the data give 0.1651; the discrete leading-order law gives 0.1638 (difference +0.0013; rule ≤ 0.004): it holds, and λ × (data − J_∞) = 0.39, the coefficient of round 58.

**Round 61: at leading order the gap sequence has unbounded memory** ([r61.py](writeup/code/study/r61.py) → [r61.json](writeup/data/study/r61.json); theory only; the information formula is our derivation, the model behind it is not). At leading order the joint law of consecutive gaps factorises over the primes q, and the local factor at q is proportional to the number of residues mod q that no prime position occupies. That is exactly the random-sieve reading of the singular series (Granville's modified Cramér model; Banks, Ford and Tao, [arXiv:1908.08613](https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.08613)): a hidden residue a mod q, a ≡ −p (where the multiples of q sit; uniform, a ≠ 0 relative to the current prime), and prime positions independent and uniform on the other q − 1 residues. Gaps then inform about a only through which residues have appeared: with m distinct residues seen, a is uniform on the q − m others, and the next position has chance 1/(q − 1) on each seen residue and (q − m − 1)/((q − 1)(q − m)) on each unseen one. The count m among the current prime and the k before it grows by one with chance (q − 1 − m)/(q − 1). So the information the last k gaps carry about the next is a coupon-collector sum:

  **I_k = Σ_{q≥3} [ H_q(1) − E H_q(m_k) ]**,  H_q(m) the entropy of that predictive law.

It reproduces both constants above, I₁ = round 56's I_∞ (to 2e-08 nats) and I₂ − I₁ = round 58's J_∞ (to 7e-08), and gives every k:

| previous gaps k | 1 | 2 | 3 | 5 | 10 | 30 | 100 | 1,000 | 10,000 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| I_k (bits) | 0.270 | 0.421 | 0.527 | 0.678 | 0.903 | 1.232 | 1.583 | 2.150 | 2.595 |

As k → ∞ the hidden residue is revealed for every q, and the limit Σ_q ((q − 2)/(q − 1)) ln((q − 1)/(q − 2)) diverges like ln ln: 1.95, 2.53, 2.94, 3.26 bits for the primes up to 10², 10³, 10⁴, 10⁵. Correspondingly I_k grows like ln ln k (from k = 1,000 to 10,000 by 0.31 nats, against ln ln 10⁴ − ln ln 10³ = 0.29). So, at leading order (x → ∞ first, then k → ∞), the prime gap sequence has unbounded memory: each further past gap still says something about the next one, ever less, and the total grows without bound, but only like ln ln k (asymptotically: from k = 10 to 100 the growth is 0.47 nats against ln ln's 0.69). At any finite x the information is capped by the entropy of the next gap, log₂(e ln x) − 1.1 bits (4.9 bits at 10¹⁰), and the large q behind the divergence need residues that are uniform at the given λ, which they are not for q comparable to λ. (It is the same divergence as in the predictability law: knowing p mod every prime up to y removes about log₂ ln y bits.) At finite x the finite-λ terms of rounds 56, 58 and 70 come on top; only k = 1, 2 have been tested against data.

**Round 62: memory tested on residues** ([r62.py](writeup/code/study/r62.py) → [r62.json](writeup/data/study/r62.json), [round62.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round62.txt), pre-registered). Beyond k = 2 gap values are too sparse to estimate, but the residues c = g mod 30 are not: the model of round 61 restricted to q = 3, 5, at the finite λ of the window (each residue class weighted by Σ e^(−g/λ) over its gaps, times the free residues mod 3 and mod 5 of the prime positions), against the 427,154,204 gaps of [10¹⁰, 2·10¹⁰):

| k | data (bits) | model at λ = 23.4 | data − model | model, λ → ∞ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.3072 | 0.3039 | +0.0033 | 0.2641 |
| 2 | 0.4704 | 0.4620 | +0.0085 | 0.4086 |
| 3 | 0.5823 | 0.5694 | +0.0128 | 0.5073 |
| 4 | 0.6655 | 0.6487 | +0.0167 | 0.5809 |

By the pre-registered rule (|data − model| ≤ 0.01 bits for every k) the reading **fails** at k = 3 and 4. The model gets the shape: every further gap adds information, decreasingly (data increments 0.163, 0.112, 0.083 bits, model 0.158, 0.107, 0.079), on the way to the λ → ∞ saturation of 0.8113 bits for these residues. But the data keep about 0.004 bits more per extra gap, the same sign as round 56's remainder above the discrete law; it was natural to suspect the finite-λ interior correlations, but a diagnostic after the failed reading ([r62b.py](writeup/code/study/r62b.py) → [r62b.json](writeup/data/study/r62b.json), [r62c.py](writeup/code/study/r62c.py) → [r62c.json](writeup/data/study/r62c.json)) shows that the registered comparator was itself truncated: at finite λ the weights e^(−g/λ) fall by e^(−30/λ) ≈ 0.27 per step inside a residue class mod 30, so the class nearly fixes g mod 7 and the prime 7 belongs in the finite-λ leading-order law. Including q = 7 brings the differences at k = 1, 2, 3 from 0.0033, 0.0085 and 0.0128 to 0.0014, 0.0047 and 0.0076 bits (k = 3 from the sixteenth review, [review16/](writeup/code/study/review16/) → [r62_model.out](writeup/data/study/review16/r62_model.out)), below the 0.01 rule at k = 3, while q = 11 adds only 0.0002 at k = 1; the remainder is presumably the interior structure of the gaps (not computed). The sixteenth review also replicated the data on [10¹⁰, 1.1·10¹⁰) (differences +0.0033 … +0.0169).

**Round 72: an online predictor that learns p from the gaps** ([r72.py](writeup/code/study/r72.py) → [r72.json](writeup/data/study/r72.json), [round72.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round72.txt); own construction, pre-registered). Rounds 45 and 61 are one thing: the hidden residue of round 61 is a ≡ −p mod q, and the past gaps reveal it, because each earlier prime p − D (D a sum of past gaps) is coprime to q. So a predictor that sees only the previous gaps can keep the set of classes p mod 30030 consistent with them, A_k = {a coprime to 30030 : a − D_j coprime for j ≤ k}, and predict the next gap as the average over A_k of the conditioned law P(g | a) of round 47 (order 2, y = 13; each class's law sums to 1 within 2·10⁻⁴ before renormalising). As k grows, A_k shrinks to the true class by coupon collecting at each q. On 20,000 target primes in [10⁹, 1.1·10⁹), the mean log-loss of the next gap:

| previous gaps known, k | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 5 | 10 | 20 | 40 | 60 | true class |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| log-loss of the next gap (bits) | 4.676 | 4.355 | 4.191 | 4.071 | 3.897 | 3.648 | 3.428 | 3.275 | 3.252 | 3.249 |
| share of the gain, data | 0.000 | 0.225 | 0.340 | 0.424 | 0.546 | 0.721 | 0.874 | 0.982 | 0.998 | 1 |
| coupon-collector formula | 0.000 | 0.210 | 0.327 | 0.410 | 0.526 | 0.699 | 0.850 | 0.969 | 0.995 | 1 |

Both pre-registered predictions hold: the total gain on real primes, 1.4264 bits, equals the model's own information I(gap; p mod 30030) = 1.4265 bits (difference -0.0002; rule 0.02), and the learning curve follows the coupon-collector shape of round 61 restricted to q ≤ 13 within 0.025 at every k (rule 0.05; the data run slightly ahead, the finite-λ weights). So the next gap can be predicted from the previous gaps alone with 1.43 bits less uncertainty (about 30 %) after some 40 gaps, within 0.025 of the coupon-collector formula, slightly ahead of it at every k (the residue repulsion, rounds 73–74); with more small primes (y > 13) the gain and the memory should keep growing, like ln ln y and ln ln k (heuristic, not computed here). The test is one number, the mean log-likelihood ratio: under the model its expectation is I_model by identity, and the data enter through the gaps that occur (standard error 0.004 bits; the absolute log-losses are both about 0.011 bits above the model's, which cancels in the gain).

**Round 73: how many gaps reveal p mod 30030** ([r73.py](writeup/code/study/r73.py) → [r73.json](writeup/data/study/r73.json), [round73.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round73.txt), pre-registered). On round 72's 20,000 targets, the number T of past gaps after which only one class p mod 30030 is left (every target was resolved by 150 gaps, and the surviving class was always the true one):

| past gaps k | 20 | 30 | 40 | 50 | 60 | 80 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P(p mod 30030 known after k gaps), data | 0.051 | 0.442 | 0.774 | 0.920 | 0.974 | 0.997 |
| coupon-collector prediction | 0.016 | 0.256 | 0.606 | 0.824 | 0.926 | 0.988 |

By the pre-registered rule (CDF within 0.03) the prediction **fails**: the largest difference is 0.198 (at k = 34), and the mean is 34.0 gaps against the predicted 39.7. A diagnostic added after the run (`r73.py diag`) separates the two assumptions. Independence across q holds to about 0.005 (the size of the cross-q dependence a first-order chain carries anyway, per the twenty-second review): the joint CDF equals the product of the per-q empirical CDFs within 0.0045. Uniformity fails: each q is resolved 11–14 % sooner than the uniform coupon collector says,

| q | 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| mean gaps until p mod q is known, data | 1.78 | 6.43 | 11.91 | 24.41 | 31.11 |
| uniform coupon collector | 2.00 | 7.33 | 13.70 | 28.29 | 36.24 |

because consecutive primes repeat their residue mod q less often than 1/(q − 1): near 10⁹ a gap is divisible by 3, 5, 7 in 44.7, 18.3 and 10.3 % of cases, against 50, 25 and 16.7 %. This is the Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan repulsion between consecutive primes, a finite-λ effect of order ln λ/λ that the leading-order coupon model of round 61 leaves out. The information curve of round 72 hides it (within 0.025), because the information a residue carries is spread over many steps; the completion time, an extreme of the collection, shows it plainly. So the memory formula of round 61 is the λ → ∞ form, and at λ ≈ 21 the gaps reveal p faster than it says.

**Round 74: the reveal time from the conditioned chain** ([r74.py](writeup/code/study/r74.py) → [r74.json](writeup/data/study/r74.json), [round74.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round74.txt), pre-registered; no new data). The correction to round 73 from our own law, with nothing fitted: a Markov chain on the classes mod 30030 whose backward step from class a is a gap drawn from round 72's conditioned law P(g | −a) (the sieve and S_{>13} are symmetric under reflection). By the Chinese remainder theorem p mod 30030 is known exactly when p mod q is known for every q, so T = max_q T_q. Over 200,000 chains:

| q | 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| mean gaps until p mod q is known, data | 1.78 | 6.43 | 11.91 | 24.41 | 31.11 |
| conditioned chain | 1.79 | 6.51 | 12.14 | 24.94 | 31.90 |
| uniform coupon collector | 2.00 | 7.33 | 13.70 | 28.29 | 36.24 |
| P(gap ≡ 0 mod q), chain | 0.4476 | 0.1824 | 0.1027 | 0.0454 | 0.0309 |

The chain gives the one-step repeat probabilities of the data within the counting noise (0.4476, 0.1824, 0.1027 against 0.4475, 0.1827, 0.1030 for q = 3, 5, 7 over all 4.8 million gaps of the window; differences at most 2.6·10⁻⁴, 1.6σ), so the one-step residue repulsion is reproduced by the conditioned law (the sieve by q ≤ 13 plus S_{>13} at λ). Two steps apart it is not: P(p_n ≡ p_{n−2} mod q) is 0.4911, 0.2367, 0.1485, 0.0765, 0.0598 in the data against 0.4943, 0.2379, 0.1495, 0.0768, 0.0605 for the chain (6–14σ for q = 3, 5, 7, 13; the twenty-second review, recounted here), so the primes repel more at lag 2 than any chain on classes mod 30030 can, the memory of round 75; the conditioned law for two gaps at once reproduces it (round 79). Two of the three registered readings pass: the mean reveal time is 34.9 gaps against the data's 34.0 (rule: within 1.0; the uniform coupon model said 39.7), and every per-q mean is within 2.5 % (rule 5 %). The first fails narrowly: the largest CDF difference is 0.033 at k = 30 (rule 0.03; it was 0.198 for the coupon model), many times the counting noise of 0.0035. The chain is 0.8–2.5 % slow, growing with q (1.008, 1.012, 1.020, 1.022, 1.025 for q = 3 … 13; at q = 3 only 1.6σ), in the direction expected from what it leaves out, which the registration named as the likely cause: each gap depends here only on the current class, not on the earlier gaps beyond it (the S_{>13} correlations between consecutive gaps, rounds 56–58).

**Round 75: where the remaining lag comes from** ([r75.py](writeup/code/study/r75.py) → [r75.json](writeup/data/study/r75.json), [round75.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round75.txt), pre-registered). Round 74's chain with the empirical table instead of the theory table: the gap before p, tabulated by p mod 30030 over the 4,814,936 primes in [10⁹, 1.1·10⁹) (at least 772 per class). This keeps the true class dependence and drops only the dependence of a gap on earlier gaps beyond the class. Over 200,000 chains the mean reveal time is **34.92** gaps, against 34.91 for the theory table and 33.99 in the data (per q: 1.79, 6.51, 12.12, 24.95, 31.90; largest CDF difference to the data 0.034; 1 chain unresolved by 150). By the registered reading the lag is (B): both chains on the classes mod 30030, with the true table and with ours, are slow by about 0.9 gaps and agree within the simulation noise (each mean has a standard error of 0.025; the twenty-second review's own chains give 34.90 and 34.96, so the resolution is about ±0.06 gaps), so in this statistic the order-2 conditioned table carries the class dependence of the gaps, and the lag is memory beyond the class. Gaps are below 30030, so the class sequence fixes the gaps, and the empirical chain differs from the data only in that memory; the stronger repulsion at lag 2 (round 74) shows it directly. That its source is the S_{>13} correlations, the primes q ≥ 17, is the Hardy–Littlewood reading (q ≤ 13 factorise once the class is fixed), supported by round 76. (The statistic is sensitive to the table: one built at 10¹² gives 36.09, per the review.)

**Round 76: a chain that holds q = 17 closes part of the lag** ([r76.py](writeup/code/study/r76.py) → [r76.json](writeup/data/study/r76.json), [round76.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round76.txt), pre-registered). The theory chain of round 74 on the 92,160 classes mod 510510 (y = 17; each class's law sums to 1 within 1.3·10⁻⁴ before renormalising), the reveal time still that of p mod 30030. Over 200,000 chains the mean is **34.73** gaps, against 34.91 at y = 13 and 33.99 in the data: 0.17 gaps lower (4.8 standard errors of the difference; the registration's "about 5 standard errors" for its 0.1 threshold was too generous, 0.1 is 2.8), so by the registered reading the explanation of round 75 is supported. The chain closes 19 % of the lag, and its largest CDF difference to the data falls to 0.027, inside round 74's rule of 0.03. (Per q: 1.79, 6.49, 12.07, 24.82, 31.77.) An observation made after the run, tested in round 77: if each prime q beyond the class contributes to the lag in proportion to 1/q², the size of its pair correlations, then q = 17's share is (1/289)/Σ_{q≥17} 1/q² = 0.21, close to the 0.19 measured.

**Round 77: a law for the lag, failed as registered** ([r77.py](writeup/code/study/r77.py) → [r77_y7.json](writeup/data/study/r77_y7.json), [r77_y11.json](writeup/data/study/r77_y11.json), [r77_y13.json](writeup/data/study/r77_y13.json), [round77.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round77.txt), pre-registered). The registered law: a chain on the classes mod P_y is slow by lag(y) = lag(13)·W(y)/W(13), W(y) = Σ_{q>y} 1/q², which predicted mean reveal times 35.24 (y = 11) and 35.69 (y = 7). The chains give **29.57** and **26.44**: far too fast, so the law fails. The registration's caveat was the cause, and it is no small effect: a chain that does not hold the class mod 13 does not know that the earlier primes avoid p's residue mod 13 (D ≢ p mod 13), so all 13 residues occur and 12 distinct ones come too soon. That is a zero-order rule left out, not a pair correlation, and the law cannot cover target primes beyond the class. (A control at y = 13 with this script, other seed and cut-off, gives 34.91, as round 74.)

What the runs do show, found after them ([r77b.py](writeup/code/study/r77b.py) → [r77b.json](writeup/data/study/r77b.json)): for each target prime q ≤ y the mean reveal time is linear in W(y), T_q(y) = A_q + B_q·W(y), and the intercept, a chain that holds every prime, lands on the data:

| target q | 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| chains used (y) | 7, 11, 13, 17 | 7, 11, 13, 17 | 7, 11, 13, 17 | 11, 13, 17 | 13, 17 |
| largest residual of the line | 0.0009 | 0.0080 | 0.0158 | 0.0015 | – |
| intercept A_q (W → 0) | 1.777 | 6.424 | 11.927 | 24.453 | 31.272 |
| primes near 10⁹ (round 73) | 1.777 | 6.434 | 11.906 | 24.407 | 31.115 |

**Round 78: the lines tested beyond their range** ([r78_y5.json](writeup/data/study/r78_y5.json), [r78_y3.json](writeup/data/study/r78_y3.json), [round78.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round78.txt), pre-registered from round 77's lines; W(5) = 0.051 and W(3) = 0.091 lie two and three times beyond the fitted range 0.013–0.031). At y = 5 (8 classes) T₃ = 1.8061 against the predicted 1.8144 and T₅ = 6.6606 against 6.6704: both pass (rules 0.01 and 0.03). At y = 3 (2 classes) T₃ = 1.8105 against 1.8441: off by 0.034, rule 0.02, **fails**; the line bends at the far end (the lag saturates; the order-2 law is also less exact there, each class's law summing to 1.0025). So, within the classes a chain holds, the lag it leaves is linear in the summed pair weights Σ 1/q² of the primes it leaves out, over y = 5–17, and extrapolating to W = 0 lands on the primes' own reveal times for q = 5–11. The weight of this is limited (the twenty-third review, [review23/linefit.py](writeup/code/study/review23/linefit.py)): for q = 3 the lag is only 0.01–0.02 and lines in 1/y or 1/y² give intercepts as good; for q = 13 the line has two points and its intercept an error of about 0.15, the size of its miss; q = 7 and 11 do favour W (residuals 0.016 and 0.0015 against 0.035–0.05 for 1/y and 1/y²); both y = 5 values fall below the line and y = 3 fails. A post hoc description from two to five chains per target, not derived.

**Round 79: the repulsion two steps apart, from the law for two gaps at once** ([r79.py](writeup/code/study/r79.py) → [r79.json](writeup/data/study/r79.json), [round79.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round79.txt), pre-registered). The twenty-second review found that primes two steps apart repeat their residue less often than any chain on classes gives. The conditioned law for two consecutive gaps at once, P_a(g₁, g₂) = ρ₀² S_{>13}({0, g₁, g₁ + g₂}) exp(L′_a + Q′_a) (first-order interior term exact for the base {0, g₁, g}; the order-2 term taken from the single-gap base {0, g} without the terms of g₁, an approximation stated in the registration), averaged over the classes, against the 4,814,934 pairs of primes two apart in [10⁹, 1.1·10⁹):

| q | 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P(p_{n+2} ≡ p_n mod q), primes near 10⁹ | 0.4911 | 0.2367 | 0.1485 | 0.0765 | 0.0598 |
| two-gap conditioned law | 0.4911 | 0.2365 | 0.1486 | 0.0764 | 0.0600 |
| z | +0.1 | -0.9 | +0.9 | -1.1 | +2.0 |
| chain of one-gap laws | 0.4943 | 0.2379 | 0.1495 | 0.0768 | 0.0605 |
| z | +14.2 | +6.2 | +6.5 | +2.8 | +6.2 |

The reading passes: the two-gap law is within 2σ of the data for every q (rule 4σ), where the chain misses by 2.8–14σ (the chain column, from the same code, reproduces the review's chain values). So the extra repulsion at lag 2 is the correlation between consecutive gaps through the three-point singular series (S_{>13}({0, g₁, g}) differs from the product of the pair factors S_{>13}({0, g₁}) S_{>13}({g₁, g})): the memory beyond the class that rounds 74–76 measured as a lag is, at this step, what Hardy–Littlewood's three-point correlation predicts, within the counting noise. (Checks: each class's two-gap law sums to 1.0006–1.0012 over spans up to 300, the price of the approximation; its first-gap marginal equals the one-gap law within 3·10⁻⁴.)

**Round 80: a chain that remembers one gap is not enough** ([r80.py](writeup/code/study/r80.py) → [r80.json](writeup/data/study/r80.json), [round80.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round80.txt), pre-registered). A second-order chain built from round 79's two-gap law (each backward gap drawn given the previous gap and the class of the prime two steps back), 200,000 chains. Both predictions **fail**. The mean reveal time is 34.92 gaps, no better than the first-order chain run with the same code (34.94; data 33.99; rule: within 0.3 of the data). Out of sample, the repeat probability three steps apart misses by 6–17σ, no closer than the first-order chain:

| q | 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| lag 2: primes | 0.4911 | 0.2367 | 0.1485 | 0.0765 | 0.0598 |
| lag 2: second-order chain | 0.4910 | 0.2365 | 0.1485 | 0.0763 | 0.0601 |
| lag 3: primes | 0.4958 | 0.2443 | 0.1591 | 0.0878 | 0.0721 |
| lag 3: second-order chain | 0.4996 | 0.2469 | 0.1605 | 0.0888 | 0.0728 |
| lag 3: first-order chain | 0.4993 | 0.2471 | 0.1604 | 0.0888 | 0.0726 |

The chain does carry the lag-2 repulsion it was built on (0.4910 against 0.4911), but the excess repulsion of the primes is as large three steps apart as two (0.0035 against 0.0032 for q = 3) and the chain does not pass it on: in the data it is not a short-range Markov effect but persists over several primes. So the memory beyond the class that slows every chain (rounds 74–76) is long-range, and the chains tested (first and second order) do not carry it; a law for the k-th neighbour itself does (round 81). (The z values here use the data's standard error only; the chains' Monte Carlo error, about 10⁻⁴, makes the 0.0003 difference between the two chains at lag 3, q = 3, insignificant, but not the 6–17σ misses.)

**Blind stress test of the two-gap law** (a helper agent's computation, [stresstest/](writeup/code/study/stresstest/); predictions written to prediction*.txt with timestamps before any prime was counted; the statistic is new, the windows are those of rounds 79 and 85). The joint distribution of two consecutive gaps (g₁, g₂) from round 79's two-gap construction, kept per cell: in 36 cells (g₁, g₂ ≤ 36) χ² = 35.1 at 10⁹ and 36.5 at 10¹² (p = 0.51, 0.45; full 18 × 18 grid, exploratory: 262.8 and 262.9 on 252), against 567 and 221 for the chain of one-gap laws and 17,970 and 8,658 for independent gaps; the mod-6 table χ² 1.8 and 8.9 on 7 against 132 for the chain; P(6 | g₂ given 6 | g₁) within the box 0.42779 by the law against 0.42748 ± 0.00040 in the primes (0.43066 for the chain; the data value rechecked here); single values of p_(n+2) − p_n at 10¹² within 1.8σ (the chain off by up to 5.9σ). One blemish, found after the cell values were seen: at 10⁹ the law overpredicts long-gap cells (max(g₁, g₂) 38–70) by 0.27 % (z −2.9), not at 10¹² (+0.04 %); the two-gap law's row sums of 1.0006–1.0012 are a likely cause.

**A skeptic's verdict on the k-th neighbour law** (a helper agent's literature check and computation, [skeptic/](writeup/code/study/skeptic/); the LO-S quotations were read through a summarizer and are unverified against the PDF). Partly new. The substance is Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's: their Conjecture 1.4 treats pairs (p_n, p_(n+k)) for k ≥ 2, they remark that the k = 2 transition matrix is not the square of the k = 1 matrix (the non-Markov point made here with chains), and their Section 2 is Hardy–Littlewood plus inclusion–exclusion truncated at the same order. What is ours is computational: the generating function per class mod 30030, the exponentiated pair term, the evaluation at finite λ, and the tests. Objections that stand: no bound on the third-order truncation; the law's mass is off by about 2·10⁻⁴, which the conditional ratios cancel by design; the result is sensitive to λ (a 2 % shift gives 9–10σ misses; λ = ln x at the window middle was not tuned); the 116 values are correlated and their errors binomial. At 10⁹ crude Hardy–Littlewood truncations pass the 4σ rule after fitting one λ shift (worst 3.1–3.3σ), so round 81 alone is weak evidence; at 10¹² they fail (6–12σ) while the law stays within 2σ, and LO-S's expanded two-term formula misses the k ≥ 2 values by up to 24σ (10⁹), 70σ (10¹²) and 33σ (10¹⁵). The decisive comparison a referee would ask for, against LO-S's own unexpanded evaluation (their Section 5), was not made.

**Independent rebuild and ablation of the k-th neighbour law** (a helper agent's computation from the formulas alone, [rebuild/](writeup/code/study/rebuild/): law.py, data.py, compare.py; own sieve, own singular-series products, a power series in z instead of the Fourier sum). At y = 7 (210, 48 classes) the law already gives the 20 repeat probabilities at 10⁹ within 1.90σ (χ² 23.9), and at y = 13 it reproduces the repo's values to 3.7·10⁻⁹; from y = 7 to 13 the values move by at most 0.14σ. Ablations (χ² over the 20 values at y = 7 / 11 / 13): full law 24 / 24 / 24; without the pair term R 593 / 382 / 247 (up to 13.5σ at k = 1); with S_(>y) ≡ 1 20,847 / 9,047 / 1,272; plain Cramér about 2·10⁶. So the singular-series factor carries most of the agreement and the pair term the rest; exponentiating the expansion matters little for the repeat probabilities (χ² 26 without it) but much for the distance distribution of D_k (band χ² 100–530 without it, 9–32 with it).

**Null test of the k-th neighbour law** (a helper agent's computation, [nulltest/](writeup/code/study/nulltest/): fakes.py, analyse.py, own_pred.json; not pre-registered). Fake prime sequences in [10⁹, 1.1·10⁹) with exactly the primes' density, compared with the law (round 81's values) on the same repeat probabilities (k = 1–4, q = 3–13, D ≤ 240): the Cramér model misses by 763σ; a sequence with the exact sieve by q ≤ 13 but independent otherwise misses by 14–16σ (χ² ≈ 1,200–1,400 on 20; it matches its own exact prediction), while the primes are within 1.9σ. Random sieves to z fail for z ≤ 31, are marginal at 47 and pass from 71 on. So the test can fail, and the primes' agreement confirms the Hardy–Littlewood correlations from the primes 17 to about 50 at 15σ; it cannot tell the primes from a random sieve to 70 or more, whose correlations at D ≤ 240 differ only by about Σ_(p>70) 1/p².

**Round 81: a law for the k-th neighbour** ([r81.py](writeup/code/study/r81.py) → [r81.json](writeup/data/study/r81.json), [round81.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round81.txt), pre-registered; own construction). Instead of chaining gap laws, count the primes between p and p + D directly. In class a, with the same ingredients as the gap law (the three-point ratios p_i for each rough interior point and the four-point ratios R_ij for each pair, around the conditioned base {0, D}), the number of interior primes has at order 2 of the Bahadur expansion the generating function

  **G_a(z) = exp( Σ_i ln(1 + p_i (z − 1)) + ½ Σ_{i≠j} R_ij w_i(z) w_j(z) ),  w_i(z) = p_i (z − 1)/(1 + p_i (z − 1)),**

(sums over the rough interior points of the class; z = 0 gives the gap law of round 47, z = 1 gives 1), and P_a(p + D is the k-th prime after p) = ρ₀ S_{>13}({0, D}) [z^{k−1}] G_a(z); the coefficient is a discrete Fourier sum over 48 points of the unit circle. (Literature: biases in the residues of consecutive primes, including patterns of r consecutive primes and hence repeats k steps apart, are Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's conjecture, an asymptotic expansion in 1/ln x derived from Hardy–Littlewood; what is ours is the generating function per class, evaluated at finite λ with no expansion in 1/ln x, and the precision tests.) Derivation: in Bahadur form P(x) = P_ind(x)·[1 + Σ_{i<j} R_ij p_i p_j (x_i − p_i)(x_j − p_j)/(p_i(1 − p_i) p_j(1 − p_j))]; under P_ind, E[z^{x_i}(x_i − p_i)] = p_i(1 − p_i)(z − 1) and E[z^{x_i}] = 1 + p_i(z − 1), so the pair term contributes R_ij w_i(z) w_j(z) relative to Π(1 + p_i(z − 1)), and the sum is exponentiated as in round 47. Repeat probabilities k steps apart, P(p_{n+k} ≡ p_n mod q | p_{n+k} − p_n ≤ 240), against the 4.8 million primes in [10⁹, 1.1·10⁹):

| k | | q = 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | k-th neighbour law | 0.4477 | 0.1824 | 0.1027 | 0.0454 | 0.0309 |
| | primes near 10⁹ | 0.4475 | 0.1827 | 0.1030 | 0.0453 | 0.0308 |
| | z | +0.9 | -1.3 | -1.7 | +1.2 | +0.6 |
| 2 | k-th neighbour law | 0.4911 | 0.2365 | 0.1486 | 0.0763 | 0.0600 |
| | primes near 10⁹ | 0.4911 | 0.2367 | 0.1485 | 0.0765 | 0.0598 |
| | z | +0.1 | -0.9 | +0.8 | -1.2 | +1.8 |
| 3 | k-th neighbour law | 0.4957 | 0.2445 | 0.1590 | 0.0880 | 0.0718 |
| | primes near 10⁹ | 0.4958 | 0.2443 | 0.1591 | 0.0878 | 0.0721 |
| | z | -0.4 | +0.8 | -0.8 | +1.6 | -1.9 |
| 4 | k-th neighbour law | 0.4972 | 0.2442 | 0.1612 | 0.0944 | 0.0774 |
| | primes near 10⁹ | 0.4972 | 0.2443 | 0.1613 | 0.0945 | 0.0772 |
| | z | +0.0 | -0.8 | -0.6 | -0.9 | +1.3 |

All twenty values are within 1.93σ (the readings: k = 2, 3, 4 within 4σ; k = 1 is the check of round 74). Where every chain failed (round 80: 6–17σ at k = 3), the k-th neighbour law, with nothing fitted, gives the long-range repulsion of the primes at every lag tested: the memory of the gap sequence beyond the class is the Hardy–Littlewood correlation of p with its k-th neighbour and the primes between, which the first- and second-order chains tested do not carry. (The total mass within D ≤ 240 is right to 2·10⁻⁴: 0.99998, 0.99978, 0.99997, 0.99925 against the data's 1.00000, 0.99999, 0.99986, 0.99919, the order-2 normalisation error, which largely cancels in the conditional repeat probabilities; it need not be spread evenly over D mod q, and the law's mass is not monotone in k as true probabilities must be.)

**Round 82: the k-th neighbour law at 10¹² and beyond the class** (r81.py with the window [10¹², 10¹² + 2·10⁹), D ≤ 320, k ≤ 6, q ≤ 23 → [r82.json](writeup/data/study/r82.json), [round82.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round82.txt), pre-registered; the law unchanged). All **48** repeat probabilities (k = 1–6, q = 3–23) are within **2.00σ** of the primes (χ² = 48.8 for 48 values; rule: every value within 4σ), including q = 17, 19, 23, which the law knows only through S_{>13}, not through the classes. A selection (k = 4, 5 in the JSON):

| k | | q = 3 | 5 | 7 | 11 | 13 | 17 | 19 | 23 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | law | 0.4588 | 0.1964 | 0.1148 | 0.0539 | 0.0389 | 0.0235 | 0.0186 | 0.0121 |
| | primes | 0.4588 | 0.1964 | 0.1148 | 0.0538 | 0.0389 | 0.0235 | 0.0186 | 0.0121 |
| 2 | law | 0.4934 | 0.2400 | 0.1535 | 0.0825 | 0.0661 | 0.0440 | 0.0373 | 0.0272 |
| | primes | 0.4934 | 0.2399 | 0.1534 | 0.0825 | 0.0661 | 0.0439 | 0.0373 | 0.0273 |
| 3 | law | 0.4967 | 0.2446 | 0.1603 | 0.0921 | 0.0756 | 0.0526 | 0.0456 | 0.0350 |
| | primes | 0.4967 | 0.2446 | 0.1602 | 0.0921 | 0.0755 | 0.0526 | 0.0456 | 0.0350 |
| 6 | law | 0.4980 | 0.2473 | 0.1633 | 0.0960 | 0.0801 | 0.0601 | 0.0531 | 0.0432 |
| | primes | 0.4980 | 0.2472 | 0.1632 | 0.0960 | 0.0801 | 0.0601 | 0.0531 | 0.0432 |
| | independent residues, 1/(q − 1) | 0.5000 | 0.2500 | 0.1667 | 0.1000 | 0.0833 | 0.0625 | 0.0556 | 0.0455 |

The repulsion is strongest for the nearest neighbour (k = 1: 0.4588 against 1/2 for q = 3) and fades with k towards the independent value; the law follows it at every k and q to the counting noise. (Mass within D ≤ 320, law against data: 1.0000/1.0000, 0.9999/1.0000, 0.9999/0.9998, 0.9989/0.9988, 0.9953/0.9953, 0.9853/0.9852.)

**Round 83: the whole distance to the k-th neighbour** ([r83.py](writeup/code/study/r83.py) → [r83.json](writeup/data/study/r83.json), [round83.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round83.txt), pre-registered). Not only residues: the law of round 81 gives the distribution of D_k = p_{n+k} − p_n itself. In 20 bands of width 12 up to 240, against the primes in [10⁹, 1.1·10⁹):

| k | data pairs (D ≤ 240) | k-th neighbour law χ² / dof | 0.999 quantile | Gamma(k) χ² | k independent gaps χ² | chain on classes χ² |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 4,814,860 | 22.2 / 19 (largest |z| 2.3) | 43.8 | 180,467 | 3,581 | 533 |
| 3 | 4,814,260 | 16.7 / 19 (largest |z| 2.0) | 43.8 | 230,396 | 9,136 | 1,717 |
| 4 | 4,811,006 | 8.2 / 19 (largest |z| 1.3) | 43.8 | 233,195 | 14,278 | 3,279 |

The reading passes for k = 2, 3, 4 (k = 1 is the gap law, χ² 26.2 / 16). The Poisson baseline at finite λ is off by 10⁵ in χ² (the sieve; a parity-aware Cramér model, negative binomial in D/2, still gives 0.8–1.2·10⁵, per the twenty-third review), the sum of k independent gaps from the true class-averaged gap law by 10³–10⁴ (it loses both the class sieve and the memory beyond it), and the exact first-order chain on classes mod 30030, which keeps the class sieve, still by 533, 1,717 and 3,279 (added after the twenty-third review, which computed it first; [r83.py](writeup/code/study/r83.py) now reproduces it): the shape of D_k carries the memory beyond the class, and the k-th neighbour law carries it. (No bands were pooled for k = 2–4; the registration's wording of the pooling rule differs from the code's forward pooling, which only affected k = 1. The χ² of 8.2 on 19 at k = 4 is low, at the 1.5 % point of the lower tail; the bands of neighbouring k share primes, so the three values are not independent.)

**Round 84: a third scale, 10¹⁵** (r81.py with [10¹⁵, 10¹⁵ + 10⁹), D ≤ 400 → [r84.json](writeup/data/study/r84.json), [round84.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round84.txt), pre-registered; the law unchanged). All 48 repeat probabilities (k = 1–6, q = 3–23) are within **2.15σ** (χ² = 50.6 for 48 values). The nearest-neighbour repulsion fades with x as the theory says: P(p_{n+1} ≡ p_n mod 3) = 0.4475, 0.4588, 0.4658 at 10⁹, 10¹², 10¹⁵ (law: 0.4477, 0.4588, 0.4658), towards the 1/2 of independent residues. A fair comparator, added after the run ([r82c.py](writeup/code/study/r82c.py) → [r82c.json](writeup/data/study/r82c.json); lesson of rounds 83, 85, 89): the exact first-order chain on the classes mod 30030 at the window's λ. For q ≤ 13 and k = 2–6 (25 values) it misses by up to 56σ at 10¹² and 33σ at 10¹⁵ (Σz² 39,039 and 14,391), against the law's 25.1 and 26.5 (the split is stored in [r90b.json](writeup/data/study/r90b.json)); for q = 17–23 it misses by up to 108σ, but there it lacks the hard rule as in round 77 (the data-to-chain ratio is close to q/(q − 1)), so only the q ≤ 13 values are a fair comparison. A stronger chain, on the classes mod 510510, still misses q ≤ 13 at 10¹² by 12.6–42.8σ and q = 17 by 9.5–24σ (twenty-sixth review; k = 2: 41.7σ for q = 3, 12.6σ for q = 13, 9.5σ for q = 17, rechecked here). Over the three scales the k-th neighbour law has now been tested on 116 values without a miss beyond 2.15σ (the values share primes, so the χ² sums 24.5/20, 48.8/48 and 50.6/48 are only indicative).

**Round 85: the last-digit table** ([r85.py](writeup/code/study/r85.py) → [r85_1e9.json](writeup/data/study/r85_1e9.json), [r85_1e12.json](writeup/data/study/r85_1e12.json), [round85.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round85.txt), pre-registered). The best-known form of the bias: a prime ending in 1 is followed by one ending in 1 far less often than 1/4. Round 81's law, split by the class of p mod 10, gives the whole table P(last digit of p_{n+k} = b | last digit of p_n = a). At 10⁹, k = 1 (law / primes):

| last digit of p_n → of p_{n+1} | 1 | 3 | 7 | 9 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.1858 / 0.1862 | 0.2963 / 0.2964 | 0.2989 / 0.2983 | 0.2189 / 0.2190 |
| 3 | 0.2409 / 0.2410 | 0.1791 / 0.1789 | 0.2811 / 0.2816 | 0.2989 / 0.2984 |
| 7 | 0.2548 / 0.2549 | 0.2698 / 0.2698 | 0.1791 / 0.1791 | 0.2963 / 0.2962 |
| 9 | 0.3185 / 0.3177 | 0.2548 / 0.2547 | 0.2409 / 0.2412 | 0.1858 / 0.1864 |

All 96 entries (k = 1, 2, 3 at 10⁹ and 10¹²) are within **2.01σ** of the primes (rule 4σ; the Pearson χ² of the tables is 25.4 and 24.5 on 36 free entries, on the low side; the sum of the 48 squared binomial z's, expectation about 48, is 34.1 and 32.6; the k-tables share primes, so no p-value is given). The binomial standard errors overstate the noise: block estimates give about 8 % smaller ones (variance 0.75–0.87 of binomial, presumably the rigidity of prime counts per class), which raises the largest |z| to 1.94 at 10⁹ and 2.08 at 10¹² (twenty-fourth review). The k = 1 entries test only the per-class gap law; the k = 2, 3 entries test the k-th neighbour law beyond it. For comparison ([r85b.py](writeup/code/study/r85b.py) → [r85b.json](writeup/data/study/r85b.json)): the uniform 1/4 misses by up to 203, 46, 15σ (k = 1, 2, 3) at 10⁹ and 595, 123, 56σ at 10¹²; the exact first-order chain on classes mod 30030 equals the law at k = 1 but misses at 10⁹ by 3.8σ (k = 2) and 7.2σ (k = 3), Σz² 88 and 255 (the review found 20.7 and 27.4σ at 10¹², 14.2 and 17.2σ at 10¹⁵). The table flattens with x as the law says (1 → 1: 0.1858 at 10⁹, 0.1981 at 10¹²; primes 0.1862, 0.1982) and with k (1 → 1 two and three steps apart: 0.2323, 0.2447). The bias had been noticed numerically before and was quantified and explained by Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan (2016) from Hardy–Littlewood, with an expansion in ln ln x/ln x and 1/ln x; here the same table comes out of the finite-λ k-th neighbour law with nothing fitted, to the counting noise (their expansion was not evaluated here for a head-to-head comparison).

**Round 86: the last-digit table at 10¹⁵** (r85.py with [10¹⁵, 10¹⁵ + 10⁹), D ≤ 400 → [r86_1e15.json](writeup/data/study/r86_1e15.json), [round86.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round86.txt), pre-registered). All 48 entries are within **2.27σ** (rule 4σ; Pearson χ² 20.0 on 36, low, at the 1.4 % point of the lower tail; Σz² 26.7 for 48; with block standard errors the largest |z| is 2.38; uniform 1/4 misses by up to 307, 60, 32σ). The bias keeps fading as the law says: 1 → 1 is 0.1858, 0.1981, 0.2063 at 10⁹, 10¹², 10¹⁵ by the law and 0.1862, 0.1982, 0.2063 for the primes.

**Round 87: a fourth scale, 10¹⁸** ([r87.py](writeup/code/study/r87.py) → [r87.json](writeup/data/study/r87.json), [round87.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round87.txt), pre-registered; the law unchanged, λ = 41.45, D ≤ 480). On the 48,428 consecutive primes in [10¹⁸, 10¹⁸ + 2·10⁶] (the list of round 17, never used for this law), all 48 repeat probabilities (k = 1–6, q = 3–23) are within **2.38σ** (Σz² 37.0 for 48; rule 4σ). The sample is small (standard errors 0.0006–0.0023), and its power is limited: at k = 1 the primes are 13σ from the independent value 1/2 for q = 3 (more for larger q, where 1/(q − 1) is off mainly because the mean gap, 41, is below 2q), but the meaningful comparator, the first-order chain on classes, differs from the law by at most 1.3 standard errors per value for q ≤ 13 and 2.7 for q = 17–23 (twenty-fifth review); over the 40 values with k ≥ 2 the primes give Σz² 29.1 against the law and 145.8 against that chain (correlated values). The law sits on the primes (P(p_{n+1} ≡ p_n mod 3) = 0.4707 by the law, 0.4698 for the primes). With rounds 81, 82 and 84 the k-th neighbour law has now passed at 10⁹, 10¹², 10¹⁵ and 10¹⁸.

**Round 88: the repeat probabilities do not depend on the conditioning level** ([r88.py](writeup/code/study/r88.py) → [r88.json](writeup/data/study/r88.json), [round88.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round88.txt), pre-registered). Round 81 recomputed with the 92,160 classes mod 510510 (y = 17) instead of the 5,760 mod 30030: the 20 repeat probabilities at 10⁹ move by at most **0.020** of the data's standard error (rule: below 0.5), and stay within 1.91σ of the primes. So the repeat probabilities of the k-th neighbour law (10⁹, D ≤ 240) are converged in the conditioning level at this precision; per D the y = 17 law moves by up to 0.33 % in the tail (D = 150, k = 1, per the twenty-fifth review), as round 59's twin tail moved with y: what the classes mod 17 add exactly, the order-2 expansion of S_{>13} already carried. (Unlike the chains of rounds 74–78, whose lag depended on y: there the memory beyond the class was missing, here it is in the law.)

**Round 89: the k-th neighbour law for twin primes** ([r89.py](writeup/code/study/r89.py) → [r89.json](writeup/data/study/r89.json), [round89.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round89.txt), pre-registered; our application of round 81's construction to twin events, not found in the literature, where twin-gap work (Wolf, Kelly–Pilling, Goldston–Ledoan) treats consecutive gaps and Gallagher's limit would give Gamma(k)). Round 59's law for the distance between consecutive twin pairs is the z⁰ coefficient of the same kind of generating function, over the interior twin starts h of each class (twin chance p_h = ρ₀² s_h, pair ratios R_hk, order 2, S_{>13} exact up to 3,000; the twenty-fifth review confirmed that the z⁰ term equals r59.py's law to 6·10⁻¹⁵); its z^{k−1} coefficient gives the distance to the k-th next twin pair. Against the twin pairs in [10⁹, 2·10⁹) in round 59's 30 bands (bands with an expected count below 50 dropped, as in round 59: one at k = 3), conditioned on d ≤ 1,200, the law averaged over the window as in round 59:

| k | pairs (d ≤ 1,200) | law χ² / dof | 0.999 quantile | largest |z| | chain on twin classes χ² | k independent twin gaps χ² |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2,889,561 | 30.4 / 29 | 58.3 | 2.03 | — (= the law) | — (= the law) |
| 2 | 2,601,773 | 14.2 / 29 | 58.3 | 1.50 | 797 | 11,361 |
| 3 | 2,055,527 | 28.3 / 28 | 56.9 | 3.08 | 714 | 6,705 |

Both readings pass (k = 1 is the control; it reproduces round 59's fit, χ² 30.4 here against 30.7 there with a different normalisation). The comparators separate two things. A sum of k independent twin gaps misses by χ² 11,361 and 6,705 (largest |z| 42 and 31), mostly because it puts twin starts into classes that are not allowed. The exact first-order chain on the 1,485 twin-start classes mod 30030 (r89.py, added after the twenty-fifth review, which built it first; averaging the chain over λ instead of its step law, the review gets 802 and 731) keeps the class sieve and still misses by χ² 797 and 714 (largest |z| 10.5): that remainder is memory beyond the class, and the law removes it, as for the primes in round 83. (The k = 2 value of 14.2 on 29 is low, at the 1 % point of the lower tail; the k-th distances share gaps, so the bands are not independent, with block dispersion 1.07–1.19 per the review. The comparison covers the bulk: the mass within 1,200 is 0.975, 0.877, 0.693 by the law and 0.975, 0.878, 0.694 in the data; at k = 1 the conditioning hides round 59's known tail miss, z = −3.7 on the mass.)

**Round 90: down to 10⁷** ([r90.py](writeup/code/study/r90.py) → [r90.json](writeup/data/study/r90.json), [round90.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round90.txt), pre-registered). Where the order-2 expansion is least accurate: the 5,096,876 primes in [10⁷, 10⁸) (λ from 16.1 to 18.4), the law averaged over the window with weights e^u/u. All 20 repeat probabilities (k = 1–4, q = 3–13, D ≤ 200) are within **1.98σ** (Σz² 13.6 for 20; rule 4σ). What matters at this width is the right λ, not the spread: the law at the single λ = 17.656, the primes' mean ln p, gives max |z| 1.67 (Σz² 13.7), and the data fix a single effective λ to 17.655 ± 0.02 (4σ band 17.56–17.79, per the twenty-sixth review), while the window's middle λ, 17.27, would miss by 11σ ([r90b.py](writeup/code/study/r90b.py) → [r90b.json](writeup/data/study/r90b.json)). The class chain mod 30030 at λ = 17.656 misses k = 2–4 by up to 17σ (Σz² 1,465 over 15 values; at k = 1 it equals the law), so the test has power. The k-th neighbour law passes in every window tested from 10⁷ to 10¹⁸ (λ = 16–41).

**Round 91: the pair correlation of the zeros, from primes** ([round91.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round91.txt), pre-registered; written up in [RIEMANN.md](RIEMANN.md) section 23). Through Goldston–Montgomery and RH (fact 2047), Montgomery's pair correlation of the zeros is equivalent to I(X, δ) = ∫₁^X (ψ((1+δ)x) − ψ(x) − δx)² dx ~ ½δX² ln(1/δ); with Montgomery–Soundararajan's second-order constant, R = I/(½δX²) = ln(1/δ) + B, B = −1.41509. At X = 10⁹ the primes give R within 0.066 of it for δ = 10⁻³ … 10⁻⁶ (rule 0.3), against 1.4 off for the leading term alone (expected: the constant is Hardy–Littlewood's, not part of the Goldston–Montgomery asymptotic) and 7–14 for the uncorrelated Cramér model, R = ln X − 3/2 (the registration wrote ln X − ½, an error found by the twenty-eighth review, which also found that the first grid of points was commensurate with the integers; the values quoted are from stratified random points). **Round 92** ([round92.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round92.txt), pre-registered): at X = 10¹⁰, δ = 10⁻³ … 10⁻⁷, within 0.057. **Round 93** ([round93.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round93.txt), pre-registered; [r93.py](writeup/code/study/r93.py) → [r93.json](writeup/data/study/r93.json)): at fixed δ = 0.1 and 0.01 the primes' R drifts with X (0.97, 1.23, 1.28 at δ = 0.1), and the explicit formula with the 2,419 lowest zeros alone reproduces every value to 0.0001–0.003; the diagonal (each zero with itself) is constant, the pairs of distinct zeros oscillate in ln X, and with the smooth Riemann–von Mangoldt density the diagonal equals Hardy–Littlewood's exact value (to O(δ²): −δ²/144), whose constant 1 − γ − ln 2π is Chan's (2002) diagonal constant ([r93_B.py](writeup/code/study/r93_B.py)); the zeros–primes agreement itself is a numerical identity checking both computations. **Round 95** ([round95.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round95.txt), pre-registered; the δ = 0.1 and 0.01 values were known beforehand): the ln X-average exceeds Hardy–Littlewood's exact value by (7/4)δ + O(δ²), from the constant 7/8 in N(T) (passed with 10,000 zeros); the thirtieth review then found the exact closed form under RH (and simple zeros) via the Guinand–Weil explicit formula, D(δ) = ln(1/δ) − γ − ln 2π + 2 ln(1 + δ) + ((2 + δ)/δ) ln(1 + δ/2), whose difference from Hardy–Littlewood is (7/4)δ − (7/8)δ² + (19/32)δ³ − …. **Round 96** ([round96.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round96.txt), pre-registered): from primes alone, the average of R over 200 log-spaced X in [10⁷, 10¹⁰] is 1.0930 at δ = 0.1 (D = 1.1027, Hardy–Littlewood 0.9359) and 3.1687 at δ = 0.01 (D = 3.2125); both readings pass, but only the δ = 0.1 one separates D from Hardy–Littlewood. **Rounds 97–98** (pre-registered): the swing of R around D has RMS (2/δ)√(2Q) over ln X (0.186, 0.282, 0.275 at δ = 0.1, 0.03, 0.01); round 97's sum-rule prediction of the repulsion effect failed, and in round 98 the swing is within 1.7σ of the registered (pinned) GUE sets at every δ (passes) but not 2σ below Poisson sets (fails); at δ = 0.01 it lies below every GUE set, z = −3.2 against stationary GUE sets (thirty-second review, not registered): the zeros are more rigid than GUE in this statistic. **Round 100** ([round100.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round100.txt), pre-registered): replicated at δ = 0.005 and 0.003 with our own stationary GUE sets, z = −4.0 and −4.6, below all 30 sets each time (the δ are strongly correlated); the thirty-fourth review explained the deficit quantitatively by the arithmetic correction to the GUE form factor (Berry; Bogomolny–Keating): there is no prime power below 2, and the predicted ΔRMS −0.0040, −0.0023, −0.0016 matches the observed −0.0042, −0.0024, −0.0016. Details in RIEMANN.md section 23. **Round 94** ([round94.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round94.txt), pre-registered; a forward check of the explicit formula, a numerical identity): the zeros alone, evaluated first, gave R at three new X at the extremes of the swing (0.853 at 3.27·10⁹, 1.482 at 3.90·10⁹, 1.125 at 2·10¹⁰ for δ = 0.1); the primes, counted afterwards, land within 0.0007 (δ = 0.1) and 0.010 (δ = 0.01). Details in RIEMANN.md section 23. **Round 99** ([round99.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round99.txt), pre-registered): the tube of fact 2048 for primes mod 4 and mod 3 (fact 2049, RIEMANN.md section 22): C_χ = 0.155568 and 0.113230, used to 0.65 and 0.62 up to 10⁹; all readings pass. **Round 101** ([round101.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round101.txt), pre-registered): the same tube for the even Legendre symbol mod 5, with an extra ln y term from the trivial zero at s = 0: C = 0.156557, used to 0.58 up to 10⁹; all readings pass.

**What this answers.** The next prime cannot be predicted from p alone, but its predictability is a parameter-free function of how much of p's residue structure is known: hit as above (roughly 1/(u ω(u)) for large y, with u = ln p / ln y; the 0.001 accuracy needs the exact product ∏(1 − 1/q), not the u-only form, which is off by up to 0.03 at small y), and about h₂(hit)/hit bits remain. It is a heuristic, the product of the Hardy–Littlewood factor and Buchstab's density of rough numbers; the data cannot test the product form as such, since at every y one of the two factors is within about 1 % of 1 (the pair factor above 0.998 for y ≥ 97, e^γω(u) within 1 % at large u). The ingredients (Buchstab, Mertens, Hardy–Littlewood) are classical; their combination into this law and its tests are ours. From the past gaps alone (rounds 72–84): they reveal p mod 30030 after about 34 gaps, which gives 1.43 bits on the next gap; the residue memory they carry reaches over several primes, is not carried by the short chains of gap laws tested, and is given, at every lag tested, by the k-th neighbour law.

**Round 104: prime-rich polynomials have low zeros** ([round104.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round104.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r104.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r104.py) → r104.json, follow-up [r104b.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r104b.py) → r104b.json, not pre-registered; PARI lfunzeros). The chain: a quadratic is prime-rich when χ_D(p) = −1 for many small p (its Bateman–Horn constant is ∏(1 − χ(p)/(p − 1)), about 1/L(1, χ_D)); then L′/L(1, χ_D) is large, and the exact identity Σ_ρ 1/|ρ|² = 2 Re L′/L(1, χ) + ln(q/π) + ψ((1 + a)/2) (fact 2049) forces zeros near the central point. L(s, χ₋₁₆₃), the L-function of n² + n + 41, has its first zero at height 0.2029 (χ₋₄: 6.02). Over all 3,040 fundamental discriminants with 3 ≤ |D| ≤ 5000, the scaled first zero g = γ₁ ln|D|/(2π) has Spearman correlation 0.87 (D < 0) and 0.87 (D > 0) with ln L(1, χ_D) and -0.60 and -0.66 with the log of the first split prime: readings (1) and (2) pass. Reading (3) fails: no Heegner discriminant is among the ten lowest (−163, with g = 0.164, ranks 11th of 1,524; the tenth has 0.158); discriminants with even smaller L(1) have lower zeros (−2787: γ₁ = 0.069). Follow-up: if the first pair of zeros takes the whole excess of the identity's right side over the median of discriminants of similar size, 2/(¼ + γ₁²) = S − S_med + 2/(¼ + γ_med²), the predicted γ₁ has rank correlation 0.987 and 0.986 with the true one, and a median error of 0.017 and 0.013 on the 200 lowest. So the lowest zero is fixed by one arithmetic number, L′/L(1, χ), which weighs every prime by ln p/p. This answers the open point of RIEMANN §18.1, where small primes alone fixed the lowest zero only to R² = 0.45 (p ≤ 199) with weights that hardly fall with p: the right variable is the whole sum, not a truncation. Euler's polynomial is prime-rich and L(s, χ₋₁₆₃) has a low zero for the same reason, read on the two sides of one identity.

**Round 105: one low zero steers the prime race** ([round105.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round105.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r105.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r105.py) → r105.json; follow-up [r105b.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r105b.py) → r105b.json, not pre-registered). For the 3,040 discriminants of round 104, the race between the primes p ≤ 10⁵ with χ_D(p) = −1 and those with +1: bias(D) = the share of primes at which the −1 side leads. The registered readings all fail, with the wrong sign: the Spearman correlation of bias with the scaled first zero is +0.33 (D < 0) and +0.05 (D > 0), with S it is -0.35 and -0.10, and the median bias of the 100 lowest first zeros is 0.43 and 0.13 (all: 0.74, 0.79). The data show why: a low zero does not add a fixed bias, it adds a slow wave in ln x. With γ₁ ≈ 0.07 (D = −2787) the wave has not turned by 10⁵ (γ₁ ln 10⁵ ≈ 0.8) and the −1 side leads at every prime; with γ₁ ≈ 0.3 it turns near x ≈ 10³ and the +1 side leads from there on (D = −232, −267, −795: bias 0.04–0.06). Follow-up: the normalised race E(x) = 1 + 2 Σ_(0<γ≤30) Re(x^(iγ)/ρ) (Rubinstein–Sarnak's explicit formula; the 1 is the prime squares) predicts the measured share for each D with Pearson correlation 0.981 and a mean error of 0.039; the lowest zero alone gives 0.889. With round 104 this closes a chain in which every link is measured on the same 3,040 L-functions: the small primes set L′/L(1, χ) (how prime-rich the quadratic is), L′/L(1, χ) sets the lowest zero (rank 0.99), and the lowest zero sets the phase of the race (0.89; 0.98 with the zeros up to 30). The links are classical (Hadamard's product, Rubinstein–Sarnak); measuring the chain end to end is ours.

**Round 106: the long-range variance shortfall is the zeros' swing** ([round106.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round106.txt), pre-registered; [r106.py](writeup/code/study/r106.py) → [r106.json](writeup/data/study/r106.json)). MERSENNE section 34 found the variance of ψ(n + h) − ψ(n) − h over the integers n in [N/2, N], N = 10⁷, below Montgomery–Soundararajan's h(ln(n/h) + B) by 0.963, 0.924, 0.879 at h = 10³, 3·10³, 10⁴, unexplained. The same mean square from the explicit formula, ψ(x + h) − ψ(x) − h = −Σ_ρ ((x + h)^ρ − x^ρ)/ρ with the first 10,000 zeros (40,000 stratified random x) plus the diagonal tail above the last zero, against a fresh sieve, as ratios to the formula: h = 1,000: measured 0.963, zeros 0.969; h = 3,000: measured 0.925, zeros 0.936; h = 10,000: measured 0.879, zeros 0.881. All three readings pass (differences 0.006, 0.012, 0.002).
