The formula is a long-run average; the pairs of distinct zeros make the variance swing slowly in ln x (rounds 96–98), and the window [N/2, N], only ln 2 long, sits in a trough of that swing (at N = 10⁶ the same page saw 1.016 and 1.037). Not a failure of the law, and not noise: a deterministic term that the zeros give exactly.

**Round 107: the lowest zero of every character mod 1009** ([round107.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round107.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r107.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r107.py) → r107.json). The family of RIEMANN §18.1's type, all 1,007 non-trivial characters mod the prime 1009, complex ones included, with γ_min the zero nearest the real axis and S = 2 Re L′/L(1, χ) + ln(q/π) + ψ((1 + a)/2). Reading (1) passes: the γ_min predicted from S (the lowest zero alone taking the excess of S over its median) has rank correlation 0.937 with the true one. Reading (2) fails: a straight-line fit of γ_min on that prediction has R² = 0.22, against 0.745 for the least-squares fit on Re χ(p), p ≤ 199, plus the parity (48 regressors, in sample). Diagnosis, after the fact: for 39 characters S lies so far below its median that the inversion has no solution (their first zero is high, and one zero cannot take a negative excess), and those points ruin a straight line. In the identity's own variable the relation is linear: Pearson 0.926 between 1/(¼ + γ_min²) and S (R² 0.86). So one weighted prime sum fixes the lowest zero of complex characters too, in rank and in the identity's variable, better than the truncated small-prime fit; but the registered measure failed and the comparison that wins was chosen afterwards.

**Round 108: the Euler-run drift was chance** ([round108.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round108.txt), pre-registered; [euler41/runs_law2.py](writeup/code/study/euler41/runs_law2.py) → runs_law_r108.json, [runs_data2.py](writeup/code/study/euler41/runs_data2.py) → runs_data_r108.json). On fresh n in [10⁷, 3·10⁷) the runs of prime values of n² + n + 41, observed / expected: r = 4: 31985 / 32039.7 · r = 5: 6422 / 6438.3 · r = 6: 1341 / 1313.6 · r = 7: 288 / 266.4 · r = 8: 67 / 54.8 · r = 9: 14 / 11.2 · r = 10: 1 / 2.3 (ratios 0.998, 0.998, 1.021, 1.081, 1.223, 1.249, 0.431). Reading (1), chance, holds; reading (2), a systematic fall, fails: the ratio at r = 8 is now above 1. The longest run in the range is again 10 values (from n = 16,168,993). Round 103's fall from 1.000 to 0.856 was one fluctuation shared by nested counts.

**Round 109: the race-variance misses of round 102 are the zeros' swing** ([round109.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round109.txt), pre-registered; [race/r109.py](writeup/code/riemann/race/r109.py) → r109.json). Round 102's means over 200 log-spaced X in [10⁶, 10⁹] of the race variance R_χ(X, δ) recomputed from the zeros of L(s, χ) alone (PARI lfunzeros up to 1500: 1,399 zeros for χ₄, 1,331 for χ₃; conjugate pairs; the pair sum of round 93 plus the diagonal tail): chi4 at δ = 0.1: zeros 2.3281, measured 2.3279, law 2.3460; chi4 at δ = 0.01: zeros 4.5575, measured 4.5748, law 4.5838; chi3 at δ = 0.1: zeros 2.1306, measured 2.1306, law 2.0583; chi3 at δ = 0.01: zeros 4.4375, measured 4.4491, law 4.2962. Both readings pass: χ₃ at δ = 0.01 within 0.012 of the measured mean (the law is 0.153 away), all four within 0.017; point by point over the 200 heights the correlation is 0.9988–1.0000. Round 102's failed χ₃ reading was the zeros' slow swing over a window three decades long, not a fault of D_χ.

**Round 110: zero rigidity is not a function of the density alone** ([round110.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round110.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/getzeros.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/getzeros.py) → [lzeros/](writeup/data/riemann/lzeros/), [r110.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r110.py) → r110.json). RIEMANN §23 noted, unexplained, that the unfolded nearest-neighbour spacing variance of ζ's first 10,000 zeros is 0.154–0.156 against 0.180 for GUE. Claim tested: the deficit is the arithmetic rigidity of L6, shared by every L-function and set by the density L = ln(qt/2π) alone. Zeros of L(s, χ_D) for D = −3, −4, 5, −163 (1,123–2,536 each; 1009 and −3635 had too few per bin), unfolded with N(t) = (t/2π) ln(qt/2πe), variance per bin of L with block-bootstrap errors. Reading (1) passes: every value lies below 0.180. Reading (2) fails clearly: at the same density the real characters of conductor 3, 4, 5 are more rigid than ζ (bin 6 ≤ L < 7: 0.132, 0.133, 0.133 against ζ's 0.155; z = −7.3 to −8.4; χ₋₄ at 7 ≤ L < 8: 0.139 against 0.157), while χ₋₁₆₃ at 9 ≤ L < 11 gives 0.162. A follow-up (not pre-registered) with complex characters, whose χ² is not principal (so the factor |L(1 + iε, χ²)|² of the Bogomolny–Keating pair correlation has no pole), gives 0.139 (mod 7, order 6) and 0.143–0.144 (mod 13, order 12) at 6 ≤ L < 8: between the real characters and ζ, so the pole alone does not sort them either. The order real q = 3, 4, 5 < complex mod 7 < complex mod 13 < ζ < χ₋₁₆₃ is not explained; at finite height the rigidity of a family of zeros depends on more than its density.

**Round 111: the same chain for cubic fields** ([round111.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round111.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r111.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r111.py) → r111.json). All S3 cubic fields with |disc| ≤ 5000 (162 totally real, 729 complex; PARI nflist), with the Artin L-function ζ_K/ζ of degree 2 and the same identity Σ 1/(ρ(1 − ρ)) = 2 L′/L(1) + ln(N/π²) + Σ_j ψ((1 + μ_j)/2). Reading (1) passes: Spearman between ln L(1) and the scaled first zero 0.78 and 0.80. Reading (3) passes: the first zero predicted from the identity has rank correlation 0.983 and 0.987 with the true one (Pearson between S and 2/(¼ + γ₁²): 0.947, 0.962). Reading (2) fails: the first prime at which the defining cubic has a root correlates only -0.07 and -0.18 with the scaled zero, because for cubics that prime is nearly always 2, 3, 5 or 7 (a prime of type (1, 2) also gives a root), so it carries little information; L(1) does. Lowest Artin zero among the complex fields: 0.3749 for x³ + 5x² + 8x + 17 (disc −4511). So the chain of rounds 104–105, local primes → L(1) and L′/L(1) → the lowest zero, holds for degree 2 Artin L-functions with the same strength; the record cubic itself (disc 5.4·10¹⁴) remains out of reach here.

**Round 112: Chebyshev's 26861 from the zeros of L(s, χ₋₄)** ([round112.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round112.txt), pre-registered; [race/r112.py](writeup/code/riemann/race/r112.py) → r112.json, check [r112_check.py](writeup/code/riemann/race/r112_check.py) → r112_check.json, r112_clusters.json). The normalised race E(x) = 1 + 2 Σ Re(x^(iγ)/ρ) over the zeros of L(s, χ₋₄), no prime counted (the registration named the 1,399 zeros below 1500; the run used the file of round 110, the 2,536 zeros below 2500 — a deviation from the registration), class 1 ahead where E < 0. Reading (1) fails: E first dips below 0 at x = 461 (and at 8,694–8,778), two short false dips where the dropped terms of order 1/ln x matter. After them every true region is found: our sieve to 10⁹ gives the regions where π(x; 4, 1) > π(x; 4, 3) as 26,861–26,863, 616,841–633,799, 12,306,137–12,382,327 and 951,784,481–952,223,491; the zeros give 26,721–27,049, 613,967–654,692, 12,062,203–12,462,871 and 940,880,069–942,418,588 (the last 1.1 % early). Reading (2) passes, 0.70 % of the primes up to 1.4·10¹¹ predicted against 0.44 % (fact 1602), but partly by luck: the predicted regions are too wide, and up to 10⁹ the zeros give 0.80 % against a true 0.015 %. So the zeros find every lead region of the mod 4 race below 10⁹, with ends off by up to 3.3 % (654,692 against 633,799) and the last region 1.1 % early; the widths and the share need the lower-order terms.

**Round 113: the same race with li(x^ρ) and the squares** ([round113.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round113.txt), pre-registered; [race/r113.py](writeup/code/riemann/race/r113.py) → r113.json; follow-up with all 2,536 zeros → r113_all.json). D(x) = π(x; 4, 3) − π(x; 4, 1) = Σ_ρ li(x^ρ) + (π(√x) − 1)/2, li(x^ρ) = Ei(ρ ln x) from its asymptotic series, the 1,399 zeros of L(s, χ₋₄) below 1500 as registered, π(√x) counted exactly (the squares, the local term). Predicted regions with D < 0 below 10⁹: 127–127, 461–463, 26,837–26,933, 616,626–634,191, 12,311,805–12,384,276 against the true 26,861–26,863, 616,841–633,799, 12,306,137–12,382,327, 951,784,481–952,223,491. Reading (3) passes: share of primes to 10⁹ 0.0057 % against 0.0150 % (round 112 gave 0.80 %). Readings (1) and (2) fail: two short false dips remain at x = 127 and 461 (the constant and cube terms, dropped here, matter there), and the region at 951,784,481 is missed (also with all 2,536 zeros, whose regions are 26,829–26,944, 616,597–633,963, 12,308,763–12,381,687). With the lower-order terms the zeros give the three regions below 10⁸ to within 0.3 % at both ends (the largest miss 0.26 %, at 26,933 against 26,863), where round 112's ends were off by up to 3.3 %: the zeros, the size term li and the squares together set the race; what is left out is visible at small x and in the shallowest region.

**Round 114: degree 3** ([round114.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round114.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r114.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r114.py) → r114.json). All S4 quartic fields with |disc| ≤ 20,000 (PARI nflist; 44, 822, 501 fields for its signature parameter s = 0, 1, 2), the Artin L-function ζ_K/ζ of degree 3, gamma shifts from lfunparams, S = 2 L′/L(1) + ln(N/π³) + Σ_j ψ((1 + μ_j)/2). Both readings pass for s = 1 and 2: Spearman between ln L(1) and the scaled first zero 0.70 and 0.80; the first zero predicted from S has rank correlation 0.985 and 0.984 with the true one (s = 0, reported only: 0.75 and 0.979). With rounds 104 and 111: in degree 1, 2 and 3 alike, one weighted prime sum fixes the lowest zero to rank 0.98–0.99.

**Round 115: S does not order the rigidity** ([round115.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round115.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r115.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r115.py) → r115_part0.json, r115_part1.json, r115.json). The spacing variance of the zeros with 100 ≤ γ ≤ 600 of 61 real L-functions, every third fundamental discriminant with 100 ≤ |D| ≤ 400 (about 744 spacings each). Reading (1) fails: Spearman with S is -0.08 (with ln L(1) +0.07, with γ₁ +0.13, with ln q +0.07); the idea from four points in round 110 is refuted. Reading (2) passes: all 61 lie below 0.180. Their mean is 0.150 (sd 0.012 across L-functions, against about 0.007 from sampling), close to ζ's 0.155, so the outliers of round 110 are the smallest conductors, 3, 4 and 5, at 0.132; why they are more rigid is open.

**Round 116: a missing small prime makes the zeros more rigid** ([round116.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round116.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r116.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r116.py) → r116_part0.json, r116_part1.json, r116.json). Found after the fact in round 115: when a small prime divides D, so that it is missing from the Euler product of L(s, χ_D), the zeros' spacing variance is lower (2 | D: 0.139 against 0.155, Mann–Whitney p = 6·10⁻⁶; 3 | D: 0.138 against 0.153; 5 | D: 0.137 against 0.152). Replication on 60 fresh L-functions (the next third of 100 ≤ |D| ≤ 400, none shared with round 115), all three readings pass: 2 | D 0.1395 (n = 24) against 0.1499, one-sided p = 0.004; 3 | D 0.1341 against 0.1504, p = 7.4e-06; 5 | D 0.1337 against 0.1491, p = 8.2e-05; and the variance falls by 0.0143 per missing prime among 2, 3, 5 (t = -12.8). This explains round 110's outliers (conductors 3, 4, 5 each miss one of the smallest primes; χ₋₁₆₃ misses only 163 and sits with ζ). The reading (*heuristic*; the per-prime test is ours as far as we found, the mechanism is Berry's and Bogomolny–Keating's, and ζ's own finite-height deficit is treated by Bogomolny, Bohigas, Leboeuf and Monastra 2006 through an effective matrix size with an arithmetic constant): in the Berry–Keating analogy the primes are the periodic orbits of the zeros' spectrum, each short orbit ln p adds weight to the form factor at small τ, and an L-function that lacks it is more rigid, as ζ is more rigid than random matrices because no prime lies below 2. Tested only for 2, 3, 5, real characters, 100 ≤ |D| ≤ 400 and heights 100–600; adding ln|D| as a covariate leaves the drops unchanged (thirty-seventh review).

**Round 117: the size of the drop, from Berry's diagonal approximation** ([round117.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round117.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r117z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r117z.py) → [lzeros/r117_part*.npz](writeup/data/riemann/lzeros/), [r117.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r117.py) → r117.json). Prime p adds to the zeros' connected pair correlation (unfolded, L = ln(qt/2π)) the term (2/L²) Σ_k (ln p)² p^(−k) cos(2πεk ln p/L); removing it changes the number variance in a window of one mean spacing by dΣ²(1) = −(4/L²) Σ_k (ln p)² p^(−k) (1 − cos a_k)/a_k² = −(2/π²) Σ_k sin²(πk ln p/L)/(k² p^k), a_k = 2πk ln p/L: Berry's 1988 number-variance formula (Nonlinearity 1, 399) taken one prime at a time, with no fitted parameter (the thirty-seventh review derived it independently; the test is ours). Measured Σ²(1) for the 121 L-functions of rounds 115–116 (zeros 100 ≤ γ ≤ 600, mean L = 9.38), least squares on indicators of 2 | D, 3 | D, 5 | D: p = 2: fitted -0.0121 ± 0.0014, predicted -0.0100 (ratio 1.22); p = 3: fitted -0.0143 ± 0.0016, predicted -0.0122 (ratio 1.18); p = 5: fitted -0.0145 ± 0.0018, predicted -0.0125 (ratio 1.16). All three readings pass: negative, within a factor 2 (in fact within 22 %), and in the predicted order (a weak test: 3 and 5 differ by 0.0002 against a standard error of 0.0016). The diagonal prediction is low by 16–22 %, each ratio 1.2–1.6σ above 1 (about 2.4σ pooled); the off-diagonal terms of Bogomolny–Keating are a candidate, not checked. So the weight (ln p)²/p^k of each small prime can be read off from how rigid the zeros are.

**Round 118: only whether a prime is present counts, not its sign** ([round118.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round118.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r118.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r118.py) → r118.json). In the diagonal approximation of round 117 a prime enters with weight |χ(p)|², so χ_D(2) = +1 and −1 should give the same rigidity. Least squares of Σ²(1) for the 121 L-functions on a constant, 2 | D, 3 | D, 5 | D and χ_D(2) (40 odd D with +1, 39 with −1): χ(2) coefficient -0.0008 ± 0.0009, within 2 standard errors of 0 (reading (1) passes), while the indicators keep their round-117 values (-0.0121, -0.0140, -0.0146). The sign of χ(2) does not matter at this precision; the common 16–22 % excess of round 117 is not a sign effect.

**Round 119: the long-H deviations of rounds 10 and 34 from the zeros** ([round119.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round119.txt), pre-registered; [r119.py](writeup/code/study/r119.py) → [r119.json](writeup/data/study/r119.json)). Var/mean of prime counts in consecutive intervals of length H per block of 10⁸ (rounds 10, 34), recomputed from the explicit formula: counts − H/ln(mid) from −2 Re Σ [li((x + H)^ρ) − li(x^ρ)] over the first 10,000 zeros of ζ (li by its asymptotic series) on the same grid, plus the diagonal tail. [1·10⁹, H = 10^5]: measured 0.3859, law 0.3875, zeros 0.3863; [1·10⁹, H = 10^6]: measured 0.2662, law 0.2784, zeros 0.2659; [3·10⁹, H = 10^5]: measured 0.4014, law 0.4116, zeros 0.4105; [3·10⁹, H = 10^6]: measured 0.2898, law 0.3068, zeros 0.2950; [5·10⁹, H = 10^5]: measured 0.4188, law 0.4235, zeros 0.4222; [5·10⁹, H = 10^6]: measured 0.3106, law 0.3209, zeros 0.3107. Reading (3) passes: the zeros are nearer the measurement than the Hardy–Littlewood law in all 6 cases. Readings (1) and (2) fail in the window at 3·10⁹ (+1.8 % at H = 10⁶, +2.3 % at H = 10⁵; the other four within 0.8 %, two within 0.1 %): there the random-phase tail or the truncation at 10,000 zeros is not good enough. What this shows (thirty-seventh review): the explicit formula reproduces the sampled values (at 10⁹, H = 10⁶, interval by interval with correlation 0.88–0.95 per block), so each window's deviation is the zeros' term for that window; it does not show a systematic effect, since the deviations are about 1σ of the block-to-block scatter, as rounds 10 and 34 said, and at H = 10⁵ about 84 % of the zero value is the random-phase tail model (14 % at H = 10⁶).

**Round 120: the same law for 7, 11 and 13** ([round120.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round120.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r120z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r120z.py) → [lzeros/r120_part*.npz](writeup/data/riemann/lzeros/), [r120.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r120.py) → r120.json). All 181 fundamental discriminants with 100 ≤ |D| ≤ 400, Σ²(1) of the zeros with 100 ≤ γ ≤ 600 on a dense grid of window starts (step 0.01 mean spacings, removing the sampling noise of round 117's random windows), least squares on indicators of p | D for p = 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, against Berry's per-prime formula averaged over each group: 2: -0.0111 ± 0.0007 against -0.0100; 3: -0.0130 ± 0.0008 against -0.0122; 5: -0.0135 ± 0.0009 against -0.0125; 7: -0.0116 ± 0.0010 against -0.0117; 11: -0.0100 ± 0.0013 against -0.0100; 13: -0.0075 ± 0.0013 against -0.0093. All three readings pass: all six coefficients negative; the fitted coefficients against the predictions have slope 1.02 through the origin; 7, 11 and 13 pooled give -0.0097 ± 0.0007. With the dense grid and the full sample, round 117's 16–22 % excess shrinks to 8–11 % for 2, 3, 5 and vanishes for 7 and 11. Each of the six smallest primes, when missing from the Euler product, lowers the zeros' number variance at ℓ = 1 by about the amount Berry's formula gives for it. (Thirty-eighth review: the fit leaves out primes above 13 that divide D; with their predicted drop as an offset, p = 2 moves to −0.0105, an excess of 5 %, and p = 13 to −0.0088 against −0.0093; their own coefficient is 1.03 ± 0.10.)

**Round 121: the same law for complex characters** ([round121.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round121.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r121z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r121z.py) → [lzeros/r121_part*.npz](writeup/data/riemann/lzeros/), [r121.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r121.py) → r121.json). For complex characters χ² is not principal, so the off-diagonal terms differ from the real case, while Berry's diagonal term (weight |χ(p)|²) does not. 88 primitive characters of order above 2 (the smallest Conrey label for each conductor) with conductors N = prime (every second prime in 101–397), 3p, 4p, 5p, 7p in [100, 400]; zeros with 100 ≤ |γ| ≤ 500 on both sides; Σ²(1) on a dense grid; fitted against x = the sum over the primes p | N of Berry's per-prime drop at the L-function's own L: Σ²(1) = a + βx gives β = 1.003 ± 0.038 (t = 26). Both readings pass (β > 0 at more than 4 standard errors; 0.7 ≤ β ≤ 1.4). Group means: prime N 0.3222 (n = 27), 2 | N 0.3088, 3 | N 0.3093, 5 | N 0.3066, 7 | N 0.3052. So at ℓ = 1 the per-prime law holds for real and complex characters alike, with slope 1.00; it is not diagonal in every detail (round 123, thirty-eighth review: at other window lengths, and at ℓ = 0.5, it fails). Exploratory, not registered: fitted per prime, the complex characters give fitted/predicted 1.04, 0.87, 0.97, 1.01 for 2, 3, 5, 7, the real ones of round 120 give 1.12, 1.06, 1.08, 0.99; a small extra rigidity for real characters (whose χ² is principal, so their off-diagonal term has a pole) would fit, but each difference is only 1–2σ.

**Round 122: the same law higher up** ([round122.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round122.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r122z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r122z.py) → [lzeros/r122/](writeup/data/riemann/lzeros/r122/), [r122.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r122.py) → r122.json). The 61 real characters of round 115's sample at heights 900 ≤ γ ≤ 1500 (about 1,020 zeros each, mean L = 10.69, where Berry's formula predicts smaller drops: -0.0079 for 2 | D against -0.0100 at 100–600). Σ²(1) = a + β·(predicted drop summed over p | D): β = 0.950 ± 0.043 (t = 22). All three readings pass. With rounds 117 and 120–121 the per-prime law holds at two heights, for 2 to 13, and for real and complex characters, with slopes 0.95–1.02.

**Round 123: each prime is a wave in the zeros' rigidity** ([round123.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round123.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r123.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r123.py) → r123.json). Berry's formula for a window of ℓ mean spacings, dΣ²(ℓ; p, L) = −(2/π²) Σ_k sin²(πℓk ln p/L)/(k² p^k), oscillates in ℓ with period L/ln p. On the 181 real L-functions of round 120, Σ²(ℓ) for ℓ = 0.5, 1, …, 8 (dense grid), per-prime least squares at each ℓ against the prediction (fitted/predicted): p = 2 at ℓ = 1, 2, 3.5, 5.5, 7.5: -0.011/-0.010, -0.034/-0.032, -0.065/-0.068, -0.090/-0.099, -0.090/-0.102; p = 5: -0.013/-0.012, -0.035/-0.033, -0.033/-0.037, -0.001/-0.002, -0.019/-0.027; p = 13: -0.008/-0.009, -0.016/-0.015, -0.000/-0.001, -0.014/-0.015, -0.005/-0.002. All three readings pass: over all 6 primes × 16 lengths the slope through the origin is 0.977; for p = 2 the drop at ℓ = 6.5 is more than 5 times that at ℓ = 1; for p = 13 the drop vanishes at ℓ = 3.5 (the trough, −0.000) and returns. The effect of p = 5 likewise vanishes near ℓ = 5.5 and comes back. Each prime missing from the Euler product shows in the zeros as a wave of period L/ln p in the window length, with the amplitude 1/(k² p^k) of its powers, to first order (the formula is Berry's; this test is ours as far as we found). The thirty-eighth review ([review38/](writeup/code/study/review38/)) confirmed every number and found the limits: the slope is 0.977 ± 0.011 by a bootstrap over L-functions (so significantly below 1; the 16 lengths are strongly correlated, worth about 2.5 independent ones); on a fine grid in ℓ the residual (fitted minus predicted) oscillates with period 1, too negative at whole ℓ and too positive at half ℓ and growing with ℓ (p = 2: −0.0087 at ℓ = 7, +0.0119 at ℓ = 7.5, se 0.0018), so the diagonal formula is rejected in detail at most lengths (χ² 15–85 on 6) and fits only near ℓ ≈ 0.75–1.1; small windows fail (slope 0.56 at ℓ = 0.5 for real, 0.30 for complex characters); the trough of p = 13 at ℓ = 3.5 is about 1σ. A period-1 residual is what a correction beyond the diagonal term, the off-diagonal (Bogomolny–Keating) part, would give; it was not modelled.

**Round 124: the record quadratics sit on the lowest zeros** ([round124.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round124.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r124.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r124.py) → r124.json; the polynomials from [polys/dist300.txt](writeup/code/study/polys/dist300.txt)). The 23 polynomials of the quadratic search with runs of 36 or more consecutive prime values have 19 distinct fundamental discriminants. For each, the scaled first zero of L(s, χ_d) as a percentile among 150 random fundamental discriminants of the same sign and size (within a factor 1.5): Euler's −163 (run 40) 0.000, Ruby's 7213 (run 45, first zero 0.0725) 0.000, Speiser's 1398053 (run 43) 0.000, the 47-family's 979373 (run 43) 0.020; median over all 19 0.020, the highest 0.207, and all 19 below the random median. All three readings pass. But most of this is the small-prime pattern (thirty-ninth review, [review39/](writeup/code/study/review39/)): 17 of the 19 discriminants have χ(p) = −1 for every p ≤ 23. Matched on χ(p) for p ≤ 7 the median percentile is 0.083 (17 of 18 below the median); matched on p ≤ 23 it is 0.220 (15 of 17 below; −163 and 7213 have no matches in range). Also, only about 40 fundamental discriminants lie near −163, so its 0.000 is against those, sampled with replacement; with another seed Speiser's 1398053 is at 0.007 and 979373 at 0.053. So the records sit on low zeros mainly because their small primes are inert, with a remainder probably from the inert primes above 23.

**Round 125: low zeros alone do not predict prime-rich quadratics** ([round125.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round125.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r125.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r125.py) → r125.json). The forward direction of round 124: of 2,000 random positive fundamental discriminants in [10⁵, 10⁶], the 15 with the lowest scaled first zero against 15 others, each with its longest run of prime values over all forms with A ≤ 300. Median best run 6 (low zeros) against 3 (random), one-sided Mann–Whitney p = 0.025: both readings fail (registered: a difference of at least 5 and p < 0.01). Several of the lowest-zero discriminants give runs of only 1–4. The two directions are not symmetric: a long run needs the very smallest primes (2, 3, 5, 7, …) to divide no value, which raises S and lowers the zero (round 124), but a low zero sums all primes with weight ln p/p and can come with a split or ramified 2 or 3, which ends every run within a few steps (thirty-ninth review: split 2 or 3 in 4 of the 15, ramified 2 or 3 in 8, runs 1–9; the only two inert at 2, 3, 5, 7, 11 give the two longest runs, 19 and 17). Rounds 125 and 126 search only forms of discriminant exactly d; records such as Ruby's (D = 36·7213) use a non-fundamental D whose conductor absorbs a split prime. A low zero is close to necessary for a record, not sufficient; a search should filter on the small primes first and use the zero (or S) to rank what passes.

**Round 126: filter on the small primes, then rank by the zero** ([round126.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round126.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r126.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r126.py) → r126.json). Round 125's lesson as a test: 600 random positive fundamental discriminants in [10⁵, 10⁶] with 2, 3, 5, 7, 11 all inert (d ≡ 5 mod 8, (d/p) = −1 for p = 3, 5, 7, 11); the 15 with the lowest scaled first zero against the 15 with the highest. Best runs, low zeros: 10–29, median 12; high zeros: 5–12, median 8; one-sided Mann–Whitney p = 1.3e-05. All three readings pass (difference at least 4, p < 0.01, and above round 125's median of 6). The longest, 29 consecutive prime values, comes from d = 847,997 (scaled zero 0.146). So once the smallest primes are excluded, the zero ranks the discriminants by how prime-rich their quadratics are. The thirty-ninth review replicated it with seed 2026 (medians 14 against 7, p = 4.7·10⁻⁶) but found that the next small primes do as well: the first prime ≥ 13 with χ(p) ≠ −1 has Spearman 0.67 with the best run against −0.64 for the zero (0.65 against −0.41 on 60 random filtered discriminants); 14 of the 15 HIGH discriminants have 13 not inert; and comparing extremes inflates the effect (LOW against 15 from the middle: 14 against 10, p = 0.015). The zero ranks about as well as the next small primes, which it largely sums up.

**Round 127: round 119 with 40,000 zeros** ([round127.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round127.txt), pre-registered; [r127.py](writeup/code/study/r127.py) → [r127.json](writeup/data/study/r127.json); zeros 10,001–40,000 from python-flint, [zeros_40000_flint.txt](writeup/data/riemann/zeros_40000_flint.txt), up to height 33,190). Unchanged except for the zeros: [1·10⁹, H = 10^5]: measured 0.3859, zeros 0.3861 (+0.0 %, tail share 0.43); [1·10⁹, H = 10^6]: measured 0.2662, zeros 0.2650 (-0.4 %, tail share 0.05); [3·10⁹, H = 10^5]: measured 0.4014, zeros 0.4046 (+0.8 %, tail share 0.77); [3·10⁹, H = 10^6]: measured 0.2898, zeros 0.2910 (+0.4 %, tail share 0.10); [5·10⁹, H = 10^5]: measured 0.4188, zeros 0.4222 (+0.8 %, tail share 0.85); [5·10⁹, H = 10^6]: measured 0.3106, zeros 0.3088 (-0.6 %, tail share 0.14). Readings (1) and (3) pass: at H = 10⁶ all three windows within 1 % (round 119: two of three), and the largest miss over the six cases falls from 2.3 % to 0.8 %. Reading (2) fails: at H = 10⁵ the random-phase tail still carries 77 % and 85 % of the value in the windows at 3·10⁹ and 5·10⁹ (43 % at 10⁹, down from 84 %), where x/H is larger and more zeros are needed. With four times the zeros the explicit formula comes closer to the sampled values, as it should. (The statistic, as in rounds 10 and 34, is the variance of the counts about H/ln(mid); the raw within-block variance would be larger, about 0.301 at 10⁹, H = 10⁶, because the trend of H/ln x adds about 0.035; fortieth review.)

**Round 128: ζ is an L-function with no prime missing** ([round128.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round128.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r128z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r128z.py) → [lzeros/r128/](writeup/data/riemann/lzeros/r128/), [r128.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r128.py) → r128.json). If the per-prime law is universal, a real character of prime conductor q has, at the same density, a number variance below ζ's by Berry's dS(q, L) only, whatever ζ's pole does. ζ's zeros with 7.6 ≤ ln(t/2π) ≤ 8.6 (26,812 of the first 40,000): Σ²(1) = 0.3204 ± 0.0006; ten real characters with q = 29 … 67 at the same density (420–969 zeros each): mean difference -0.0041 ± 0.0017 against the predicted -0.0045. Reading (1) passes. (After the fortieth review the zero files of D = −47 and −59 were completed with one close pair each that PARI's first run had missed, at 482.53 and 344.36; the result above is after the fix and moved by less than 0.0002. The error bar is conservative: the scatter of the ten differences gives ±0.0009–0.0010. The registration's reading (2) is garbled and repeats (1) in substance, and its estimate of the predicted size, −0.002 to −0.004, was low; the computed predictions are −0.003 to −0.0065; reading (1) is the operative one.) ζ's spacing variance here is 0.1602 (the characters' mean is 0.1583), consistent with round 110's χ₋₁₆₃ at 0.162 at higher density. So at this precision ζ fits the same law as the Dirichlet L-functions, as the L-function with nothing missing from its Euler product.

**Round 129: ζ's rigidity is the sum of the primes' waves** ([round129.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round129.txt), pre-registered; [r129.py](writeup/code/study/r129.py) → [r129.json](writeup/data/study/r129.json); the formula is Berry's, 1988, computed here for the first time in this repo). Σ²(ℓ) of ζ's zeros with 7.6 ≤ ln(t/2π) ≤ 8.6 (26,812 zeros, mean L = 8.173) against Berry's sum over prime powers below e^L, (2/π²)[Σ sin²(πℓk ln p/L)/(k² p^k) + ∫₁^∞ sin²(πℓτ)/τ² dτ], and against GUE: at ℓ = 1, 2, 4.5, 6, 8 the data are 0.3204, 0.3715, 0.4027, 0.3903, 0.3633, Berry gives 0.3196, 0.3695, 0.4013, 0.3865, 0.3621, GUE 0.3442, 0.4157, 0.4985, 0.5275, 0.5567. Both readings pass: Berry is nearer than GUE at every ℓ ≥ 2, and within 0.0037 of the data for all ℓ from 1 to 8; at ℓ = 8 the data lie -0.193 below GUE. The number variance peaks near ℓ = 4.5 and falls again, which no random-matrix ensemble does and the waves of the small primes together do (without p = 2 Berry's curve peaks near ℓ = 2.5; the p = 2 term alone near 5.9). Limits (fortieth review, [review40/](writeup/code/study/review40/)): the data lie above Berry's leading-order formula at nearly every ℓ, by about 0.002 (0.5 %), in both halves of the height range, about 3σ at ℓ = 2 and 6 (block errors 0.0003–0.0022), so the residual is systematic, probably lower-order terms; the five ℓ values share the same zeros; and 25–30 % of Berry's value comes from the saturation term ∫₁^∞, which assumes a form factor of 1 beyond the Heisenberg time rather than computing it from primes. Evaluating at the mean L or averaging over the range differs by at most 0.0006, and moving the cutoff from τ = 0.9 to 1.1 by at most 0.001.

**Data repair after the fortieth review** ([lowzero/fixpairs.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/fixpairs.py)). PARI's lfunzeros at its default subdivision misses some close pairs of zeros (spacing about 0.015–0.03). A scan of all 258 saved zero sets for a drift of the zero count against N(t) found 22 sets missing one pair (one set two); searching the largest gaps again with 256 subdivisions completed all of them (no drift left). The complex-character sets of round 121 had the same defect on 15 of 176 sides, also completed; round 121's β becomes 1.005 ± 0.039 (was 1.003 ± 0.038). Rounds 117, 118, 120 and 128 change by at most 0.0001 (round 128 was repaired first and is quoted after the repair). Round 122's β becomes 0.944 ± 0.042 (was 0.950 ± 0.043) and round 123's slope over 96 points 0.991 (was 0.977; the review's bootstrap interval [0.957, 0.998] belonged to the incomplete data); for p = 13 the drops at ℓ = 2, 3.5, 7.5 become −0.0153, +0.0008, −0.0021; all registered readings still pass. Spacing statistics of rounds 110 and 115–116 were computed on the fly with the same routine; redone on the completed sets, round 116's replication gives 0.1390 against 0.1498 (2 | D, p = 0.0027), 0.1339 against 0.1501 (3 | D), 0.1331 against 0.1489 (5 | D) and a slope of −0.0145 per missing prime (t = −12.9), unchanged in substance ([r116_complete.json](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r116_complete.json)).

**Round 130: degree 4** ([round130.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round130.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r130.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r130.py) → r130.json; field list [f5list.txt](writeup/code/study/lowzero/f5list.txt) from PARI nflist). All 131 quintic fields with Galois group F5 and |disc| ≤ 10⁷, whose ζ_K/ζ is an irreducible Artin L-function of degree 4 (S5 is not in nflist and A5 needs data files not installed here). The first zero predicted from S = 2 L′/L(1) + ln(N/π⁴) + Σ_j ψ((1 + μ_j)/2) has rank correlation 0.964 with the true one (medians over |ln N′ − ln N| < 0.5, since the fields are sparse); ln L(1) against the scaled zero gives 0.405. Both readings pass, the second only just (threshold 0.4). Baselines (forty-first review): S alone, with no window, already has Spearman −0.919 with γ₁, and the window medians alone give 0.56–0.60; the first pair of zeros makes up a median 33 % of S itself, so part of the agreement is built in; L′/L(1) here is PARI's evaluation through the functional equation, not a truncated prime sum. So the identity's right side fixes the lowest zero in degrees 1, 2, 3 and 4 alike (0.99, 0.98, 0.98, 0.96), with the caveat that S contains the zero it predicts. Second check (own short script): with median windows ±0.3 and ±1.0 the rank correlation is 0.963 and 0.957; and for the field x⁵ − x⁴ + 2x³ − 4x² + x − 1 (N = 35,152) the zeros below 200 plus the smooth tail give Σ 1/(ρ(1 − ρ)) = 2.0679 against S = 2.0678, so the identity holds in degree 4 too.

**Round 131: every race between two classes, from the zeros of all characters** ([round131.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round131.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r131.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r131.py) → r131.json). For a prime q the race of class b against class 1 is, from the explicit formula, E_b(x) = (1/φ(q)) Σ_(χ≠χ₀) (χ̄(b) − 1)(−Σ_γ x^(iγ)/ρ_χ − ε(χ)), with ε = 1 for the real character (its prime squares) and 0 for complex ones. From the zeros with |γ| ≤ 50 of every non-principal character mod q = 5, 7, …, 31 (PARI), no prime counted, the predicted share of primes p ≤ 10⁶ at which class b leads class 1, against a sieve: 137 races, Pearson 0.992, mean absolute error 0.027. Both readings pass. Mod 19, for example, class 1 trails 10 of the 17 other classes at 95.5–100 % of the primes (the other seven at 0.65–0.93), and the zeros say so race by race. The forty-first review confirmed the formula, sign and conjugation, and found the figures not inflated by races near 0 or 1 (106 races with measured share in [0.1, 0.9]: Pearson 0.987, mean error 0.030; a baseline from the residue/non-residue indicator alone gives 0.29); the script takes the class index from znprimroot, which equals PARI's generator of znstar for every q ≤ 31. So the phase and size of every race between residue classes up to 10⁶ is set by the low zeros of the characters mod q together with the prime squares of the real one. Second check (own short script with Conrey labels and PARI chareval, primes from sympy, x ≤ 2·10⁵, zeros |γ| ≤ 30, q = 7, 11, 13, 19, 23): 63 races, Pearson 0.979, mean absolute error 0.036. Ablation, after the fact ([r131_ablate.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r131_ablate.py) → r131_noeps.json): without the prime-squares term (ε = 0) the same zeros give Pearson 0.903 and a mean error of 0.099, four times worse; the local term is needed alongside the spectral one.

**Round 132: the zero adds to the next small prime, but less** ([round132.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round132.txt), pre-registered; [lowzero/r132.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r132.py) → r132_part0.json, r132_part1.json, r132.json). All 600 filtered discriminants of round 126 (2, 3, 5, 7, 11 inert), the best run of prime values over all forms with A ≤ 300, regressed on the standardised ln p₁₃ (the first prime ≥ 13 that is not inert) and S = 2 L′/L(1) + ln(d/π) + ψ(½): ln p₁₃ +2.61 ± 0.11 (t = 24), S +0.87 ± 0.11 (t = 8); R² 0.586 with p₁₃ alone, 0.267 with S alone, 0.626 with both. Both readings pass, but the reading of them was wrong (forty-first review, [review41/](writeup/code/study/review41/)): what S adds is local data, not the zero. With the second and third non-inert primes ≥ 13 as covariates S falls to t = 1.8; with the Euler sum Σ_(13≤p≤100) χ(p) ln p/(p − 1) its sign reverses (t = −5.5); with that sum and ln d (S contains ln(d/π)), S adds nothing (t = 0.6, ΔR² = 0.000; R² 0.774). So the best run is set by the local factors, the first non-inert prime and the next Euler factors up to about 100, plus the size of d; S is itself a sum of those local factors, and the zero adds nothing beyond them. Second check: the best runs of 60 random discriminants of the 600, recomputed with sympy's isprime, agree exactly.

**Round 133: why the class of 1 is last mod 8 and mod 12** ([round133.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round133.txt), pre-registered; [race/r133.py](writeup/code/riemann/race/r133.py) → r133.json). FACTS 1605: π(x; 8, 1) < π(x; 8, a) for a = 3, 5, 7 at every x in [23, 2·10⁸], and the same mod 12 from 463 on. Mod 8 and mod 12 all three non-principal characters are real (from D = −4, 8, −8 and −4, −3, 12), each with the prime-squares term 1, so π(x; q, a) − π(x; q, 1) ≈ (√x/ln x)(2/φ(q)) Σ_(χ(a) = −1) (1 + 2 Σ_(γ>0) Re(x^(iγ)/ρ)). From the zeros with γ ≤ 50 of the three L-functions, no prime counted, class 1 is predicted last at every prime in [23, 10⁷], mod 8 and mod 12: reading (1) passes. Reading (2) fails as registered, because the registration misread the fact: mod 12 the sieve (counts from p = 3; a first run counted from 23, a bug, fixed) gives ties, not a strict lead, at 37–43 (two primes each in classes 1 and 11) and 433–461 (classes 1 and 7), and strict last place from 463 on, as FACTS 1605 says; mod 8 the share is 1. So the squares (all ≡ 1 mod 8 and mod 12) push class 1 back: each race b against 1 has the same mean lead as the mod-4 race, √x/ln x, but its oscillation comes from two characters at once, and their low zeros never line up to overturn it below 10⁷. At the tie points (exactly the primes 37, 41, 43, 433, 457, 461 mod 12) the zeros predict a strict last place where the counts tie (forty-first review). Second check (own short script, zeros γ ≤ 30, 200,000 log-spaced x in [23, 10⁷]): class 1 last at every point, smallest margin 0.056 (mod 8) and 0.089 (mod 12) in the normalised units, so the prediction does not hang on the zero cut-off.

**Round 134: the period-1 residual is the off-diagonal term of Bogomolny and Keating** ([round134.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round134.txt), pre-registered, with the predictions for every L-function frozen in [lowzero/bk/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/bk/) and pushed before any zero of test B was computed; [lowzero/r134z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r134z.py) → lzeros/r134_B.npz, [lowzero/r134.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r134.py) → r134_A.json, r134_B.json; calibrated reading [lowzero/r134b.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r134b.py) → r134b_A.json, r134b_B.json). Round 123 left a residual of period 1 in ℓ on the diagonal law. The two-point function of Bogomolny and Keating for a primitive Dirichlet L-function of conductor k (J. Phys. A 46, 2013, 095202; *heuristic*, from Hardy–Littlewood in progressions; the ratios conjecture gives the same function) has two terms. Its diagonal term is ζ's with the primes dividing k removed, which is the per-prime law of rounds 117–123 (so that law is theirs, and Bogomolny–Leboeuf's of 1994, not only Berry's). Its off-diagonal term is (1/4π²)·e^(iεL)·|ζ(1 + iε)|²·Φ_off(ε)·∏_(p | k) F_p(ε) + c.c., with L = ln(kE/2π) and F_p = (p − p^(−iε))/(p − 2 + p^(iε)). In unfolded units e^(iεL) = e^(2πir): period 1. Integrated to the number variance ([bk/derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/bk/derivation.md); *derived* here from their formula), a prime p | k changes Σ²(ℓ) by the diagonal term plus ΔΣ²_off(ℓ) = (4/L²)∫₀^ℓ (ℓ − r)·Re[e^(2πir)·|ζ(1 + iε)|²·Φ_off(ε)·∏_(q ∈ S) F_q(ε)·(F_p(ε) − 1)] dr, ε = 2πr/L, S the other primes dividing k. The two changes cancel at r = 0, so for small ℓ the total is −(2 ln²p/((p − 1)L²))·(ℓ² − sin²(πℓ)/π²): at ℓ = 0.5 that is 1 − 4/π² ≈ 0.6 of the diagonal term, at ℓ = 1 it equals it, which is why rounds 117–122 fitted at ℓ = 1 and round 123 failed at 0.5. The off-diagonal term is not additive over the bad primes. Looking back (the fits were known, so this is no test): on round 123's 96 coefficients the full law gives χ² 15.7 against 684.1 for the diagonal law, both regression-matched and with OLS errors ([bk/predict_out.txt](writeup/code/study/lowzero/bk/predict_out.txt)), and ζ's Σ²(ℓ) of round 129 is within 0.0008 of it at all 16 lengths (Berry's formula: 0.0037), which accounts for round 129's +0.002.

*Test B (blind):* all 184 fundamental discriminants with 400 < |D| ≤ 700, zeros with 100 ≤ γ ≤ 600 (173,126 zeros up to 600 in all; the completeness repair added 26 zeros to 11 of the sets, [lowzero/r134count.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r134count.py) → r134_repair.json, and no set is left with a count drift above 0.02), the regression of round 123 on the data and on the frozen predictions. Every registered reading passed. (1) The slope γ of (fit − diagonal law) on the predicted off-diagonal part over the 96 cells is 0.932, bootstrap error 0.026 (the full law predicts 1, the diagonal law 0). (2) χ² with OLS errors: 27.7 for the full law, 834.9 for the diagonal law. (3) At ℓ = 0.5 the fitted drops are 0.636 of the diagonal law's (full law 0.560). (4) For p = 2 at ℓ = 6, 6.5, 7, 7.5, 8 the residuals on the diagonal law are −0.0050, +0.0071, −0.0072, +0.0083, −0.0074, against the predicted −0.0051, +0.0074, −0.0075, +0.0091, −0.0081 (errors 0.0007–0.0008). (5) With no fitted constant the mean of Σ² minus the full law is within 0.0005 at every length. (6) The pooled slope per L-function is 0.975 ± 0.014. *Test A (not blind: the thirty-eighth review had computed Σ²(ℓ) of these sets before the data repair):* the 88 complex characters of round 121 pass the same readings: γ = 0.943 ± 0.104, χ² 14.4 against 121.3 on 64 cells, ratio at ℓ = 0.5 0.472 (predicted 0.550), level within 0.0008, pooled slope 0.965 ± 0.043. So real and complex characters follow one law, as the paper says; the guess of round 121 that real characters are more rigid is withdrawn.

*After the forty-second review* (own implementation from the paper, [review42/](writeup/code/study/review42/); it matches the frozen predictions to 7·10⁻⁸ on 24 L-functions of B and 10 of A and reproduces the readings). Three corrections. (a) **The amplitude is short.** The registered window 0.7–1.3 for γ was set for an error of 0.12; the real error is 0.024. With the full law taken as true and the residual rows resampled, γ comes out at 1.001 ± 0.024 and only 1 of 1,000 sets falls at or below 0.932 (two-sided p = 0.003, z = −2.9; r134b.py). So the off-diagonal term is confirmed in sign, period and shape, and its amplitude is 0.93 ± 0.02 of the predicted one. The shortfall is the same for p = 2 (0.939 ± 0.026) and p = 3 (0.961 ± 0.044), below and above ℓ = 4 (0.902 ± 0.052, 0.941 ± 0.026), in both halves of the height band, and for 4 ∥ D and 8 | D; the bias of the variance estimate (2·10⁻⁶), the unfolding, the height average, the truncation of the prime sums and missing zeros are excluded ([review42/causes_out.txt](writeup/code/study/review42/causes_out.txt)). Evaluating the off-diagonal term alone at L + 0.3 removes it (γ = 1.008). The cause was *open* here and is settled in round 136 (two fresh tests give 0.998 ± 0.014: this sample was low); the paper claims no more than a relative precision of order E^(−1/2). By the review's simulation the registered readings (1)–(4) pass in 74 % of the cases at a true amplitude of 0.8 or of 1.2 ([review42/power_out.txt](writeup/code/study/review42/power_out.txt)), so "every reading passed" fixes the amplitude only to about ±20 %. (b) **The registered χ² flatters.** Its errors come from regressing Σ² itself on the indicators, which counts scatter that the law predicts, and its cells are correlated (about 6.0 independent lengths of 16 in B, 3.7 in A): under the full law it has mean 41.4 and 99 % point 70.6, so the threshold 96 could not fail. With the covariance of the residuals across lengths (GLS) the full law has χ² 81.7 (null mean 106.1, 95 % point 135.0) and the diagonal law 715.8 in B; 81.5 (null mean 80.2) and 828.6 in A. The diagonal law stays rejected, by a factor 9, not 30. (c) **Reading (5) could not tell the laws apart:** the diagonal law is within 0.0031 too. A χ² of the mean level over the 16 lengths does: 20.9 (full) against 153.2 (diagonal) in B, 15.3 against 141.3 in A. Comparators ([review42/comparators_out.txt](writeup/code/study/review42/comparators_out.txt), GLS χ² in B, full law 85.9 by the review's code): off-diagonal sign flipped 1,577; one bad prime at a time, without the product over the others, 210.9 (test A cannot tell: 68.7 against 73.5); the diagonal law with a free sinusoid and a linear term per prime, 24 parameters, 494.1. So the product over the bad primes is supported, and no generic oscillation with up to 24 parameters matches the law that has none. *Status:* the formula is Bogomolny–Keating's. The number-variance integral per bad prime and its small-ℓ form are elementary consequences, derived here. A numerical test of the bad-prime factors on zeros was not found in print (the paper has no numerics; the twelve works that cite it were screened by title, Smith's thesis and Conrey–Snaith read; Rubinstein's thesis not opened).

**Round 135: why so many χ² values are low: rigid class counts** ([round135.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round135.txt), pre-registered, pushed 47 s before the results, so the commits show the order only; derivation and predictions in [noise/](writeup/code/study/noise/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/noise/derivation.md), law.py → law_output.txt); [r135.py](writeup/code/study/r135.py) → [r135.json](writeup/data/study/r135.json)). LOGIC.md lists a family of χ² values below their degrees of freedom. *Class counts.* From the Hardy–Littlewood pairs, the counts N_a of primes in (x, x + H] in the reduced classes mod q have, with λ = ln x, S_q = Σ_(p | q) ln p/(p − 1) and B = 1 − γ − ln 2π,

    Var(N_a)/mean = v_q = 1 − [ln(H/q) + S_q − B]/λ,    Cov(N_a, N_b)/mean = −Λ(m)/(φ(m)·λ),  m = q/gcd(b − a, q),

so the χ² for equal classes has E[χ²]/dof = v_q + (ln q − S_q)/((φ(q) − 1)·λ) (*conjecture*, Hardy–Littlewood; within that model the dropped terms are of order H^(−1/2) and H/x, not 1/λ). The logarithm sits only on pairs in the same class: each class is as rigid as the total count, and the classes do not share that rigidity out. The restricted sums behind this are in substance Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan (2016, Prop. 2.1), Montgomery–Soundararajan (2004) and Goldston–Yıldırım (1998, eq. 1.13). *Tables of events.* For the table of classes of (p_n, p_(n+k)) per block, the column sums of the residual table are exactly class contrasts, so the block χ² splits exactly into a column part (C − 1 directions, which inherit the rigidity) and a within part ((R − 1)(C − 1) directions, which should keep the value of a short-range chain); the law takes the short-range base from a first-order chain on the classes mod 30030 and replaces its class-count covariance by the one above (*heuristic*; not found in print; the base is a sieve model, so only the class-count part is free of one). *Test:* three windows of 10⁸ at 3·10¹¹, 6·10¹¹, 9·10¹¹ (11,105,436 primes), 300 blocks of 10⁶, none used before; every registered reading passed.

| statistic | measured | law | z | multinomial | sieve chain |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| classes mod 3, χ²/dof | 0.493 ± 0.040 | 0.477 | +0.4 | 1 | 0.807 |
| classes mod 4 | 0.439 ± 0.034 | 0.488 | −1.5 | 1 | 0.807 |
| classes mod 10 | 0.496 ± 0.023 | 0.496 | +0.0 | 1 | 0.807 |
| classes mod 210 | 0.575 ± 0.007 | 0.564 | +1.6 | 1 | 0.807 |
| table (3, k = 1): all / column / within | 0.766 / 0.587 / 0.945 | 0.763 / 0.564 / 0.962 | +0.1 / +0.5 / −0.2 | 1 | 0.971 |
| table (3, 2) | 0.635 / 0.508 / 0.762 | 0.739 / 0.486 / 0.993 | −2.8 / +0.5 / −3.9 | 1 | 0.917 |
| table (10, 1) | 0.851 / 0.579 / 0.942 | 0.872 / 0.578 / 0.970 | −1.0 / +0.0 / −1.1 | 1 | 0.980 |
| table (10, 2) | 0.871 / 0.513 / 0.990 | 0.881 / 0.509 / 1.005 | −0.5 / +0.2 / −0.5 | 1 | 0.968 |

The class counts scatter at 0.44–0.57 of multinomial, as the formula says, and mod 210 the sieve chain's 0.807 is about 35 errors away. In all four tables the column part is within 0.5 errors of the law (0.51–0.59). One cell misses: the within part of the mod 3, k = 2 table is 0.762 ± 0.060 against 0.993. It had no registered reading (the falsifier was set on q = 10), so the round passes as registered; the review below shows that the miss is chance. Looking back with the same law ([noise/law_output.txt](writeup/code/study/noise/law_output.txt); the values were known): the last-digit tables of rounds 85–86, χ² 25.4 and 24.5 on 36 at 10⁹ and 10¹², are predicted at 25.9 ± 7.1 and 27.7 ± 7.6 (20.0 at 10¹⁵ against 31.1 ± 9.4); round 13's table at the three test windows is within 1.4 errors; round 13's training window (0.691 against 0.856) and round 83's bands at k = 4 (8.2 against 23.5, where the nominal 19 was itself too low a reference) stay unexplained. The family was also assembled from low values: rounds 15, 71, 82 and 84 hold statistics of the same kind that are not low.

*After the forty-third review* (own sieve and code on 19 windows, [review43/](writeup/code/study/review43/); it reproduces r135.json to four decimals; the main points recomputed here on the three registered and six fresh windows, 2·10¹¹ to 10¹², at block lengths 10⁴, 10⁵, 10⁶: [r135b.py](writeup/code/study/r135b.py), [r135c.py](writeup/code/study/r135c.py) → [r135c.json](writeup/data/study/r135c.json)). (a) **The class-count law holds beyond the registered case.** Over nine windows, χ²/dof for equal classes mod 3, 4, 5, 7, 210 is 0.648, 0.658, 0.664, 0.679, 0.735 at H = 10⁴ (law 0.647, 0.658, 0.666, 0.679, 0.734) and 0.555, 0.578, 0.579, 0.597, 0.651 at H = 10⁵ (law 0.562, 0.573, 0.581, 0.593, 0.649), every |z| ≤ 1.3; at H = 10⁶ mod 210 is 0.573 ± 0.004 against 0.564 (+2.4 errors; +1.9 on the review's 15 windows) and mod 3 is 0.525 ± 0.024 against 0.477 (+2.0), so at the largest block length the law may be slightly low. The slope in ln H, its sharpest prediction, is −0.083 per decade against −0.085 for q = 210 (review); the law also fits q = 8, 9, 12, 30 and single windows at 10⁹, 10¹⁰ and 10¹⁴ (review; three cells beyond 2.5 errors at 10¹³). (b) **The miss is chance.** For q = 3 the within part is exactly the dispersion of the number of pairs p_n ≡ p_(n+k) mod 3 per block against the binomial. In the same windows it is 0.996 ± 0.008 at H = 10⁴ and 1.002 ± 0.026 at H = 10⁵; on the six fresh windows at H = 10⁶ it is 1.052 ± 0.063 (review, twelve fresh windows: 0.947 ± 0.040). With the law's own error, not the shrunken empirical one, the registered value was 2.9 errors low, the lowest of about a dozen cells. (c) **The column part is the class-count law again, and needs no sieve model.** The column sums of the residual table are c = (I − Pᵀ)N up to edge terms, so the column part is fixed by the block's class counts: E[col]/(C − 1) = tr[M̃⁺(I − Pᵀ)K(I − P)]/(C − 1), with P the transition matrix, M̃ = Σ_a (diag P_a − P_a P_aᵀ) and K the covariance above (for q = 3: α/(1 − α)·[v₃ + ln 3/(2λ)], α the chance to change class). With the primes' own P this gives 0.565, 0.490, 0.576, 0.510 for the four registered tables (the sieve-based law: 0.564, 0.486, 0.578, 0.509), and it fits five tables at three block lengths on nine windows with |z| ≤ 1.1 at 10⁴ and 10⁵, and (3, 1), (3, 2) at +2.2 at 10⁶ (the same excess as the class count mod 3 there). So "the column part agrees" was reading (1) counted again, in the form of Rao and Scott's design-effect correction of a χ² (1981, 1984; not read here); what is not in print as far as found is its use on tables of primes. (d) **The registered readings could not test the sieve base.** A law with no short-range part at all (within part 1, column part from the formula) passes every registered reading too. What tests the within part is the block length: for consecutive primes (k = 1) it is below 1 already at H = 10⁴, on nine windows 0.955 ± 0.005 (mod 3), 0.984 ± 0.002 (mod 5), 0.986 ± 0.001 (mod 7), and falls to 0.949 ± 0.015 and 0.974 ± 0.009 at H = 10⁶ for mod 5 and mod 7 (mod 3: 0.961 ± 0.044); for k = 2 it stays at 0.99–1.02. So part of the shortfall of the k = 1 tables lies outside the class counts mod q; it is the rigidity of the counts of gaps in a class (rounds 40–41), *proposed*, and its size is *open*. (e) **A wrong comparator:** a first-order chain on the classes mod q gives 1 only for k = 1; for k = 2 it gives 0.91 (mod 3) and 0.99 (mod 10) in total, since two steps of a chain are not multinomial rows.

**Round 137: consecutive primes are more regular than a chain, by Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's repulsion** ([round137.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round137.txt), pre-registered; derivation and frozen predictions in [within/](writeup/code/study/within/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/within/derivation.md), law.py: exact sums, no simulation); [r135b.py](writeup/code/study/r135b.py) on 24 windows → r135b_new1..4.json, [r137.py](writeup/code/study/r137.py) → [r137.json](writeup/data/study/r137.json)). Round 135's review left the within part of the tables for consecutive primes below 1. *The law.* The within part is 1 + (2/(C − 1)²)·Σ_j (1 − j/N)·τ_j, with τ_j the covariance at lag j of the table's increments projected on the within directions (*proved*, up to terms of order 1/N); any first-order chain has every τ_j = 0. At leading order the only term that is not a chain is the repulsion between p_n and p_(n+2) in Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's conjecture (2016), which gives

    k = 1:  E[within]/dof = 1 − 1/((φ(q) − 1)·λ),        k = 2:  1 − 1/(3(φ(q) − 1)·λ) + 2B_q²/(φ(q) − 1)²,   B_q = φ(q)·P(p_(n+1) ≡ p_n) − 1

(*conjecture*; for q = 3: the number of gaps divisible by 6 in a block, given the class counts, has variance N·P(1 − P)(1 − 1/λ); for k = 2 the two terms nearly cancel, which is why next-but-one primes look like multinomial rows). This is known in substance: the content is their non-adjacent term, read here as a block variance; the within-part formula itself was not found in print. The registered predictions use a finite-λ version (the triple law of three consecutive primes summed over the classes mod 30030 for lag 1, a chain on those classes for longer lags, and a long-range tail from the pair correlations of the primes above 13), because the leading order alone is too coarse. *Test:* 24 windows of 10⁸ from 6.0·10⁹ to 8.4·10⁹ (λ = 22.7 against 27.0 before), blocks of 10⁴: mod 3, 5, 7 for k = 1: 0.9490 ± 0.0027, 0.9760 ± 0.0009, 0.9807 ± 0.0006 against 0.9486, 0.9783, 0.9809; k = 2: mod 3 1.0086 ± 0.0029 (1.0059), mod 10 1.0066 ± 0.0011 (1.0084). Every registered reading passed, including the contrast with the old height, T = 1.00 ± 0.09 (law 0.85; no dependence on height 0).

*After the forty-fourth review* (own sieve and statistic on all 24 windows, [review44/](writeup/code/study/review44/): every cell of r137.json reproduced to 10⁻¹⁵; the leading-order algebra re-derived from the paper and confirmed, constants included). What the pass is worth: (a) **The registered bands were wide**, because the law carried its own error (±40 % on the tail) into them. With the measurement error alone the law is at z = +0.1, −2.4, −0.4 for mod 3, 5, 7, the leading order at −2.5, −10.0, −18.7 and the law without its tail at −1.5, −10.6, −15.6. So the data confirm the finite-λ law in level, reject the closed form alone at this precision and need the tail; mod 5 is 2.4 errors low. (b) **The height contrast is weaker than registered:** its error left out the noise of the old-height values; with it T = 1.00 ± 0.23, so no height dependence is rejected at 4.3 errors, while a plain 1/λ scaling of the old deficits (T = 0.56) cannot be told from the law. (c) **The tail is too small.** The fall of the within part per unit ln H is −0.0089 ± 0.0010 for mod 5 against the law's −0.0042 (2.1 times; also 2.6 times at the old height) and −0.0072 ± 0.0006 against −0.0055 for mod 7; the registered slope reading passed only because its band admitted tails from 0.1 to 2.1 times the law's. The review rebuilt the tail from responses measured on the primes and found the law's computation right for its premises; the truncation at 13 adds 10–20 %; leakage of the class-count rigidity is 3 %. The excess sat in the mixed directions mod 5 (the order-4 character against the quadratic one), −0.0083 ± 0.0021 per unit ln H per direction over both heights; round 139 tested that on fresh windows and it is not there (−0.0003 ± 0.0030). (d) **The k = 2 readings rest on a choice made after seeing the validation stretch** (their lag-2 term instead of the chain's): with the chain's term the mod 3 cell would have failed (about 1.020 against 1.0086), and for mod 10 the closed form (1.0060) fits better than the law (1.0084). (e) A first-order chain with the primes' own matrix gives 0.9998 ± 0.0009 (mod 5) and 0.9990 ± 0.0005 (mod 7) in blocks of this size, so the terms of order 1/N are about 0.001. *Status:* the within part is below 1 by the amount the Hardy–Littlewood triples give at lag 1 (level confirmed on fresh data at a new height, to 0.002–0.003); its growth with the block length is about twice the pair-correlation tail for mod 5 and is *open*.

**Round 138: three zeros at a time: the missing orbits in the three-point function** ([round138.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round138.txt), pre-registered with thresholds from measurement errors and frozen predictions for every L-function; derivation [lowzero/triple/derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/triple/derivation.md); [lowzero/r138z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r138z.py), [lowzero/r138.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r138.py) → r138_Fn.json and four more, [lowzero/r138d.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r138d.py) → r138d.json; reviewed, see the end of this paragraph). The physics picture of rounds 117–136: the zeros of L(s, χ) are the levels of a chaotic system whose periodic orbits are the prime powers, and a prime dividing the conductor is a missing orbit. Rounds 134 and 136 confirmed its two-point function. The three-point function with all arithmetic terms is known for ζ (Bogomolny–Keating 1996; Conrey–Snaith 2007–2008 from the ratios conjecture, compared there with 100,000 zeros of ζ); for Dirichlet L-functions and for the conductor's primes no test was found in print. *The law* (*conjecture*; the ratios recipe and the Bogomolny–Keating form were coded separately and agree to 10⁻⁷): every prime p | k is deleted from every prime sum and Euler product of the connected three-point function. *The statistic:* κ₃(ℓ), the third cumulant of the number of zeros in a window of ℓ mean spacings; κ₃ = k[3] + 3Σ² − 2ℓ with k[3] the integral of the connected three-point function over the cube. A bad prime changes κ₃ in two ways (*derived* here): it removes the triple-orbit term (the orbits p^m, p^n, p^(m+n)),

    Δκ₃(ℓ; p) = (6/π³)·Σ_(m,n ≥ 1) sin(mθ)·sin(nθ)·sin((m + n)θ)/(m·n·(m + n)·p^(m+n)),    θ = πℓ·ln p/L,

up to +0.017 for p = 2 near ℓ = 4 (this is minus the third cumulant of the prime's wave with a uniform random phase: elementary), and it changes the off-diagonal part by a small remainder (−0.007 to +0.006): the large drop of the variance (3ΔΣ² down to −0.33) cancels inside the three-point function, as it must in any model where the primes contribute independent additive waves, so the cancellation itself is no test of the off-diagonal terms. For GUE κ₃ is +0.045, +0.011, …, +0.002 at ℓ = 0.5, 1, …, 8; the law puts the zeros 0.006–0.020 below that for ℓ ≤ 6 and 0.0005–0.004 below for longer windows. *Test Fn (blind):* all 186 fundamental discriminants with 1,000 < |D| ≤ 1,300, zeros at heights 100–600 (188,343 zeros to 600; one set, D = 1153, excluded by the registered completeness rule with a count deviation of 1.51 against the limit 1.5; nothing was found missing in it).

| reading (Fn, 185 L-functions) | measured | full law | alternatives |
|---|---|---|---|
| level: mean κ₃ − law at 16 lengths | within 0.00042 (errors 0.0001–0.0004); Hotelling p = 0.50 | 0 | GUE is 0.008–0.02 away |
| arithmetic amplitude of the level γ_A | 1.0005 ± 0.0064 | 1 | GUE 0 |
| triple-orbit amplitude γ_Q (p = 2, 3, 5; 48 cells) | 0.998 ± 0.009 | 1 | orbit not removed: 0 |
| off-diagonal remainder γ_off | 0.989 ± 0.020 | 1 | diagonal only: 0 |
| χ² of the per-prime coefficients on 16 lengths, p = 2 / 3 / 5 | 11.3 / 20.9 / 10.0 | 16 | GUE 4,636 / 611 / 105; orbit kept 6,365 / 1,325 / 121 |

Every registered decision passes: the full law is not rejected anywhere, and GUE, "the conductor does not remove the triple-orbit term", "bad primes act through the two-point function only" and "through the triple-orbit term only" are each rejected. The factorial cumulant k[3] is within 0.0013 of the law at every length. "Bad primes act through the two-point function only" is rejected at more than 2,000 errors, but that alternative differs from the law by the known term 3ΔΣ², so this says no more than the per-prime χ² against GUE. *Test O (registered as optional: zeros known; κ₃ had been computed on these sets for its noise only, as regression residuals, which is how the three sets that missed a pair were found; no mean or coefficient had been looked at):* the four earlier samples give the same, each passing every decision: γ_Q = 0.993 ± 0.009 (rounds 117/120), 0.995 ± 0.010 (test B of round 134), 1.008 ± 0.009 (test Dn of round 136), 1.002 ± 0.011 (test C, heights 600–1,000; two sets excluded by the completeness rule), Hotelling p = 0.18, 0.17, 0.60, 0.54. Over all 910 zero sets (732 different L-functions: test C repeats the discriminants of rounds 117/120 at other heights): γ_Q = 0.9990 ± 0.0042, γ_A = 1.0020 ± 0.0026, γ_off = 1.002 ± 0.009, each read with the other part held at the law. *Test Bp (registered secondary, blind):* test B's 184 discriminants at the new heights 600–1,000 (303,500 zeros to 1,000; one close pair of χ₋₅₃₆ at 992.79 added by the completeness repair): every decision passes again, Hotelling p = 0.68, γ_A = 0.996 ± 0.006, γ_Q = 0.999 ± 0.011, γ_off = 0.996 ± 0.026, χ² of the per-prime coefficients 15.8, 12.1, 13.2 on 16. By-product: κ₃ is a sharp completeness test of a zero list; preparing this round found the three sets that missed a close pair (see round 136).

*After the forty-fifth review* (the law coded again from the papers' own formulas, [review45/](writeup/code/study/review45/): κ₃ within 2.3·10⁻⁵ of the frozen predictions for all 186 L-functions, within 3.7·10⁻⁶ on 13 recomputed in full; the GUE values to 10⁻¹⁴ from the sine kernel; every reading of r138_Fn.json reproduced exactly; the predictions read no zero file and are byte-identical to the registered ones). No error that changes a decision; four statements were too strong and are corrected above or here. (a) **The two amplitudes are correlated** (their templates overlap by 0.57). Fitted jointly they are γ_Q = 0.991 ± 0.013 and γ_off = 0.974 ± 0.027 on Fn, and 0.9975 ± 0.0061 and 0.996 ± 0.013 over the five samples. So the missing-orbit term is confirmed to 0.6 %, not 0.4 %; per prime on Fn: p = 2 to 1.5 % (0.987 ± 0.015), p = 3 to 4 % (1.03 ± 0.04), p = 5 to 15 % (0.86 ± 0.15); for p ≥ 7 the data cannot tell the law from "orbit kept" (χ² against the law 27.1, 12.1, 18.7 on 16 for p = 7, 11, 13). (b) **What the statistic sees:** κ₃(ℓ) reads only a projection of the three-point function, its integral over one distance at fixed other; a change with zero such integral is invisible. The bad-prime factors of the off-diagonal part are tested through their net remainder of about 0.005, to about 3 %. Complex characters are untested. (c) **Comparators on Fn** (level / per-prime contrasts): orbit term with its sign flipped, 268 / 177 errors; only the first traversal m = n = 1, 37 / 21; the law at L ± 0.3, 8.3–8.8 / 6.1–6.2 (so L is fixed to about ±0.04); GUE plus the random-phase diagonal term alone, 66 / 40; a free quadratic in ℓ per prime, χ² 438, 392, 58 on 13 against the law's 11.3, 20.9, 10.0 with no parameter: the data resolve the saw-tooth shape. (d) No subset fails: by sign of D, by height and by size of |D| the largest level deviation is 2.5 errors and γ_Q runs from 0.985 to 1.011. The single level offset is (−3 ± 6)·10⁻⁵. Including the excluded set changes nothing (γ_Q = 0.9994). (e) Procedure: the registered rule asked for a finer recomputation before excluding D = 1153; it was searched with 256 subdivisions (repair138.py) and nothing was found, its deviation of 1.51 being an excursion of S(t). The estimator's edge bias, left open in the registration, is measured at (0.5 ± 0.2)·10⁻⁴. *Status:* on fresh data the third cumulant of the zeros of Dirichlet L-functions, its level, its shape in ℓ and its dependence on the primes 2, 3 and 5 dividing the conductor are those of the ratios conjecture and the semiclassical trace formula; no such test was found in print (Conrey–Snaith 2007 compared ζ's triple correlation with 100,000 zeros; Rudnick–Sarnak's figures, Rubinstein's thesis and Rumely's computations were not opened).

**Round 142: L′ at the zeros, and the primes dividing the conductor** ([round142.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round142.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts and frozen predictions [lowzero/lprime/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/lprime/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/lprime/derivation.md)); output and the per-zero values [r142/](writeup/data/study/r142/); forty-eighth review with its own code [review48/](writeup/code/study/review48/)). Measured: L′(ρ, χ_D) at the 449,710 zeros with 100 ≤ γ ≤ 600 of 547 real Dirichlet L-functions not used for this before (L′ by PARI; an independent route through the Hurwitz zeta function agrees to 10⁻¹² in log|L′| on 94 zeros). Every registered decision passed. What each reading is worth, after the review:

| reading | measured | what it is |
|---|---|---|
| second moment Σ\|L′(ρ)\|² against its asymptotic with all lower-order terms | pooled ratio 0.9998 ± 0.0004; per-prime coefficients +0.0004, +0.0012, +0.0007, +0.0011 (± 0.001) for p = 2, 3, 5, 7, expected 0 | *theorem-grade* (GRH; Gonek's method; for ζ proved by Milinovich 2008 and unconditionally by Durkan–Hughes–Pearce-Crump 2026; the reviewer's own derivation for χ_D equals the frozen prediction to 10⁻¹⁵): it checks the zeros and the L′ values. The 0.9 % scatter per L-function is the theorem's error term. |
| negative moment Σ1/\|L′(ρ)\|²: the factor p/(p + 1) per prime dividing D | amplitude 1.065 ± 0.034 as registered; 1.02–1.07 ± 0.04 over estimators (the terms have a tail of index 3/2) | *conjecture* (Pearce-Crump 2026, Conjecture 7; only lower bounds are proved; no numerics in print): a real test. "No factor" is rejected for p = 2, 3, 5, 7 (31, 20, 8.6, 7.0 errors); the factor 1 − 1/p of the second moment is rejected only for p = 2 and 3. The level is 0.976 of the leading term untruncated, known to a few percent only. |
| shift of the mean of log\|L′(ρ)\| per prime dividing D | +0.0433, +0.0389, +0.0301, +0.0236 for p = 2, 3, 5, 7; 0.892 ± 0.010 of the registered first-order size ln p·ln(p/(p − 1))/L | a *restatement* of the two-point law of rounds 134–138: by the Hadamard product the mean of log\|L′\| is an integral of ln\|r\| against the pair correlation. With the swapped term included (which the registration estimated at −10 to −18 % and did not freeze) the prediction is +0.0428, +0.0374, +0.0296, +0.0236 and the amplitude 1.013 ± 0.009. |
| variance of log\|L′(ρ)\| per prime dividing D | −0.194, −0.107, −0.054 (leading order −0.293, −0.182, −0.107) | no exact prediction: the registered heuristic's 1/L term is 26 % too large (amplitude 0.741 ± 0.010, the same on every split) and it fails for the third and fourth cumulants. *Open:* a first-order formula (it needs the three-point function). |
| a prime dividing D leaves no trace at the phase γ·ln p | cos and sin coefficients 0 ± 0.001 | explicit formula (χ(p) = 0). For good primes the data give χ(p)·L·mean(p^(−iγ)) = 1.0015, 0.9992, 0.9992, 1.0000 times −ln p/√p for p = 2, 3, 5, 7 (errors 0.001–0.003): Landau's formula, a check of the zeros. |

So one reading tests a conjecture that had no numerical support: the negative moment of L′ at the zeros carries the factor p/(p + 1) for each prime dividing the conductor, to about 4 % of its amplitude. The second moment and the phase readings are verifications; the mean shift is the pair-correlation law again (and agrees with it to 1 %); the variance drop per prime has no theory yet. The set D = 1153, excluded by the completeness rule, is complete (the review found the flag to be an excursion of S(t)); including it changes nothing.

**Round 158: twin primes across residue classes, and the end of an open point** ([round158.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round158.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts and frozen predictions [twinclass/](writeup/code/study/twinclass/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/twinclass/derivation.md)); results [r158/](writeup/data/study/r158/)). Rounds 41 to 44 left twin counts per residue class more even than the Hardy–Littlewood four-point sums predict (Fisher p ≈ 0.002 over three windows), and round 155 gave the term linear in the prime errors. *Derivation (exact inside Hardy–Littlewood):* that linear term is already contained in the four-point sums of rounds 42 and 43, as an identity (the regression coefficient of the twin class counts on the prime class counts is 1.0000 ± 0.0005 in 18 cases), so it cannot explain the deficit; on the three seen decades the deficit sits in the remainder, as an anticorrelation between the nine sub-windows of a decade 2.6 times the predicted one, for twins only (the pairs at distance 4 to 12 scatter as expected). *Test (blind; no twin class count existed above 2·10¹⁰):* the decade [2·10¹⁰, 2·10¹¹) in nine sub-windows.

| reading | measured | predicted | rule |
|---|---|---|---|
| class-level coefficient of twin counts on prime counts, mod 30030 | 0.967 ± 0.021 | 1.000 | 3 errors (0: 46 errors away) |
| remainder at the fine level (30030 within 2310) | 1.018 ± 0.013 | 1 | 3 errors (independence: 1.475) |
| linear part, mod 30030 | 1.011 ± 0.013 | 1 | 3 errors (Poisson primes: 2.51) |
| anticorrelation between sub-windows, beyond the prediction (joint z of mod 2310 and 30030) | −0.24 | 0 | ≤ −3 confirmed; ≥ −0.92 chance favoured |

The three registered readings pass, and the open point reads as chance: on the fresh decade the excess anticorrelation is absent (z = −0.61 and +0.26), and mod 210 the twin counts are there less even than predicted (ratio 1.69), not more. So the extra evenness of rounds 41 to 44 was a fluctuation of the selected case, at the 2 to 3 errors its own noise allowed; one decade has limited power (a real effect of the seen size would have given −1.85 on average). Not yet reviewed. The first three readings test a structure that the four-point sums contain, so they are a check of Hardy–Littlewood at the level of classes, not a further law.

**Round 157: the conductor and the root number of an elliptic curve read from a_p alone** ([round157.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round157.txt), pre-registered; [spectrometer_blind.py](writeup/code/study/resonance/spectro/spectrometer_blind.py), test set [testset157.py](writeup/code/study/resonance/spectro/testset157.py); results [r157/](writeup/data/study/r157/), scored by [score157.py](writeup/code/study/resonance/spectro/score157.py) → [score157_out.txt](writeup/data/study/r157/score157_out.txt)). By round 156 the sum Σ_p λ(p)·ln p·e(−2√(rp/N)) has a main term at every rational r with a known constant. Used backwards this is a detector: from the list a_p, p ≤ 3·10⁶, of a curve whose equation is not used, form S(α) = Σ_p λ(p)·ln p·Hann(√p)·e(−α√p) and, for every candidate N′ from 2 to 6000 and both signs, match S against the lines α_r = 2√(r/N′), r = a/b with b ≤ 4, weighted as the law says; the estimate is the best of the 11,998 candidates. *Test (blind):* 40 curves drawn by a seeded rule from 2,221 isogeny classes of conductor ≤ 3000 in a box of Weierstrass equations (20 with N ≤ 500, 20 above; conductors 33 to 2945; 26 with additive reduction, 14 semistable), none used before.

| reading | result | rule |
|---|---|---|
| conductor and root number both right | 40 of 40 | at least 30; falsified below 20 |
| the truth among the three best | 40 of 40 | at least 34 |
| root number at the true conductor | 40 of 40 | at least 36 |
| constant c of the law, estimate against B/L(1, Sym²) | median error 1.3 %, largest 7.5 % | median at most 5 % |

The score of the true candidate is between 21.5 and 50.8 errors (median 35.8) and beats the best rival by at least 10.6; the best rivals are mostly multiples and divisors of N (4N, 9N, N/3, N/4, N/5) and, for N above 2000, the neighbours N ± 1; the others are noise at 4 to 8 errors. Chance: 1 in 11,998 per curve. *What this is.* A use of the law, not a further law: the bias between a_p and the fractional part of 2√(rp/N), of relative size x^(−1/4), is strong enough at 3·10⁶ to identify N and ε for every curve tried, also at additive primes, where the rule for the lines had been checked on two curves only. The conductor of a curve is known from its equation, and tests that find N from the functional equation exist; a search for prior art on a detection from the prime side was not made. *Review (fifty-ninth, own code in [review59/](writeup/code/study/review59/)):* no leak (the truth is computed after the ranking and used only to score; hashes match); the statistic recomputed by direct sums on four curves agrees (z 40.88, 50.81, 21.55, 22.22 against 40.86, 50.82, 21.50, 22.18). Weakened: the chance level of 1 in 11,998 overstates the task, since the bad primes show in the data (a(l) ∈ {0, ±1}) and leave a median of 49 candidates; and the classical check of the functional equation finds N and ε for the same 40 curves from the primes below 150 alone (40 of 40; 34 of 40 below 80), with modularity only. What the round adds is a test of the line weights of the law on 40 new curves, additive primes included, and the constant c to a median of 1.3 %.

**The one-point law and murmurations (prior-art gate of 25 November 2026;** note, scripts and outputs [universal/murmur/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/murmur/), [murmur_gate.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/murmur/murmur_gate.md)**).** Murmurations are the oscillating averages of ε_f·a_f(p) over a family as a function of p/N. (1) *Known.* The mechanism of fact 2054 (the Petersson modulus c = N, the kernel J_(k−1)(4π√(p/N))) is in print: Miller–Montague (arXiv 0911.1830, Lemma 3.5), Cowan (arXiv 2408.12723, Theorem 1.4), Sarnak's letter of August 2023 (level 1), and with natural weights Zubrilina (arXiv 2310.07681). Fact 2054 is Miller–Montague's formula at the test function h·X⁻¹; its novelty statement is corrected. A statement for one form is in print only on the prime side (Iwaniec–Luo–Sarnak 2000, Appendix C; Sawin–Sutherland, arXiv 2504.12295, an identity for one elliptic curve from which the constant c_E can be read off), and the murmuration papers describe the correlation as visible only on average over a family. (2) *Derived here (exact algebra; the averaging step heuristic).* A Bessel term c·√y·J_(k−1)(4π√(ρy)) of a density is the line r = ρ of the zero statistic with amplitude c/(2π√ρ); the gamma factors and the level cancel. Averaging the one-point law of a single form, with its rational lines, over squarefree levels with natural weights (Plancherel measure at good primes, λ(p)² = 1/p at bad primes) gives Zubrilina's density, local factor by local factor (exact for the exponents −6 to 6); its leading constant γ/2π² = 0.862991 in her notation is the mean of c_f over that family. So her density is the family average of the law for one form. (3) *Prime levels.* The same average over prime levels gives another density, for weight 2: M(y) = 2π√y − 12y + (12/π)·Σ_(r ≤ 2√y) ν′(r)·√(4y − r²), with ν′ a sum of an explicit multiplicative function over the squarefree divisors of r; its line amplitudes also follow from fact 2054 with Hecke twists, without the conjecture (12 amplitudes equal to 10⁻⁶). On trace-formula data (the class-number formula for Σ_f ε_f·a_f(p) checked against PARI on 1,014 pairs (N, p), no mismatch; 103 prime levels 1500 to 2297 and 120 squarefree levels 1500 to 1699, p ≤ 12N, 48 bins): at prime levels χ² = 16 for this density and 908 for Zubrilina's; at squarefree levels 606 and 10. Whether the prime-level density is in print was not settled. (4) *Not found in print:* a predicted murmuration density for any family of degree ≥ 3 (for symmetric squares it is posed as a problem by Lowry-Duda, arXiv 2506.01640, and by Kundu and Müller, arXiv 2507.00738); the constants B_L(1)/L(1, Ad L) and Π G_p(1/p) appear in none of the sources read. The one-point law in degree 3 gives such a density; its derivation and a blind test are the next step. Not read: Hughes–Miller, Fiorilli–Miller, Ricotta–Royer, the published versions of Zubrilina and of Bober–Booker–Lee–Lowry-Duda.

**Round 156: the resonance at √(p/N) along twin primes and at shifted primes** ([round156.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round156.txt), pre-registered; scripts, frozen predictions and discovery outputs [resonance/spectro/](writeup/code/study/resonance/spectro/); results [r156/](writeup/data/study/r156/); own check [check156.py](writeup/code/study/resonance/spectro/check156.py) → [check156_out.txt](writeup/data/study/r156/check156_out.txt)). Round 153 read the one-point law on primes: Σ_p λ(p)·ln p·e(−2√(p/N)) has a main term with the constant c of fact 2055. *Statements (heuristic; each constant is a product of local factors, a density of the constellation times the resonance per class mod l):* (1) summed over twin primes (weight ln p·ln(p + 2)) the constant is 2C₂·c, the twin constant times the single-prime constant, with the label on either member, and the same for p with 2p + 1 prime; (2) for the label at a shifted prime, Σ_p λ(p + h)·ln p·e(−2√((p + h)/N)), the constant is Π_l L_l with L_l = 1 + ρ_l/(l − 1) for l ∤ h and 1 − ρ_l for l | h (l ∤ N), neither 1 (all integers) nor c (primes): for the Hecke character of Q(i) with 2cos 4θ, 0.7791 at h = 2 and 1.2466 at h = 6; (3) at every rational line r = a/b, Σ_p λ(p)·ln p·e(−2√(rp/N)) has the constant c(r), a product of local factors, zero when a cube divides b.
