Labels: the curves 11a and 15a, the Hecke character of Q(i), the cubic of discriminant −23. A scan of e(αp^β) over α for the exponents β = 1/5, 1/4, 1/3, 2/5, 1/2, 2/3, 3/4, 1 on the discovery range (primes below 3·10⁷, four labels of degree 2) found peaks at β = 1/2 only (power 77 to 609; every other exponent and every scrambled label at most 17). *Test (blind; nothing above 3·10⁷ had been computed):* primes in (3·10⁷, 10⁹]; reading = smooth sum divided by the law, expected 1 + 0i; errors fixed in advance from the discovery range.

| reading | measured | error fixed in advance |
|---|---|---|
| twin primes, label on p, four labels pooled | 0.934 | 0.042 |
| per label (11a, 15a, Q(i), cubic), label on p | 1.165, 0.861, 0.937, 1.007 | 0.173, 0.070, 0.064, 0.106 |
| label on p + 2 | 0.764, 0.999, 0.996, 1.074 | 0.172, 0.070, 0.064, 0.144 |
| p with 2p + 1 prime | 1.005, 1.010, 0.928, 1.015 | 0.176, 0.072, 0.076, 0.138 |
| shifted primes, Q(i): h = 2, h = 6 | 0.991, 1.022 | 0.026, 0.024 |
| shifted primes, cubic: h = 2, h = 6 | 0.976, 1.003 | 0.049, 0.041 |
| slope of the 60 strongest lines: Q(i), cubic | 1.0020, 0.9956 | 0.0050, 0.0062 |

χ² on the 24 twin numbers (real and imaginary parts): the law 13.7; no resonance 1,465; the constant of all integers 394; the single-prime constant without the twin factor 81. χ² on the shifted primes: the law 3.5; the constant of all integers 238; the constant of the primes 5,766. Lines: χ² 57.7 and 60.4 on 120 numbers (no lines: 40,592 and 26,166); the four lines registered as largest deviations of the discovery range are within 1.2 errors; the six lines predicted zero are below 1.3 errors. Every registered decision passes and no falsifier is met. Lowest reading: 15a on twin primes with the label on p, 0.861 ± 0.070 (2.0 errors), which pulls the pooled value to 1.6 errors below 1. Not testable here: the local factor at l = 23 of the cubic (22/21 against 20/21 for the two members; 0.4 errors each). One chunk of the sums was recomputed after a full disk cut its record short. Own check: the reading for Q(i) on twin primes recomputed with other code (own sieve: 3,271,614 twin pairs, as in the run; two squares by PARI): 0.9369 + 0.0296i against 0.9370 + 0.0295i.

*What this is.* Conjectures of Bateman–Horn type for the resonance, with numerics; the single-prime law itself is the conditional heuristic of Iwaniec, Luo and Sarnak. For twin primes the data cannot tell the derived constant from plain factorisation (they differ only for the cubic, by 5 %). The shifted-prime constants are the sharper reading, and what they decide is the rule at the prime 3 (inert in Q(i)), which turns the ratio between h = 6 and h = 2 into exactly 1.6: without the factor at 3 the two readings miss by 8.0 and 11.3 registered errors, with the rule for l | h ignored by 16.6, while the factors at l ≥ 5 show only together, at about 3 errors (fifty-ninth review, [review59/](writeup/code/study/review59/); it also recomputed the sums on 9,897,064 primes of [5·10⁸, 7·10⁸) to 10⁻¹⁴ and the constants 0.77910 and 1.24657). In print, as far as read: bounds without a main term for λ(p − 1) (Pitt 2013) and for sums over primes with e(α√p) (Zhao 2006, Jiang–Lü 2017: abstracts only); a resonance main term over all integers (Ren–Ye). A main term along twin primes, at shifted primes or at rational lines on primes was not found; unknown until Zhao and Jiang–Lü are read in full. Not registered, discovery range only: from a_p for p ≤ 3·10⁶ alone, a matched filter over the predicted lines finds the conductor and root number of ten elliptic curves (conductors 11 to 5077) among all N ≤ 6000 ([spectrometer.py](writeup/code/study/resonance/spectro/spectrometer.py)); a bounded check, not a blind test. Not yet reviewed by an independent agent.

**Round 155: the error of the twin-prime count follows the primes and their L-functions, with a selection rule** ([round155.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round155.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts, frozen predictions and the discovery outputs [twinzeros/](writeup/code/study/twinzeros/) ([twinzeros.md](writeup/code/study/twinzeros/twinzeros.md)); results [r155/](writeup/data/study/r155/); own check of the selection rule [check155.py](writeup/code/study/twinzeros/check155.py) → [check155_out.txt](writeup/data/study/r155/check155_out.txt)). The Hardy–Littlewood conjecture gives the number of prime pairs; what the error is made of was open (LOGIC.md: twins have no L-function, so no explicit formula supplies the term). *Statement (heuristic; derived two ways, from local densities and from the major arcs):* the error of a pattern is, to first order, the sum of the errors of its members, each weighted by the mean of the character over the admissible classes. For pairs (n, n + h):

  Σ_(n≤x) Λ(n)Λ(n + h) − S(h)·x ≈ 2S(h)·[(ψ(x) − x) + Σ_χ μ(q)·χ̄(h)/Π_(p|q)(p − 2)·ψ(x, χ)],

the sum over the even primitive characters χ of odd squarefree conductor q prime to h. So twin primes carry the zeros of ζ with coefficient 2S(2) = 2.6406 and the zeros of L(s, χ₅) at one third of that, and carry no line of the characters mod 3 and mod 4: for an odd character the terms of the two members cancel (for twins above 3, p ≡ 2 and p + 2 ≡ 1 mod 3). Pairs (n, 2n + 1) carry ζ at two scales, S at x and S/2 at 2x, and do carry the characters mod 3 and mod 4; the triples (0, 2, 6) and (0, 4, 6) carry the character mod 3 with opposite signs. The closed form equals the enumeration over classes for all 661 primitive characters of conductor 3 to 60 and seven gaps (own check, 4,627 cases, largest difference 1.3·10⁻¹⁴). *Test (blind; no pattern had been counted above 2·10⁹):* all patterns on (2·10⁹, 2·10¹⁰]; the error divided by √x is fitted in frequency space, band γ from 2 to 6,000, against θ(x) − x and θ(x, χ) for every primitive character of conductor up to 30 (each stands for the whole comb of its zeros by the explicit formula).

| reading | measured | predicted |
|---|---|---|
| twins: coefficient of ζ | 2.772 ± 0.119 | 2.641 (with factor 1: 1.320) |
| twins: joint amplitude of the predicted characters | 1.065 ± 0.045 | 1 |
| twins: character mod 3 | −0.034 ± 0.106 | 0 |
| twins: character mod 4 | 0.038 ± 0.107 | 0 |
| twins: χ² of the 13 coefficients predicted zero | 15.76 | 13 (limit 34.53) |
| twins per conductor 5, 7, 15, 21 | 1.12 ± 0.12, 1.13 ± 0.14, 1.12 ± 0.08, 0.93 ± 0.10 | 1 |
| twins on the explicit zero lists: ζ, χ₅, χ₋₃, χ₋₄ | 2.76 ± 0.26, 1.15 ± 0.22, 0.16 ± 0.22, −0.01 ± 0.22 | 2.64, 0.88, 0, 0 |
| gaps 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 30: coefficient of ζ | 2.69, 5.28, 2.39, 3.51, 5.38, 7.15 (errors 0.12 to 0.19) | 2.64, 5.28, 2.64, 3.52, 5.28, 7.04 |
| the same gaps: joint amplitude | 0.970, 1.010, 0.965, 1.014, 1.023, 0.960 (errors 0.042 to 0.051) | 1 |
| (n, 2n + 1): ζ at x and at 2x | 1.329 ± 0.124, 0.730 ± 0.085 | 1.320, 0.660 |
| (n, 2n + 1): characters mod 3, mod 4 (amplitude) | 0.880 ± 0.065, 1.114 ± 0.119 | 1, 1 |
| triples (0, 2, 6), (0, 4, 6): coefficient of the character mod 3 | −3.24 ± 0.92, +3.30 ± 0.94 | −2.86, +2.86 |

All ten registered decisions pass (four primary on twins, six secondary). The factor 1 for ζ is 12 errors away, a line mod 3 at the size of ζ 25 errors. Nearest to its limit: the joint amplitude of the characters for the triple (0, 2, 6), 0.72 ± 0.09 (−2.95 errors; limit 3), against 0.92 ± 0.09 for (0, 4, 6); and the gap 8 coefficient of ζ at −2.1 errors. The band γ from 2 to 300 alone is weak (ζ 1.34 ± 0.74).

*What this is and is not.* The factor 2 is the linear response of a product of two densities: a Cramér model with no zeros gives the same regression coefficient (2·S of the model; [cramer_ctrl.py](writeup/code/study/twinzeros/cramer_ctrl.py)). What the data show is that pair counts follow the real fluctuations of the primes, zero by zero and character by character, as a product of local densities does, with the selection rule above; terms quadratic in the zeros and the minor arcs are treated as noise, and their size is measured (0.5 to 0.6 of the Poisson variance), not derived. In print (first reading): the ζ part averaged over all gaps (Korevaar and te Riele, Math. Comp. 79, 2010, who state that they have no approximation for a single gap); terms linear in exceptional zeros with a singular series (Pintz, arXiv 1804.05561, major arcs only).

*What this is worth (fifty-seventh review, own code in [review57/](writeup/code/study/review57/), note [review155.md](writeup/code/study/review57/review155.md)).* Not broken for pairs; two sentences weakened. (1) *Recount and refit.* The reviewer's sieve gives the same counts in all 4,715 cells of the holdout (45,108,724 twin pairs, 45,102,596 pairs at distance 4, 43,779,393 pairs (n, 2n + 1), 4,258,577 and 4,255,419 triples) and the weighted sums to 6·10⁻¹⁵. An estimator of its own (least squares on cell increments, all 95 characters mod 420 free) gives for twins ζ 2.763 ± 0.117 (jackknife 0.121), χ₅ 0.923 ± 0.104, χ₋₃ −0.067 ± 0.105, χ₋₄ 0.021 ± 0.106: the registered errors are right to 10–20 %. On (10⁸, 2·10⁹], inside the discovery range, the same estimator gives ζ 2.692 ± 0.087 and χ₅ 0.814 ± 0.079. (2) *Prior art: known in substance.* The structure (factor 2, local density, mean of χ̄ over the admissible classes, sum over the zeros of the L-functions) is a theorem for the Goldbach problem averaged over n in a residue class (Bhowmik, Halupczok, Matsumoto and Suzuki, Mathematika 65, 2019, Theorems 2 and 4; the ζ part is Fujii 1991, as quoted by Goldston and Suriajaya; the restriction to odd squarefree moduli is in Granville's refinement of Goldbach, as quoted there). The pair (n, n + h) is the same bookkeeping with the second member a + h in place of c − a, which turns the factor 2 into 1 + χ(−1): the cancellation of the odd characters. For a single gap it is a conjecture (a major-arc explicit formula for one pair count, minor arcs without lines at x^(1/2)), and Hardy–Littlewood with error x^(1/2+ε) does not imply it at the scales of the data. So the formula is the expected one; ours are the statement for a single gap and its measurement. Not opened: Granville 2007, Fujii 1991, Montgomery–Vaughan 1973, Montgomery–Soundararajan (Beyond pair correlation), Brent 1975, Bogomolny–Keating 1995–96, Friedlander–Goldston, Languasco–Perelli–Zaccagnini, Heath-Brown 1983 (Prime twins and Siegel zeros): unknown until read. (3) *What the data test.* A product law: the pair count responds to the real local fluctuations of the primes as the product of its two members' densities. Independence of pair and prime errors and thinning of one member are consistent with every theorem for a fixed gap; on pseudo-data the estimator is unbiased (2.62 ± 0.12 with the law planted) and the data are 23 errors from independence, 12 from thinning (which predicts a line mod 3 of size −S; missed by 11.7 errors) and 10 from a constant coefficient 4. (4) *Where the weight sits.* By band the ζ coefficient is 1.3 ± 1.6 for γ in [2, 100), 0.9 ± 1.4 in [100, 300), 3.13 ± 0.45 in [300, 1000), 2.76 ± 0.35, 2.78 ± 0.22 and 2.77 ± 0.18 above: the reading is "pair counts follow θ(x) − x at scales x/6400 to x/300"; the lowest zeros taken one by one carry nothing measurable at this height. (5) *The cancellation, a second way.* Twins counted per class of p mod 5, 7 and 105 follow the class form of the law (own classes 1.830 ± 0.054, 1.620 ± 0.039, 4.272 ± 0.031 against 1.760, 1.584, 4.225; other classes 0.026 ± 0.054, 0.017 ± 0.028, 0.009 ± 0.008 against 0); in each class mod 5 the odd characters are present at the predicted ±0.44 and cancel only in the sum. A prediction not in the registration: for the gap 4 the coefficient of χ₅ has the opposite sign, −0.851 ± 0.103 against +0.923 ± 0.104 for the gap 2 (predicted ∓0.880). (6) *Weakened.* The triple (0, 2, 6): the signs mod 3 hold (−2.87 ± 0.91 and +2.95 ± 0.91), but its joint amplitude is 0.746 ± 0.087 on the holdout and 1.102 ± 0.061 on (10⁸, 2·10⁹], 3.4 errors apart, mostly from the conductor 21 (0.18 ± 0.21 against 1.26 ± 0.15); the mirror triple is stable (0.98, 1.03). Unresolved; chance is not excluded (about 0.01 after allowing for the number of readings). The largest coefficient predicted zero, an odd character of conductor 105 at 3.8 errors on the holdout, is at −0.2 on the other range. Not redone by the review: the fit for (n, 2n + 1), the fit on the explicit zero lists, the gaps 6 to 30.

**Round 154: consecutive primes do not remember their non-abelian labels** ([round154.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round154.txt), pre-registered; derivation, labels, scripts and frozen predictions [consec/](writeup/code/study/consec/) ([consec.md](writeup/code/study/consec/consec.md)); results [r154/](writeup/data/study/r154/); own check [check154.py](writeup/code/study/consec/check154.py) → [check154_out.txt](writeup/data/study/r154/check154_out.txt)). Lemke Oliver and Soundararajan's bias couples the residue classes of consecutive primes. Ash, Bate and Gross (Exp. Math. 18, 2009) saw the same for Frobenius classes in non-abelian fields, tested against independent draws, and asked (their Question 3) whether the abelian subfield is the only source. *Statement (heuristic, from Hardy–Littlewood):* every term of the expansion behind the bias is a product of major-arc pieces, and Λ(n)·a(n) has none when the form behind the label a(n) is cuspidal; so, given the congruence part of the labels (the quadratic character, p mod 4, p mod 3), the non-abelian parts of the labels of p_n and p_(n+k) are independent Chebotarev draws, at every lag and every gap. No alternative with a size could be derived. *Labels:* the number of roots of x³ − x − 1 and x³ + x − 1 mod p (discriminants −23, −31), the ideal class of p in Q(√−47) and Q(√−39), the sector of the angle of p = a² + b², and for the curve 37a the sign of a_p, a_p mod 2 and a_p mod 3. *Test (blind; nothing above 10⁸ had been labelled):* (10⁸, 10⁹] for the five lattice schemes (45,086,079 primes), (10⁸, 4·10⁸] for 37a (15,574,871 primes); lags k = 1, 2, 3.

| reading | result | rule |
|---|---|---|
| contingency inside the fibres, pooled | χ² = 119.5 on 102, p = 0.11 | falsified below 10⁻⁵ |
| 75 correlations of non-abelian labels | largest \|z\| 2.44, Σz² = 85.5 | falsified at 5 |
| within part of the block tables, non-abelian | 1.0004 ± 0.0011 | 1 within 4 errors |
| the same for the congruence part | 0.932 to 0.950 (0.938 to 0.951 from Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan) | not in the verdict |
| label against the neighbour's congruence class, 66 cells | largest \|z\| 2.20 | below 4.5 |
| gap-conditioned tables | every p ≥ 10⁻³, every contrast at gaps divisible by q below 4 errors | p ≥ 10⁻³, 4 errors |
| run lengths, triples | every p ≥ 0.11 | p ≥ 10⁻³ |
| counts per block of 100 and of 1,000 primes against Poisson | 1.00001 ± 0.00015 and 0.99970 ± 0.00049 | 1 within 4 errors |

Every registered decision passes, and the four watch items of the discovery range are fluctuations (p = 0.98, 0.35, 0.19 and z = 0.90). A correlation of 1.5·10⁻³ between the non-abelian labels (one thirtieth of the measured correlation of the quadratic characters) would have shown at 5 errors for the cubic fields. Own check: the lag-1 tables of two holdout segments recounted with other code (sympy primes, PARI root counts) agree in every cell, and from the summed tables the correlation inside the split fibre of the two cubics is between −0.0007 and +0.0003 at lags 1 to 3 (largest |z| 2.27). So for these eight labels the answer to Question 3 is yes to this precision: the memory of consecutive primes sits in the congruence part alone. A null result, in line with the derivation; the same data show that class counts of non-abelian labels per block are Poissonian for short blocks and rigid for long ones (in substance the variance law of Bui, Keating and Smith 2016; 102 of 102 cells inside the registered band; not part of the verdict). In print (read): Ash–Bate–Gross 2009, Ash–Beltis–Gross–Sinnott 2011, Lemke Oliver–Soundararajan 2016; the conditional statement and its test were not found there; not read in full: Thorner 2014, David–Devin–Nam–Schlitt (arXiv 2105.05048), Devin (arXiv 2105.02492), Kimura (arXiv 2101.05193). *Review (fifty-ninth, [review59/](writeup/code/study/review59/)):* the scheme of Q(√−47) relabelled with other code (reduction of the prime form) on the whole holdout: 45,086,079 primes, 10,982,359 pairs inside the split fibre, and the four lag-1 correlations equal to the stored ones to six digits (largest |z| 2.01).

**Round 153: the one-point law on primes alone** ([round153.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round153.txt), pre-registered; statements, constants, scripts, frozen predictions, sums and output [resonance/primeside/](writeup/code/study/resonance/primeside/) ([primeside.md](writeup/code/study/resonance/primeside/primeside.md)); fifty-sixth review with its own code [review56/](writeup/code/study/review56/)). By fact 2052 the law is equivalent to a statement on primes with no zeros in it: for a label a(p) attached to an L-function of degree 2, conductor N, weight k, S(x) = Σ_(p≤x) a(p)·ln p·e(−2√(p/N)) ~ K·x^(3/4), K = ε·c·e^(−i(2k−1)π/4)·(2√2/3)·N^(−1/4): a bias of relative size x^(−1/4) between the label of a prime and the fractional part of 2√(p/N), where the classical prime races have x^(−1/2). Fifteen cases: p = a² + b² with a(p) = 2cos(mθ_p), θ_p the angle of a + bi, m = 4, 8, 12, 16, 20 (N = 4); the same in Q(√−3) with m = 6, 12; a(p) = (number of roots of a cubic mod p) − 1 for the cubics of discriminant −23 and −31; two characters of the class group of discriminant −47; the elliptic curves 11a and 37a; Sym² of 11a (degree 3, kernel e(−3(p/121)^(1/3)), exponent 2/3); Δ. Constants from L-values, nothing fitted. *Test (blind; ranges never computed):* (3·10⁷, 10⁹] for the eleven cases with complex multiplication or a class group, (3·10⁷, 3·10⁸] for the curves, (3·10⁵, 2·10⁶] for Δ; reading = smooth sum over the range divided by the law.

| family | cases | reading |
|---|---|---|
| Q(i) | 5 | 0.9996 ± 0.0085 |
| Q(√−3) | 2 | 1.0035 ± 0.0081 |
| cubics and the class group of −47 | 4 | 1.010 ± 0.018 |
| 11a, 37a, Sym² of 11a, Δ | 4 | 1.031 ± 0.035 |
| all | 15 | 1.0031 ± 0.0055 |

χ² on the 30 readings: the law 18.7; constant 1 (the theorem for all integers) 6,772; no effect 33,344; the conjugate phase 66,479; no root number 65.7. Every registered decision passes; other error estimates for the pooled reading give ± 0.004 to ± 0.007.

*What this is worth (review).* All sums were recomputed with other methods (every one of the 67 chunks to 6·10⁻¹⁵) and the constants to 7 digits. Three corrections. (1) For the eleven lattice cases "constant 1" is the wrong null: lattice points with the density of the primes and no arithmetic already give c = 1/L(1, χ_D) (4/π for Q(i)). Against that baseline χ² is 3,236, so the law still wins clearly, but for m = 8 and m = 12 the two differ by 1 % and 4 % only. What the primes add is the factor c·L(1, χ_D): predicted 1.592, 1.155, 0.645 for m = 4, 16, 20 and 3.642 for the cubic of discriminant −23; measured 1.597 ± 0.025, 1.139 ± 0.020, 0.663 ± 0.019, 3.705 ± 0.098. (2) The content is a sieved lattice: lattice points coprime to all primes up to P, taken with the density of the primes, give exactly c_P = (1/L(1, χ_D))·Π_(p≤P split)(1 − λ_ψ²(p)/(p − 1)) (checked on the lattice), so the law says that primes behave like the lattice sieved by the small primes at scale x^(1/4) in direction: a Hardy–Littlewood statement. The data fix c to 0.6 %, which reaches primes up to about 100 (χ² 44 for the product cut at 100, 21.7 at 1,000: not separated from the full product). (3) The decision "control frequencies have rms within [0.6, 1.6] errors" could not fail, since each error was defined as that rms. *Directions in the Gaussian primes.* The excess of the primes sits in zones of width √r around the axes and around the directions of Pythagorean triples; a direction of hypotenuse h has weight (4/π)·μ(h)/φ(h) (3-4-5: −1/4 of the axis; 7-24-25: 0), because a Gaussian prime a + bi never has 3a + 4b divisible by 5 while lattice points do. Pooled amplitude of six independent ray zones: 0.991 ± 0.007 (the registered 12 zones counted each mirror pair twice). One axis zone read 5.7 errors from its model: the model lacked a first-order angular term of the half zone and the excluded line b = 0; with both, all axis zones are within 0.3 errors, and on all lattice points the corrected model matches the exact sums to 40 in 320,000. *Proof note, refereed* ([resonance/arcs/](writeup/code/study/resonance/arcs/)): the axis zone follows from the narrow-sector theorem for Gaussian primes (Ricci; Stucky 2021, Theorem 1.1) at width x^(−1/4), a theorem from the literature (the referee questions the generality in which one lemma of the cited proof is stated; see the note); each ray follows from the sector theorem in a residue class at width x^(−1/4), which is now proved up to one cited input (fact 2059, [rh/rh_proof.md](writeup/code/study/resonance/arcs/rh/rh_proof.md), refereed: sectors of width x^(−θ) in residue classes for θ < 64/231, from a published subconvex bound; in print before: 5/24; the input not read in the original is the zero-free region with growth bounds for Hecke L-functions with modulus, Coleman 1990), so the rays no longer depend on GRH; the lemma the first referee questioned is false as printed and a corrected one is proved (fact 2058; the published theorem stands), and its counterexample also violates a large-sieve inequality conjectured by Duke in 1989; the constant c is the sum over all rays; what stays open is the region between the zones. *In print:* the asymptotic with its constant for level 1 (Iwaniec–Luo–Sarnak 2000, Appendix C, conditional heuristic); Gaussian primes in narrow sectors. Not found: a numerical test on primes, the cases with a level, the directions of Pythagorean triples.

**Round 152: degree 4 again, and the genus-2 offset that did not replicate** ([round152.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round152.txt), pre-registered; scripts, frozen predictions, zeros and output [universal/blind4/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/blind4/) ([bt4_test_out.txt](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/blind4/bt4_test_out.txt), [offset_note.txt](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/blind4/offset_note.txt)); fifty-eighth review with its own code and a replication [review58/](writeup/code/study/review58/) ([r18_final_out.txt](writeup/code/study/review58/r18_final_out.txt))). Round 151 left degree 4 at 0.914 ± 0.046 and six Sym³ L-functions with root number −1 at 0.11 ± 0.42. *Test (blind):* 120 new primitive L-functions of degree 4 drawn by a seeded rule: 60 Sym³ of elliptic curves, balanced in the root number, 40 genus-2 curves (conductors 1047 to 2917, zeros to 150), 20 Rankin–Selberg products of two curves; slopes on the law of fact 2055, jackknife errors over L-functions.

| class | n | slope |
|---|---|---|
| all | 120 | 0.939 ± 0.028 |
| Sym³, root number −1 | 30 | 0.963 ± 0.134 |
| Sym³, root number +1 | 30 | 1.010 ± 0.065 |
| genus 2 | 40 | 0.912 ± 0.033 |
| genus 2, root number +1 / −1 | 14 / 26 | 0.882 ± 0.033 / 1.336 ± 0.138 |
| Rankin–Selberg products | 20 | 1.028 ± 0.084 |
| lines, all | 120 | 0.985 ± 0.040 |

Every registered decision passes and the falsifier (slope outside 1 ± max(4 errors, 0.10)) is not met; no L-function was excluded. The watch item of round 151 was a fluctuation: Sym³ with root number −1 follows the law. Read after the test, not registered: the genus-2 sums seemed to carry an additive term that does not change sign with the root number, +5.47 ± 1.59 at T = 150 (+4.97 ± 1.27 with round 151's fifteen curves).

*What this is worth (review).* The sums and every slope were reproduced exactly. (1) *The additive term does not replicate.* The reviewer wrote down both outcomes first (a real term near +5; a fluctuation near +1.4, the bounded contribution of the lowest zeros) and computed the 45 curves of the same box that had not been drawn (conductors 1055 to 2981): mean +0.58 ± 1.84, 0.45 errors from the fluctuation value and 2.4 from +5; the 39 curves not yet seen when the note was written: +1.49 ± 1.99. The spread per curve is 12.4, not the 9.4 assumed, so the two outcomes were 1.9 errors apart, not 2.5; the old and the new sample differ by 4.39 ± 2.24. The subgroup of even conductors, +11.3 before, reads −6.5. The claim of an additive term of +5 is withdrawn; all 100 curves together give +3.00 ± 1.10, which still allows a smaller one. "Independent of the root number" was not supported by the first sample either (+6.69 against +3.55). (2) *What remains for genus 2: a slope below 1.* The replication reads 0.948 ± 0.031, rounds 151 and 152 0.914 ± 0.027, all 100 curves 0.929 ± 0.020 (root number +1: 0.917 ± 0.020), where Sym³ and the products read 1.01 and 1.03. Inside the falsifier's tolerance; origin not identified (a bias of the constant c for curves with large c is one candidate; the constants of genus 2 come from the Euler product only, exact to p ≤ 1000). *Open.* A second agent looked for the cause ([review58/genus2/](writeup/code/study/review58/genus2/), [genus2.md](writeup/code/study/review58/genus2/genus2.md)) and found none: the constants recomputed with other code for 51 curves (94 % of the weight), exact to p ≤ 10⁵, move the slope from 0.9286 to 0.9288; the local factor and the form B(1)/L(1, Ad) check on random Satake parameters; the zeros of four heavy curves recomputed are identical; the deficit has no trend in the height (0.903, 0.968, 0.915 on the thirds of (0, 150]) and is the same for every window; one curve (conductor 3⁷) is not generic and does not carry it. What it found instead: the deficit sits at the line r = 1 only (the lines 2, ½, 3, ⅓ pooled: 0.982 ± 0.027) and in the arithmetic part of the constant (0.895 ± 0.028 on c − 1 against 0.983 ± 0.048 on 1); and it is not established that genus 2 stands alone: all 195 L-functions of degree 4 of rounds 151 and 152 with the replication read 0.938 ± 0.018, Sym³ with the products 0.989 ± 0.043, so "degree 4 reads 6 % low at these heights" is as good a description. A singular series cut at about 100 would fit (0.979 ± 0.023; post hoc, one free number, no derivation). A registered test on 72 new curves is drafted there, not run (3 to 4 hours of zeros). (3) The registered noise model is too small for genus 2 (scatter 1.29 and 1.80 of it for the real and imaginary parts; 0.89 for Sym³, 0.76 for the products); the slopes use jackknife errors and stand. (4) The common structure in t of the two genus-2 samples (correlation of the window profiles 0.62) is the leakage of the law's own lines p ≤ 13 (0.21 after subtracting it). (5) One curve (conductor 1403) overflowed PARI's memory because lfungenus2 leaves the root number unset; with the root number set its 117 zeros to height 60 are identical and it is included (residual +24.1; without it the mean is +0.04 ± 1.81).

**Round 151: the one-point law, blind, in degrees 1 to 4** ([round151.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round151.txt), pre-registered, with an amendment for the product term committed before any reading; scripts, frozen predictions, zeros and output [universal/blind/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/blind/); fifty-fifth review with its own code [review55/](writeup/code/study/review55/)). *Theorem:* degree 1 (c = q/φ(q)) and the exact zeros-to-primes identity in every degree (fact 2056). *Conjecture (fact 2055):* Σ_(0<γ≤T) e^(2iθ_L(γ))·r^(−iγ) = −ε·c_L·ρ_L(r)/√(ab)·T/2π for every primitive L, c_L = B_L(1)/L(1, Ad L) = Π_p G_p(1/p). *Test:* 130 primitive L-functions drawn by a seeded rule, every one with PARI's functional-equation check passed before the registration; predictions frozen and committed before a zero was computed. Errors below are jackknife errors over L-functions; the registered noise model was too small by a factor 1.5 to 6 in variance (χ²/n = 3.1 with the registered errors), so every registered error was too small.

| family | degree | n | slope on the law | registered error |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| complex Dirichlet characters, q = 17–59 (theorem) | 1 | 38 | 1.004 ± 0.009 | 0.008 |
| rational newforms, weights 4–12 | 2 | 30 | 1.002 ± 0.018 | 0.011 |
| Sym² of elliptic curves | 3 | 30 | 1.003 ± 0.040 | 0.022 |
| Sym³ of elliptic curves | 4 | 15 | 0.899 ± 0.103 | 0.053 |
| genus-2 curves | 4 | 15 | 0.917 ± 0.051 | 0.030 |
| all | | 128 | 1.002 ± 0.008 | 0.006 |
| degree ≥ 2 | | 90 | 0.997 ± 0.016 | |
| degree ≥ 3 | | 60 | 0.969 ± 0.031 | |
| Artin factors of 30 cubic fields (zeros of ζ_K without those of ζ) | 2 | 30 | 0.964 ± 0.018 | 0.012 |

Lines at r = 2, ½, 3, ⅓: 1.005 ± 0.005. The 27 L-functions with root number −1: 1.002 ± 0.027. Complex root numbers: 1.004 − 0.012i (the conjugate root number would give 0.02, none 0.38). The amplitude follows the constant: exponent 1.007 ± 0.015 on all, 1.016 ± 0.030 in degree ≥ 2. Against the alternatives in degree ≥ 2 (χ² on 90 with the measured noise): the law 94.5, constant 1 for every L 1,679, 1/L(1, Ad) without the product B 478.5; in degree 4 alone 30.0, 216.1, 66.5. The cubic field of discriminant −239, where 3 splits completely and c = 0: +14.2 − 10.5i against 0 (1.5 errors; a law with constant 1 would give −47.7). The registered falsifier (pooled slope outside 1 ± 0.05, or a family outside 1 ± 0.10 by more than 4 errors) is not met.

*What is weak (review).* 96 % of the weight of the pooled slope is degree 1 and 2; the statement for degree ≥ 3 is 0.969 ± 0.031 and for degree 4 0.914 ± 0.046, 1.9 errors low. The constants are not the cause: recomputed independently, Sym³ within 0.3 % and genus 2 within 0.24 %. The six Sym³ L-functions with root number −1 read 0.11 ± 0.42 (2.1 errors from 1; height only 70): not significant, to be watched. The one registered decision that failed, the Artin factors (3.0 registered errors), is 2.0 errors with the jackknife and sits at the edges of the sharp window: the bands (0, 50] and (250, 300] read 0.90 and 0.92, the middle 0.99, a Hann window 0.993; the prime side of fact 2056 reproduces the zero sums field by field (rms 0.13 with the Hann window), so the zeros are right and the prime sums themselves do this. Two characters mod 17 were excluded by the completeness rule; that was a fault of the rule (the offset of the counting function was taken on the wrong branch when Re L(½, χ) < 0), both sets are complete and read 0.948 and 1.007; with them: 1.0015 ± 0.0076 on 130. Products: the registered term reads 1.06 ± 0.27 and the amended one (all lines a/b) 0.89 ± 0.35, which the design cannot separate; the second product reading (zeros of the Artin factor under the phase of ζ) reads 1.335 ± 0.058 and fails: a term of the zero density with a stationary point at t = 2π, of size about ln|d|, is missing from the registered mechanism (with it, found afterwards: 0.967 ± 0.017). The ten pairs of elliptic curves reuse old zero lists and are not blind. The amendment was committed after the zeros of the cubic fields had been computed and before any reading; its numbers come from the frozen constants.

**Round 150: maxima and the frozen partition function of |L| per missing prime** ([round150.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round150.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts and frozen predictions [lowzero/freeze/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/freeze/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/freeze/derivation.md)); output [r150/](writeup/data/study/r150/); not reviewed). On intervals of length 2π take M = max ln|L(½ + it, χ_D)| and Z_β = ∫|L|^(2β). The picture of a log-correlated field (Fyodorov, Hiary and Keating) says that above β = 1 the partition function freezes: it is carried by the maximum, and its dependence on arithmetic stays that of β = 1. *Theorem-level controls:* the second moment per missing prime with its lower-order term, and the two exact operations on one L-function: removing the Euler factor of p from an L-function that has it, and adding it with an independent phase to one that lacks it. *Question:* does the contrast between characters with p | D and p ∤ D equal the removal (γ = 0) or the addition (γ = 1)? *Test (blind; 240 characters with 1,500 < |D| ≤ 2,400):* γ = −0.04 ± 0.10 for the mean of M and −0.07 ± 0.11 for ln Z₃ (variance of M: 0.07 ± 0.03): removal, and addition is rejected. Frozen: the slope ln Z₃ − ln Z₂ per missing prime is 0.98 ± 0.02 of the critical factor, where a factor that follows β gives 3.4 to 3.9. The amplitude of the shift of the maximum on the exact critical factor is 0.93 ± 0.02 (the leading factor 1 − 1/p alone would give 1.18), inside the registered band and 3 errors below 1. Every registered decision passes. As in round 147, the removal reading tests that a missing prime is an absent Euler factor and nothing more; the frozen slope is the reading with content. No statement on maxima or freezing for Dirichlet L-functions in the t-aspect, or per prime dividing the conductor, was found in print.

**The one-point law after round 148: a theorem on average, a singular series, and all degrees** (facts 2052–2057 in [FACTS.md](FACTS.md); the exact formulas in every degree: [degd/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/degd/), refereed; proof notes [proof/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/ecross/proof/) and [family/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/ecross/family/), each refereed by an independent agent with its own code; the general statement and its first test [lowzero/universal/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/) ([general_law.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/general_law.md), [results_table.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/universal/results_table.md))). Round 148 left a conjecture for the zeros of one elliptic curve. Four things were added.

1. *The exact link to primes (theorem, unconditional; fact 2052).* For smooth windows the sum Σ_ρ h(ρ)·X(ρ)^(−1), which is Σ_γ H(γ)·e^(2iθ(γ)) under GRH, equals a sum over prime powers with the kernel e^(i(2θ(t) − t ln n)), and by stationary phase with the resonant kernel e(−2√(n/N)). Over all integers the same kernel has an exact dual side whose first term gives the law with constant 1 (fact 2053). Both identities hold for every cuspidal L-function of GL(d), unconditionally, with the resonant kernel e(−d·(n/Q)^(1/d)) (fact 2056; checked on a complex character, Δ and a symmetric square to 10⁻¹²); the stationary-phase error is proved to be o(T) with the closed kernel only for d ≤ 2.
2. *The law on average over a family (theorem; fact 2054).* Over the newforms of prime level N with harmonic weights, the mean of (root number × one-point sum) is −(1/2π)·∫H·(1 + o(1)), unconditionally for T ≤ exp(c·√(ln N)) and under GRH for Dirichlet L-functions for T ≤ N^(1/3). The main term is a single term of the Petersson formula, the modulus c = N, whose Kloosterman sum is −1 for every prime; no other modulus resonates. The constant 1 is the harmonic mean of the conjectured c_f (under GRH for Sym²f; computed at six levels: 1.015), and the Hecke-twisted means at every rational line agree with the conjecture prime by prime, which is what tests the shape of c_f. The range is the opposite of the data's (level large against height), so this is a law on average, not for one form at large height. "Almost all forms" needs the second moment Σ_f ω_f·|Z_f + ε_f·c_f·∫H/2π|² = o(T²), and that route is closed at one named point ([second/second_moment.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/ecross/family/second/second_moment.md); exact Petersson double sums at N = 10,007 and 5,003, validated against the 15 eigenforms of level 61): the T² term of the second moment is located (in the moduli c = N·m with m ≲ T/2π, one per height lag 2πm, and inside each Kloosterman sum in two single exponentials per divisor q of m, with mean weight μ²(q)/q², the q-th term of ζ(2)/ζ(4)), generic pairs of primes contribute nothing, and what is left to bound are bilinear forms in Kloosterman sums over primes with both variables of length N·T² against the modulus N. The sharp one-modulus bound gives T³·ln(NT)·ln²T where o(T²) is needed, and the published bilinear bounds (Fouvry–Kowalski–Michel 2014; Kowalski–Michel–Sawin 2017; Blomer–Fouvry–Kowalski–Michel–Milićević 2017) need shorter variables. Computed: the quantity is 0.8 of its diagonal at every height tried (T = 6 to 30), so its minor arcs are small in the data; not proved.
3. *What the constant is (computed; level 1).* c_f = B_f/L(1, Sym²f) = Π_p (1 − λ_f(p²)/p) = Σ_q μ(q)·λ_f(q²)/q: the Hardy–Littlewood singular series of the resonance over primes. Each major arc a/q contributes the weight (φ(q)/q)·λ_f(q²) of the integer sum (for Δ at q = 2, 3, 5, 7: 10.866, 12.928, 12.689, 22.08 against 10.867, 12.933, 12.633, 22.24). In every degree the local factor is G_p(1/p) with G_p(X) = 1 − (|λ(p)|² − 1)·X + … of degree ≤ d − 1, and the major arc at q is computed exactly (fact 2057). So for one form the law is a singular-series conjecture in the family of this study's other laws; the open part is a minor-arc bound o(x^(3/4)) (known: x^(5/6)). It does not follow from GRH for Dirichlet L-functions, and it does follow from GRH for L(s, f) with the one-over-one ratios conjecture, which is the recipe written as a hypothesis.
4. *All degrees (conjecture; fact 2055).* For a primitive L-function of degree d: Σ_(T₁<γ≤T₂) e^(2iθ_L(γ)) = −ε_L·c_L·(T₂ − T₁)/2π, c_L = B_L(1)/L(1, Ad L), with lines at rational r; c = 0 for products. Degree 1 is a theorem (c = q/φ(q)). First test, with the predictions fixed before the zeros were computed (file times and a hash, not a registration in this repository):

| family | degree | L-functions | slope on the law |
|---|---|---|---|
| complex Dirichlet characters (theorem; control) | 1 | 4 | 0.997 ± 0.033 |
| level-1 forms of weight 12, 16, 18, 20, 22, 26 | 2 | 6 | 0.963 ± 0.036 |
| Artin L-functions of the cubic fields of discriminant −23, −31 | 2 | 2 | 1.035 ± 0.057 |
| symmetric squares of elliptic curves | 3 | 6 | 0.936 ± 0.068 |
| genus-2 curves | 4 | 2 | 1.31 ± 0.39 |
| all | | 20 | 0.985 ± 0.021 |
| the 10 first excluded (review): weight 2 with real-quadratic coefficients, weight 24, nebentypus of order 6 | 2 | 10 | 1.005 ± 0.055 |

The three forms with root number −1 give sums of the opposite sign, and the sums of the complex characters lie at 152.5°, −155.9°, −147.3°, 148.8° where −ε·c gives 153.6°, −153.6°, −148.3°, 148.3°. Lines at r = 2, ½, 3, ⅓: 0.981 ± 0.015. For Δ a second computation (431 zeros, 20 < γ ≤ 400) gives −145.03 − 1.20i against −142.78; the constant 1 of the integer sum would give −60.5 and the Petersson term c = 1 alone −21.3. Products: L(f)·L(f̄) for X₁(13) gives 0 within 2 errors; ζ_K for the cubic field of discriminant −23 does not (−62 against 0), and a finite-height term found afterwards (the degree-1 phase meets the lines r = 1/k of the other factor: −c_ρ·Σ_(k≤T/2π) ρ(1/k)) accounts for about half of it: not a registered prediction. Degree 4 is measured to 40 %.

*Review (fifty-fourth, own code [review54/](writeup/code/study/review54/)).* All 20 sums and all constants were recomputed. Ten of the 33 planned cases had been left out because PARI gave too few zeros; the cause is not its zero finder but the embedded coefficients, accurate to about 10⁻¹⁹ only, which ruins the values of L once e^(πt/2) exceeds 10¹⁹ while the functional-equation check still passes. With the q-expansions embedded exactly, all ten were computed and pass: pooled 1.005 ± 0.055, among them a negative constant (weight 24: c = −0.086, sum +5.8 − 5.9i against +4.2 − 5.9i), two forms of one Hecke orbit with c = 0.040 and 2.32 (sums +13.9 and −155.1 against −2.5 and −147.9), and the first complex root number in degree 2 (nebentypus of order 6, level 13, 925 zeros each to height 600: the part of the sums that is odd under f ↔ f̄ is 30 ± 11 against 26; without the root number 0, with its conjugate −26). On 158 L-functions (these 20 and the 138 curves) the amplitude goes as c^(1.00 ± 0.02); χ² on 316 readings: the law 332, constant 1 for every L 2,573, 1/L(1, Ad) without the product B 2,125, no root number 19,456. The shortfall of 1.5 % is not significant (1.3 errors), depends on the window (Sym²: 0.936 sharp, 1.057 with a Hann window) and is of the size seen in degree 1, where the law is a theorem (finite-height prime sums differ from their asymptotic by ±3 %). Two secondary terms were found: for conductor 1 the squares of primes add up coherently (e(−2√(p²)) = 1 and Λ_f(p²) has mean −1) to a term of order √T, computed exactly (weight 12 at T = 600: −4.8 + 10.4i predicted, −8.8 + 7.7i measured; weight 24: imaginary part −5.90 against −5.90), so the imaginary parts of the level-1 sums are not noise; and in a product every line a/b of one factor has a stationary point under the degree-1 phase of the other, not only a = 1: for the cubic field of discriminant −23 the sum over a ≤ 12 gives −44.6 − 8.3i against −57.6 − 5.7i measured on the zeros of the Artin factor (a = 1 alone: −31.4), which leaves a residual of about one error. *In print:* degree 1 (Arias de Reyna 2014; Durkan, Hughes and Pearce-Crump 2026, whose Theorem 5 gives −T/2π and every rational line for ζ; Dirichlet characters by the same proof). For degree 2 the core of the law is in print, a point the searches of rounds 148–151 missed because the source could not be opened and which the fifty-sixth review found: Iwaniec, Luo and Sarnak (2000, Appendix C): for a cusp form of level 1 they derive, under two hypotheses on sums of Hecke eigenvalues and RH for sym²f, Σ λ_f(n)Λ(n)·e(−2√n)·φ(n/X) = Z·X^(3/4) + O(X^(5/8+ε)) with Z built from Q(1, f)/L(1, sym²f), Q an absolutely convergent Euler product; they report numerical experiments of Rubinstein (private communication, 1998) in which the corresponding phases at the zeros of L(s, Δ) are badly distributed mod 1 while those of ζ are not, and state that the same happens for cusp forms on GL_m. So the phenomenon and, for level 1, its constant are theirs. Not found in print: the zero-side law with root number and general conductor, the lines at rational r, the constant as B_L(1)/L(1, Ad L) with the local factor G_p in every degree, the family-average theorem, and any published numerical test (zeros or primes).

**Round 149: the mean square of a product of three different L-functions** ([round149.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round149.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts, sets and frozen predictions [lowzero/moment3/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/moment3/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/moment3/derivation.md)); values and output [r149/](writeup/data/study/r149/); fifty-third review with its own code [review53/](writeup/code/study/review53/)). The statistic is M₃ = ∫₁₀₀¹⁰⁰⁰ |L(½ + it, χ₁)·L(½ + it, χ₂)·L(½ + it, χ₃)|² dt for three different real primitive characters. *Theorem:* for one character the mean square has the classical main term; for two different characters the full quadratic polynomial is proved (Topacogullari 2021), as T → ∞: at q ≈ 10³ and T ≤ 10³ its error bound exceeds the main term. For three, only the order T·(ln T)³ is known, under GRH and the Ramanujan conjecture (Hagen 2024). *Conjecture:* the leading term h(0)·T·Π ln(q_j T), with h(0) containing Π L(1, χ_iχ_j)², is Heap's Conjecture 4 (2013); the cubic polynomial with all lower-order terms, P(t) = Σ_m h[m]·Π_(j∉m) (ln(q_j t/2π) + 2γ), is the recipe of Conrey, Farmer, Keating, Rubinstein and Snaith with one swap per L-function (8 terms), worked out here; at these heights the leading term is only 35–160 % of P. *Test (blind):* 56 characters with 100 ≤ |D| ≤ 1,300, |L| on a grid of step 0.05; all 1,540 pairs as control, 2,000 triples.

| reading | measured | recipe | alternative |
|---|---|---|---|
| one L-function, second moment (theorem) | 0.9973 ± 0.0012 | 1 | |
| pairs (theorem) | 0.9950 ± 0.0030 | 1 | leading term only: 1.50 |
| triples, level, raw | 0.994 ± 0.012 | 1 | diagonal of the product's approximate functional equation: 1.113 |
| triples, level, registered pair control | 1.006 ± 0.008 | 1 | 1.113 |
| triples, level, per-character offsets removed (review) | 0.998 ± 0.007 | 1 | 1.113 |
| three-body Euler factor (0 = absent) | 0.995 ± 0.019 | 1 | 0 |
| leading term only (Heap's statement) | 1.76 ± 0.07 | | 1 |

Every registered decision passes.

*What this is worth (review).* The level is the content. No theorem gives it for three L-functions, and the one diagonal model that is exact for one L-function and within 1.2 % for two (the sharp-cutoff diagonal of the product's approximate functional equation) reads 1.113; its excess is absent from the data at 102 ± 6 % (95 ± 7 % with the registered pair control). The registered statement that this model is fixed to ±2 % was wrong: smooth weights move it between 0.90 and 1.08, but those versions fail the theorem controls on singles and pairs by 3–14 %; the weights that pass both controls give 1.113 to 1.19. The registered pair control is biased up by the per-character offsets that the singles show (0.27 % low each), so the level to quote is 0.998 ± 0.007. The first-order lower terms are fixed at amplitude 1.00 ± 0.02, the second-order ones at 1.02 ± 0.08; the third order is not constrained. The three-body Euler factor (A₃/ΠA_ij from 0.28 to 2.45 over the triples, mostly the pattern of χ_j(2)) is carried at 0.995 ± 0.019, but every diagonal-type model and the random Euler product carry it too: this reading confirms the Euler-product structure at k = 3 and does not separate the recipe from the diagonal. Corrections: the stated quadrature error (2·10⁻⁵) is exceeded for 82 triples (worst 2.9·10⁻⁴, an end-point term; the level moves by 10⁻⁶); the lower bound cited as unconditional is the project's own sketch, Hagen's bounds are both conditional. Watch item: the 109 triples whose conductors share primes in two pairs read 0.972 ± 0.014 (one of 11 subgroups). *In print:* the conjectures and the theorem for pairs; no numerical test of the mean square of a product of two or three different L-functions was found.

**Round 148: a one-point law for the zeros of an elliptic curve, and no cross term between two curves** ([round148.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round148.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts and frozen predictions [lowzero/ecross/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/ecross/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/ecross/derivation.md)); results [r148/](writeup/data/study/r148/); fifty-first review with its own code [review51/](writeup/code/study/review51/)). The question had two opposite answers: the recipe gives the zeros of two elliptic curves a swapped cross term as in round 143, a prime-side argument gave none. With θ_E(t) = (t/2)·ln N − t·ln 2π + Im lnΓ(1 + it): *Theorem:* the explicit formula turns Σ_{T₁<γ≤T₂} e^(2iθ_E(γ)) into −(e^(3πi/4)/√2)·N^(−1/4)·Σ Λ_E(n)·n^(−1/4)·e(−2√(n/N)) over 2π√(n/N) in (T₁, T₂], for smooth windows: with a window h of width Δ between T^(1/2+δ) and T^(1−δ) on both sides the error is O(√N·T^(1/2)), unconditionally, and before stationary phase the formula is exact (proof note [gl2_onepoint_proof.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/ecross/proof/gl2_onepoint_proof.md) with [check_gl2.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/ecross/proof/check_gl2.py): zeros against primes to 10⁻⁹ on the exact form; refereed by an independent agent with its own code: facts 2052 and 2053 in [FACTS.md](FACTS.md)). With sharp edges on both sides the method of Arias de Reyna 2014 (Theorem 3.1, where for ζ the sum is −ψ(T/2π) + O(T^(1/2)·ln²T)) gives in degree 2 only O(√N·T·ln²(NT)), the size of the main term itself, so the sharp form decides nothing (an error o(T) there is unproved, not disproved); the proved bound is also far weaker than the agreement seen at these heights (0.03–0.24 on sums of size 10–100). The kernel is the Voronoi kernel of GL(2), not a linear twist, and it resonates. *Conjecture (the recipe):* the main term Σ_{T₁<γ≤T₂} e^(2iθ_E(γ)) = −w·c_E·(T₂ − T₁)/2π with w the root number and c_E = B_E/L(1, Sym²E) (B_E an absolutely convergent Euler product), and a line −w·c_E·ρ(r)/√(ab)·(T₂ − T₁)/2π at every rational r = a/b in Σ e^(2iθ_E(γ))·r^(−iγ) (ρ multiplicative, ρ(p) ≈ a_p/√p). It is a prime number theorem for the resonance: Σ_{n≤x} Λ_E(n)·e(−2√(n/N)) ~ (2√2/3)·e^(−3πi/4)·w·c_E·(x³/N)^(1/4). Over all integers the resonance is a theorem (Voronoi summation); over primes it is open even under GRH, which gives only the trivial bound x^(3/4)·N^(−1/4)·ln x for a smoothed sum, the number of zeros in the window (unconditionally x^(5/6), Zhao 2006, level 1; the asymptotic with an unnamed constant is a heuristic of Iwaniec, Luo and Sarnak). The constant has a clean origin: over all integers the same sum has an exact dual side (Voronoi) whose first term gives the law with c = 1; writing Λ_E = (λ·ln) ∗ μ_E and applying that identity for each divisor gives c_E = B_E/L(1, Sym²E) and the line factors, the one unjustified step being the divisors for which the inner sum is short. *Proved from the recipe:* the swapped cross term of two curves, X₁₂ = (1/T)·Σ e^(2iθ₁(γ₁) − 2iθ₂(γ₂))·K(γ₂ − γ₁), has mean (1/4π²)·w₁w₂c₁c₂·∫|L(1 + iu, E₁ × E₂)|²·A₁₂(iu)·K(u)du, and this is exactly the sum over rational r of the products of the two curves' lines. So for two curves with independent fluctuations the cross term holds as soon as the lines hold. *Test:* the 138 curves of round 144 (zeros 20–400), 9,453 pairs; the line at r = 1 was measured before registration (0.9875 ± 0.0116), all else blind.

| reading | measured | law | other hypotheses |
|---|---|---|---|
| 12 lines at r ≠ 1 (2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 3/2 and inverses), pooled | 0.987 ± 0.008 | 1 | none: 0 |
| same, law without the root number | 0.008 ± 0.008 | 0 | 1 |
| X₁₂ on the frozen law (Gaussian width 0.5) | 0.967 ± 0.012 | 1 | none: 0; r = 1 line only: 0.40 |
| X₁₂ beyond the product of the measured r = 1 sums | 0.963 ± 0.016 | 1 | r = 1 line only: 0 |
| lines paired r with 1/r | 0.006 ± 0.022 | 0 | 1 |
| X₁₂ without root numbers | 0.006 ± 0.009 | 0 | 1 |
| continuum: X₁₂ minus the measured 13 lines minus the law of the others, mean | −0.0011 ± 0.0010 | 0 | −0.0055 |

Every registered decision passes.

*What this is worth (review).* The content is the one-point law; X₁₂ adds nothing to it. With no law at all, the products of the measured lines of the two curves (89 lines with ab ≤ 30) predict X₁₂ with slope 1.017 ± 0.007 and less scatter than the law (0.056 against 0.066); what is left, the continuum, has mean −0.0003 ± 0.0009, where an isogenous pair would give about 2.08: no correlation between a_p of one curve and a_p′ of another along p′N₁ ≈ pN₂ above about 0.15 % of that. The slopes below 1 are one deficit, not several: the 13 lines pooled give 0.987 ± 0.006 on the fresh curves (2.2 errors; 0.992 ± 0.006 with the 20 development curves, which ran high), the same on heights 20–200 and 200–400, and X₁₂ carries it twice (0.978 ± 0.012 expected from the measured lines, times 0.989 ± 0.005). The registered allowance of 2 % for finite-height terms has no basis: the recipe gives the density −w·c_E/2π at every height with no lower-order term. The data fix the constant: the amplitude goes as c_E^(1.01 ± 0.03) across curves (c_E from 0.12 to 3.42), and a constant, 1/L(1, Sym²E) alone, c_E² or √c_E fit 3 to 13 times worse. The prime sum itself, without zeros: 1.008 ± 0.020 of the law for all 138 curves at x = 5·10⁵. One curve's zero side and prime side differ by more than the others (N = 1,671), not explained. Corrections: the r = 1 slope was fitted through the origin (with an intercept 0.9865 ± 0.0124); the continuum has a slope on the law of −0.011 ± 0.005. *In print:* the degree-1 case (Arias de Reyna 2014; Schoißengeier), the bound for the resonance over primes (Zhao 2006), and, as the fifty-sixth review found after this round, the prime-side asymptotic with its constant for a cusp form of level 1 as a conditional heuristic (Iwaniec–Luo–Sarnak 2000, Appendix C, with unpublished numerics of Rubinstein for the zeros of L(s, Δ)): the law is theirs in substance for level 1. Not found: the zero-side law with root number and conductor N, its rational lines, or a published numerical test.

**Round 147: the spacing of neighbouring zeros per prime dividing the conductor** ([round147.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round147.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts and frozen predictions [lowzero/spacing/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/spacing/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/spacing/derivation.md)); results [r147/](writeup/data/study/r147/); fiftieth review with its own code [review50/](writeup/code/study/review50/)). Rounds 115–116 measured that each small prime dividing the conductor lowers the variance of the unfolded nearest-neighbour spacings by about 0.014 and gave no law for the size. *Theorem:* by the explicit formula the counting function of the zeros of L(s, χ_D) contains for each prime p ∤ D the wave dN_p(t) = (1/π)·arg(1 − χ_D(p)·p^(−1/2)·e^(it ln p)) and none for p | D (the projection of N − θ/π on it is 1.000 in the data). *Model (the wave closure):* spacing statistics are not linear in the density; their change per prime follows from the wave only if the rest of the spectrum is the same with and without p and independent of p's phase. Then the zeros with p good are the zeros with p missing, re-mapped by x → x + dN_p(x) with a random phase, and the statistics follow from the sine-kernel process under that map. It predicts per missing prime a lower Var(s) (−0.0126, −0.0144, −0.0136 for p = 2, 3, 5 at ln(qt/2π) = 9.4) and a higher mean gap ratio ⟨r̃⟩ = ⟨min(s_n, s_(n+1))/max(s_n, s_(n+1))⟩ (+0.0026, +0.0038, +0.0050): the wave makes neighbouring spacings unequal, so its absence makes them more equal. The slow-wave limit of the same map (a random dilation; per prime this is what the effective-matrix-size law of Bogomolny, Bohigas, Leboeuf and Monastra with bad-prime constants amounts to) predicts a variance drop 1.25–1.3 times larger and no change of the gap ratio at order 1/L² or 1/L³. *Test (blind; these statistics had not been computed on these sets):* amplitudes on the law's pattern for p = 2, 3, 5.

| zero sets | L-functions | Var(s) amplitude | gap-ratio amplitude |
|---|---|---|---|
| B, Dn, Fn (heights 100–600; primary) | 550 | 0.954 ± 0.013 | 1.025 ± 0.091 |
| C, Bp (heights 600–1,000) | 363 | 0.912 ± 0.018 | 0.954 ± 0.150 |
| all five | 913 | 0.940 ± 0.011 | 1.006 ± 0.078 |

The gap ratio rises per missing prime as predicted; "no change" is rejected at 10 errors, and a test that uses no law (L-functions with p | D against p ∤ D, matched on the other two primes; permutations) gives +0.0026, +0.0029, +0.0038 for p = 2, 3, 5 at 4.9, 5.0 and 5.6 errors. The slow-wave limit is rejected on both statistics. The variance amplitude is not 1: on the primary sets it passes the registered measurement test (3.7 errors), on the others and on all five it does not (4.7 and 5.7 errors); it lies inside the ±10 % that the registration stated as the closure's own uncertainty. The registered height dependence (same discriminants, 100–600 against 600–1,000): −0.032 ± 0.039 where the law gives 0 and "no dependence on height" +0.16, which is 4.9 errors off, below the registered 5: not decided. The level formula misses the deviation from the sine kernel by 11 % (+0.003 in Var), as declared.

*What this is worth (review).* It tests the closure, not arithmetic: nothing beyond the wave enters. The review ran the map on the real zeros. Adding p's wave with a random phase to the zeros of the L-functions with p | D gives 0.985 of the law; removing the true wave from the zeros with p ∤ D gives 0.914; the data (0.94) lie between. Both numbers are nearly free of noise, so the two directions differ by 7 % of the effect: the zeros are not exactly "a base spectrum plus an independent wave", and the closure holds to about ±4 %. What is learned is the response of real spectra to a known perturbation and the size of the failure of independence. A reading with conjectural content, made after the test: the number of gaps below 0.3 mean spacings is 0.776 ± 0.006 of the sine kernel's and 0.995 ± 0.008 of what the Bogomolny–Keating pair correlation with bad primes gives (below 0.15: 0.998 ± 0.023). A new fact, not explained: for one L-function the spacing statistics of adjacent height bands are anticorrelated (−0.38 to −0.44 for Var, −0.29 to −0.34 for ⟨r̃⟩; non-adjacent bands and a GUE control: 0), so designs that pair height bands need a paired error. Three L-functions were excluded by the completeness rule at its threshold (1.51 against 1.5); they are complete and change the amplitudes by at most 0.001. *In print:* the effective matrix size for ζ (Bogomolny–Bohigas–Leboeuf–Monastra 2006; Forrester–Mays 2015; Bornemann–Forrester–Mays 2017), the gap ratio of ζ's zeros (Nishigaki 2025), spacings of Dirichlet L-zeros averaged over characters (Rubinstein). No spacing or gap-ratio statistic per prime dividing the conductor was found.

**Round 144: degree 2: the zeros of elliptic-curve L-functions** ([round144.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round144.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts and frozen predictions [lowzero/deg2/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/deg2/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/deg2/derivation.md)); readings, zeros and repair logs [r144/](writeup/data/study/r144/); forty-ninth review with its own code [review49/](writeup/code/study/review49/)). For the L-function of an elliptic curve E the orbit p^k carries the weight W_p(k) = (α_p^k + β_p^k)², with α_p + β_p = a_p/√p: a_p²/p on the first traversal and (a_p²/p − 2)² on the second. A prime with a_p = 0 is absent on the first traversal and doubled on the second; a prime with large |a_p| is doubled on the first. So, unlike in degree 1, primes can make the zeros less rigid than random matrices. The two-point law (the ratios recipe; Bui–Keating–Smith 2016 and Smith's thesis, where only the prime side was checked) has a diagonal term with these weights and an off-diagonal term with ζ(1 + x)ζ(1 − x)·L(1 + x, Sym²E)·L(1 − x, Sym²E)/L(1, Sym²E)²; the mean density is twice that of a degree-1 L-function of conductor √N. *Test (blind):* 138 semistable curves with conductors 307–2,929 drawn by a seeded rule, zeros with 20 ≤ γ ≤ 400 (114,703), the number variance Σ²(ℓ) for ℓ = 0.5, …, 10 against a prediction frozen per curve. As registered, 136 curves (two excluded by the completeness rule): level within 0.0007 at all 20 lengths (Hotelling p = 0.11); arithmetic amplitude 0.997 ± 0.003; "every good prime has weight 1": χ² 9,090 against 35.8 for the law on the p = 2 contrasts; second traversal of 2: 1.048 ± 0.022; first traversal of 2: 0.990 ± 0.005; the off-diagonal factor with the symmetric square: 1.03 ± 0.05; all 15 curves predicted above GUE at ℓ = 6 are above it (mean +0.0623 ± 0.0014, law +0.0627). Every registered decision passes. The two exclusions were a bug: the repair of missed close pairs searched overlapping windows and stored the found pair twice (deg2_zeros.py, fixed); with the duplicates removed all 138 curves are complete and nothing changes (Hotelling p = 0.19, the same amplitudes).

*What this is worth (review).* Most of it is the explicit formula. The number variance is the variance of S(t + w) − S(t), and S carries at each prime power a line of amplitude (α_p^k + β_p^k)/(k·p^(k/2)): the weight W_p(k) is the Euler product itself, and for the lines the band resolves their variances add (a theorem for the range τ < ½, n below about 10³, under GRH for L(s, E)). With the law replaced by "the lines n ≤ 1,000 with their weights, the plain GUE form factor beyond", no zero used, the registered regressions give 0.989 ± 0.004 for the arithmetic amplitude, 1.056 ± 0.023 for the doubled orbit, 0.989 ± 0.005 for the first traversal of 2, and +0.0542 and +0.0628 for the curves above GUE at ℓ = 4 and 6 (zeros: +0.0544, +0.0623). Summed exactly over the band, the primes up to 10⁵ give 70–93 % of each curve's deviation from the law; with that removed the amplitude is 1.000 ± 0.002. So the weights per traversal, the doubled orbit, the bad primes and "less rigid than GUE" verify the zero lists and the Frobenius traces to 0.1–0.3 %; they do not test the conjecture. The conjecture sits where the lines run out. (a) Lines never give the level: zeros minus lines at ℓ = 4 is 0.17 with n ≤ 10³ and still 0.087 with n ≤ 10⁷ (the diagonal sum diverges like ln ln); the GUE form factor beyond τ = ½, itself a conjecture in degree 2, supplies that, confirmed to about 1 %. (b) The recipe's arithmetic beyond lines plus GUE is small (+0.0003 to +0.0014) and the zeros prefer it (Hotelling p 0.10 for the law, 0.011 without it). (c) The off-diagonal factor of each curve, |L(1 + iε, Sym²E)|²: 1.03 ± 0.05 where lines plus GUE give 0.43, so 12 errors from "no arithmetic in the off-diagonal term"; this is the main test of the round, registered only as secondary. A hint, not a result: with the prime part removed the remainder lies 0.0005 ± 0.0001 below the law, inside the registered allowance of 0.002 and of unknown origin. Also corrected: the frozen predictions average over the height band with 10 nodes, which is not converged for single curves at ℓ ≥ 8 (errors up to 0.004 for one curve, mean over curves 3·10⁻⁵, decisions unaffected); χ² for the p = 3 contrasts is on 80 cells, and both per-prime χ² pass against a proper null (p = 0.46 and 0.30). *In print:* GUE at leading order for five curves (Rubinstein 2004); lower-order terms in the family aspect near the central point (Huynh–Keating–Snaith 2009); the prime side (Bui–Keating–Smith 2016; Smith 2016). No test of arithmetic terms on the zeros of one degree-2 L-function in the t-aspect was found.

**Round 146: the variance of ln|L′(ρ)| per missing prime** ([round146.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round146.txt), pre-registered; derivation, scripts and frozen predictions [lowzero/varlp/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/varlp/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/varlp/derivation.md)); values and output [r146/](writeup/data/study/r146/); fifty-second review with its own code [review52/](writeup/code/study/review52/)). Round 142 measured that the variance over the zeros of ln|L′(ρ, χ_D)| is lower for each small prime p | D and had only a heuristic for the size. Since ln|L′(ρ)| is the sum of ln|γ − γ′| over the other zeros plus a smooth term, its variance over zeros is an integral over the two- and three-point functions of the zeros conditioned on a zero at γ; with the bad-prime factors of rounds 136 and 138 this gives the drop with no model step and no parameter. First order: ΔVar = −½·Li₂(1/p) + (ln p/L)·ℓ·(2 + ℓ) with ℓ = ln(p/(p − 1)) and L = ln(qt/2π); the registered prediction is the full integral. *Test:* L′ at the 247,489 zeros at heights 600–1,000 of sets C and Bp (363 L-functions), where L′ had not been computed; the zeros themselves were the tests of rounds 136 and 138, which confirmed the two laws used here, so this is a new statistic on those zeros, not new zeros.

| p | 2 | 3 | 5 | 7 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| measured (errors 0.0023–0.0030) | −0.1943 | −0.1069 | −0.0498 | −0.0316 |
| three-point formula (frozen) | −0.1965 | −0.1083 | −0.0530 | −0.0338 |
| the wave of p alone, all orders (review) | −0.1849 | −0.0978 | −0.0448 | −0.0268 |
| first order = round 142's heuristic | −0.1697 | −0.0824 | −0.0307 | −0.0136 |
| leading order −½·Li₂(1/p), matched | −0.2933 | −0.1843 | −0.1063 | −0.0763 |

Every registered decision passes (all four within 1.1 errors; χ² = 3.0 on 4; amplitude between leading order and the formula 1.027 ± 0.018).

*What this is worth (review).* Most of the agreement is not three-point content. Leading order is one missing step of Selberg's prime sum. The review built the comparator "explicit-formula wave of p plus independence" (round 147's closure: the good spectrum is the missing one warped by p's wave; ln|L′| gains −ln|1 − χ(p)p^(−1/2−iγ)| and the log of the local density): its first order is exactly the formula above, which equals round 142's heuristic, and its second order supplies two thirds of what that heuristic missed. What the three-point law adds beyond this closure is −0.012, −0.011, −0.008, −0.007 (6 % of the drop for p = 2, 21 % for p = 7). The data carry 0.79 ± 0.14 of that part here and 1.14 ± 0.09 on round 142's heights (retrospective), 1.04 ± 0.08 pooled; the closure alone is rejected at χ² = 35.5 on 4. So the three-point law is tested beyond the explicit formula at about 6 errors and to ±14 %, not to ±1.8 %. The all-positive deviations are not significant (round 142's heights deviate the other way; no trend with L). Corrections: the registration text writes the statistic with ln(qγ/2π) where the code subtracts ln ln(qγ/2π) (with the text's version: amplitude 1.030 ± 0.018, no decision changes); the two L-functions excluded by the completeness rule are complete (with them: 1.029 ± 0.018); trimming the lowest 0.1 % of |L′| moves the p = 2 reading by 1.7 errors, so close pairs carry part of the drop; the inputs of the prediction scripts were missing from the repository and are now in [lowzero/varlp/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/varlp/). *In print:* the central limit theorem for ln|ζ′(ρ)| with variance ½·ln ln T (Hejhal 1989; Çiçek 2021, under RH and a spacing hypothesis). Not found: lower-order or arithmetic terms of this variance, the first-order formula, or a use of the three-point function for it.

**Round 145: moments of low zeros over all characters mod q, beyond the proved range: a measurement, no verdict** ([round145.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round145.txt), pre-registered; derivation and scripts [mock/](writeup/code/study/mock/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/mock/derivation.md)); readings [r145/read_out.txt](writeup/data/study/r145/read_out.txt); *not reviewed*). Hughes and Rudnick (2003) proved that over the characters mod a prime q the m-th moment of a linear statistic of the low zeros, Σ_j f(γ_j·ln q/2π) with f̂ supported in [−σ, σ], is Gaussian for σ < 2/m, and conjectured the random-matrix (unitary) moments beyond; no numerical test beyond that range was found in print. The statistic needs no zeros: by the explicit formula it is a prime sum, and one FFT over the residues mod q gives it for every character (checked against direct sums to 10⁻¹⁴ and against the zeros of all characters mod 101 and 151 to 10⁻⁸; the gamma term printed in the paper has a misprint, the correct one is ln(q/π) + Re ψ(1/4 + a/2 + ir/2)). At finite q the cumulants split exactly: products of prime powers that are equal as integers give the cumulants of independent phases χ(p) (strongly non-Gaussian at these q: κ₃/κ₂^1.5 = −0.24 at σ = 0.4 for q ≈ 10⁴); pairs that differ by less than q give an exact deficit −1/(q − 2) per term; what is left, S_m, comes from pairs that wrap around the modulus and is the only place where correlations of Λ and Λ∗Λ in progressions mod q, hence the random-matrix term, can enter. The unitary prediction for the third cumulant vanishes up to σ = 1, not only up to 2/3 (from Soshnikov's support condition). *Measured* on 16 fresh primes q from 3·10⁴ to 10⁶, with f̂ a triangle: (T0) inside the proved range S_m = 0 to 10⁻⁵, as it must. (T1) Beyond it: S₃ is not zero on (2/3, 1]: +0.00037 at σ = 0.9 and +0.00099 at σ = 1.0 pooled, and S₃·ln q is 0.011, 0.014, 0.014, 0.013 at q ≈ 3·10⁴, 10⁵, 3·10⁵, 10⁶: a term falling like 1/ln q, which neither confirms nor contradicts a limit of zero. (T3) For the fourth cumulant at σ = 1.2–1.4 a fit S₄ = α + β/ln q gives α = −0.049, −0.079, −0.119 against the unitary −0.020, −0.040, −0.068: neither registered hypothesis ("converges like 1/ln q to the unitary value", "the pilot's deviation persists") fits. The registered noise model was far too wide (the scatter between moduli of one size is 10–30 times smaller than its errors), so the errors quoted in the output are upper bounds. *Verdict:* no test of the conjecture at these q: the finite-q terms are as large as the random-matrix term and fall only like 1/ln q, and the lower-order terms of the 3- and 4-level densities that a test would need were not found in print. What stands is the exact bookkeeping and the measured S_m, usable when that theory exists.

**Round 143: the swapped term, tested blind: a Hardy–Littlewood statement about pairs of primes, seen through the zeros** ([round143.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round143.txt), pre-registered; derivation, frozen predictions and test in [lowzero/swap2/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/swap2/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/swap2/derivation.md), swap2_test.py); readings [r143/readings_all.txt](writeup/data/study/r143/readings_all.txt); forty-seventh review with its own code [review47/](writeup/code/study/review47/)). The statistic of round 141's review: X₁₂ = (1/T)·Σ_(γ₁, γ₂) e^(2iθ₁(γ₁) − 2iθ₂(γ₂))·K(γ₂ − γ₁) over the zeros of two L-functions, K a Gaussian of width σ; the recipe's swapped term gives E X₁₂ = (1/4π²)·κ₁κ₂·ε₁ε̄₂·∫H(u)K(u)du with κ = q/φ(q), ε the root numbers and H(u) = |L(1 − iu, ψ)|²·A₁₂(−iu), ψ = χ₁χ̄₂. Predictions frozen per pair (H to 4·10⁻⁵ against exact L-values). *Test:* the 546 real L-functions of rounds 117–136 among themselves (148,785 pairs, never used for this statistic), the same L-functions at heights 600–1,000, and the 88 complex characters of round 121 (both half-lines, with 60 real ones: 25,725 pairs).

| reading (σ = 0.5) | measured | law |
|---|---|---|
| real pairs, slope of X on the law | 1.0002 ± 0.0031 | 1 |
| split: the part with H ≡ 1 / the part H − 1 | 0.997 ± 0.007 / 1.0008 ± 0.0034 | 1 / 1 |
| five bins of \|ln(q₂/q₁)\| from 0 to above 1 | 0.998 to 1.004 (errors 0.004) | 1 |
| imaginary part | 0.002 ± 0.002 | 0 |
| heights 600–1,000 (15,931 pairs) | 0.993 ± 0.007 | 1 |
| complex characters, complex slope | 1.008 − 0.001i ± 0.006 | 1 |
| complex: coefficient of the predictor without root numbers | −0.001 ± 0.005 | 0 |
| complex: the part from Im J (the direction of the pairing) | 1.014 ± 0.014 | 1 (reversed: −1) |
| the 87 pairs (χ, χ̄): zeros at +t against zeros at −t | 1.004 ± 0.032 | 1 |

At σ = 1 the same (1.0010 ± 0.0039 for real pairs). The one-point sums: Σ_γ e^(2iθ_χ(γ)) = −ε_χ·κ·T/2π with slope 0.998 ± 0.003 for real and 1.013 ± 0.007 for complex characters (0.22 without the root number), and for every L-function the sum over zeros equals a sum over primes near n = qγ/2π to 0.2 in 40.

*What this is worth (review).* The statistic is a functional of the primes: X₁₂ = (σ/√2π)·∫e^(−σ²ξ²/2)·G₁(ξ)·conj G₂(ξ)dξ exactly, with G_j(ξ) = Σ_γ e^(2iθ_j(γ) − iξγ), and by the explicit formula and stationary phase G_j(ξ) is the prime sum −(i^a/√q)·e^(ξ/2)·Σ_n χ_j(n)Λ(n)·e(−n·e^ξ/q) over a window (the analogue for L(s, χ) of Arias de Reyna's theorem for ζ; computed: 0.45 % rms). From primes alone (n ≤ 3.3·10⁷, no zero used) the review obtained X for 300 pairs within 2·10⁻⁴ of the zeros' values, against a scatter about the law of 0.048: the zeros add nothing. So X₁₂ is a sum over pairs of prime powers with q₂n₁ − q₁n₂ small, weighted by χ₁(n₁)χ̄₂(n₂)Λ(n₁)Λ(n₂). Its mean has two parts. *Lines* at rational detunings, each the product of two prime-number-theorem-in-progressions means (theorem for fixed q): they give the whole shape of the law: the H ≡ 1 term is the product of the two one-point sums (coefficient 1.003 ± 0.003 when the measured sums are used; this also removes the 0.8 % excess of the complex slope), the lines at ±ln 2 carry 2ψ(2) and ψ̄(2)/2 (measured 2.04 ± 0.14 and 0.43 ± 0.05), the root numbers come from Gauss sums, and the law is negative exactly when ψ(2) = −1. And a *continuum*, which the law says has mean zero: χ₁Λ and χ₂Λ are uncorrelated on near-collinear pairs beyond the Hardy–Littlewood singular series of the two linear forms. That is the conjectural content: a twisted Hardy–Littlewood statement with a shift range that does not grow with the height, not known even under GRH and out of reach of Bombieri–Vinogradov or Barban–Davenport–Halberstam (which are linear in Λ). The coefficient of H − 1, 1.0008 ± 0.0034, confirms it to 0.3 % for primes up to about 10⁷; "no term", "half" and "product of one-point terms only" were already excluded by the theorem-level lines, so their rejections by hundreds of errors add nothing. No subset is off by more than 1.4 errors (by ψ(2), conductor size, height half, side of the complex zeros). *Status:* the identity between zeros and primes and the line structure: theorem in substance; the zero-mean continuum: Hardy–Littlewood-type *conjecture*, confirmed numerically to 0.3 %. In print: the same mechanism for one Dirichlet L-function (Bogomolny–Keating 2013) and family-averaged cross correlations (Yıldırım 1991; Kandhil–Languasco–Moree 2024); not found: the cross swapped term with root numbers for distinct moduli, or a numerical test of it. A direct count on primes would test the same statement more cheaply and further. (The registration's manifest lists a hash of freeze.py from before its data path was made relative; the frozen prediction files match their registered hashes.)

**Round 141: two L-functions on the same primes: mostly a theorem, and one term that is not** ([round141.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round141.txt), pre-registered; derivation and frozen predictions [lowzero/cross/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/cross/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/cross/derivation.md)); test rcross.py → rcross_NN.json; forty-sixth review with its own code [review46/](writeup/code/study/review46/)). Two L-functions are two spectra on the same orbits, the orbit p^m carrying the phase χ(p)^m; so their zero counts in a common window should be correlated through every prime. For distinct real characters, with δn_j the number of zeros of L(s, χ_j) in (t, t + w] minus its smooth value and ψ = χ₁χ₂,

    Cov(δn₁, δn₂)(w) = (2/π²)·Σ_p Σ_m Re[ψ(p)^m]·sin²(m·w·ln p/2)/(m²·p^m)   (+ a "swapped" term, below):

characters that agree at p give positively correlated counts, characters that disagree negatively correlated ones (correlation coefficients up to 0.25 for p = 2, 0.16 for 3, 0.10 for 5). *Test (blind):* the 185 complete sets of round 138, all 17,020 pairs, the pair covariances regressed on the per-prime predictors, errors by a jackknife over L-functions. Amplitudes: 0.9992 ± 0.0009 (p = 2), 0.9978 ± 0.0011 (3), 1.0002 ± 0.0020 (5), 0.9999 ± 0.0023 (7); second traversals 1.000 ± 0.009 and 1.030 ± 0.015; primes above 7: 1.0018 ± 0.0017; the full law per pair 0.9996 ± 0.0004; the profile in w for p = 2 and 3: χ² 8.4 and 22.2 on 16. The registered law passes; "independent spectra" is 2,300 errors away. *What this is worth (review):* it is a theorem, not a test of a conjecture. The window count is S_j(t + w) − S_j(t), and by the explicit formula every L-function carries, at each frequency ln n below its Heisenberg frequency ln(qt/2π), a line of amplitude −(2/π)·χ_j(n)·Λ(n)·sin(w·ln n/2)/(√n·ln n) (Landau's formula); the long-range covariance of two such counts is the sum over common lines. The explicit formula cut at n ≤ 10⁴, with no zero used, run through the registered regression gives 0.9992, 0.9986, 1.0005, 0.9994 and 0.9998 for the same readings, and it reproduces the small negative intercepts (−2.4·10⁻⁴ against −2.2·10⁻⁴ measured). Primes beyond the Heisenberg range carry 0.3 % of the law's spread at w = 0.3 and nothing above. So these readings verify the zero lists and the Kronecker symbols to 0.04–0.2 %, and rejecting independent spectra restates the explicit formula; the registration was wrong to place conjectural content in the full-law and small-window readings.

*The swapped term* is the conjectural part: the ratios recipe adds, for two different L-functions, a term with the phase e^(2i(θ₂(t₂) − θ₁(t₁))) and the factor H(ε) = L(1 − iε, ψ)·L(1 + iε, ψ̄)·A₁₂, the cross version of the Bogomolny–Keating off-diagonal term; in equal windows it survives the average over t only for nearly equal conductors ("two spectra locked to nearly the same Gram lattice"). Registered reading on the 3,044 near pairs: coefficient 0.517 ± 0.022 against 1. It is there (24 errors from zero) and the registered law fails. The review traced the failure to the predictor, not to the constant: derived again by two routes (the recipe's two mixed configurations, and the product of two one-point terms), the constant is the registered one; but the frozen predictor integrated H numerically over the window and so picked up H's own oscillating content near the Heisenberg frequency, which lies beyond the recipe's accuracy and is not in the data (its coefficient per width fell from 0.87 to 0.31). *Looking back* (the failure was known, so none of this is a test): with H's smooth part only, the registered regression gives 0.962 ± 0.033, flat over the widths; a statistic that cancels the phase exactly, X₁₂ = (1/T)·Σ e^(2iθ₁(γ₁) − 2iθ₂(γ₂))·K(γ₂ − γ₁) over pairs of zeros with a Gaussian K, uses every pair of L-functions whatever their conductors and gives 1.004 ± 0.007 of the recipe's value over all 17,020 pairs (R² 0.91; H replaced by 1: R² 0.12), with the right sign where ψ(2) = −1 (−0.0705 against −0.0699). Also measured: the one-point term of each L-function, Σ_(100 < γ ≤ 600) e^(2iθ_χ(γ)) = (1.008 ± 0.007)·(−(k/φ(k))·500/2π), the analogue for L(s, χ) of a theorem for ζ (Arias de Reyna 2014). *Status:* the diagonal law is a consequence of the explicit formula (verified to 0.04 %); the swapped term at short distance is the open test, retrospectively at 1.004 ± 0.007, and a registered re-test on pairs not yet used for it (the 546 earlier sets among themselves, and the complex characters, where the root numbers enter) is the next step. In print: the leading-order independence of distinct L-functions (Rudnick–Sarnak 1996; Bogomolny–Leboeuf 1994 set the cross term to zero); no numerical test of a cross term on zeros was found.

**Round 140: the faster fall is Hardy–Littlewood at all orders: no anomaly** ([round140.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round140.txt), pre-registered with model values frozen in [allorder/reg_numbers.json](writeup/code/study/allorder/reg_numbers.json); derivation [allorder/derivation.md](writeup/code/study/allorder/derivation.md); instrument of round 139; [r140.py](writeup/code/study/r140.py) → [r140/readings.json](writeup/data/study/r140/readings.json); *not yet reviewed*). Round 139 left one thing standing: for consecutive primes the within part falls with the block length 1.4 to 2 times faster than the linear-response comparator truncated at 17. Worked out without the shortcut: (a) the within directions are doubly centred, so every class-level four-point term of Hardy–Littlewood vanishes in every direction, Jacobi-sum pairings included (*proved*; checked to 10⁻¹⁶): no direction type can be special, which is what round 139 found; (b) the comparator does not converge in its truncation: every further prime p adds response power about 0.3/p, the same in all directions and for q = 3, 5, 7, and it switches on inside H = 10⁴–10⁶, so the first-order sum over all primes is a closed expression, slope = comparator − 0.625·κ/λ; (c) that first-order sum overshoots, and the answer needs all orders. The all-order statement has no formula here: it was computed with a local model (a number with no prime factor up to P is taken as prime independently, with probability ∏_(p ≤ P)(1 − 1/p)⁻¹/λ; P = 10⁵; random 120-digit starting points). That is a sieve model, used as the comparator that says whether the deficit is ordinary; it explains nothing by itself. *Test:* 24 fresh windows at 2.40·10¹¹–2.424·10¹¹ (λ = 26.2), bands from measurement errors only.

| cell (q, k) | measured slope | model | z | comparator at 17 | first order |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7, 1 | −0.0064 ± 0.0010 | −0.0075 | +1.1 | −0.0043 | −0.0097 |
