| 9, 1 | −0.0060 ± 0.0012 | −0.0067 | +0.6 | −0.0039 | −0.0080 |
| 11, 1 | −0.0073 ± 0.0007 | −0.0070 | −0.6 | −0.0047 | −0.0084 |
| 13, 1 | −0.0062 ± 0.0005 | −0.0065 | +0.6 | −0.0047 | −0.0080 |
| 11, 2 | −0.0020 ± 0.0007 | −0.0031 | +1.5 | −0.0016 | −0.0034 |
| 13, 2 | −0.0028 ± 0.0006 | −0.0026 | −0.3 | −0.0018 | −0.0033 |

All six readings pass (joint χ² 4.7 on 6); the levels at H = 10⁴ agree to 0.0003. Pooled over the four k = 1 cells the measured slope is 5.6 errors below the comparator, 5.3 errors above the first-order sum, and 0.7 errors from the model. So the deficit of rounds 137 and 139 is what Hardy–Littlewood gives when every prime is taken at every order; the truncated pair-correlation comparator and the first-order sum both fail, on opposite sides. The thread that began with the 3.9-error cell of round 135 ends here: three effects were chased (that cell, the mixed directions mod 5, the common deficit); the first two were chance or selection, the third is ordinary. *Open:* a formula for the all-order slope (the higher orders nearly halve the first-order excess; why is not derived).

**Round 139: the mixed-direction excess mod 5 was a selection effect; a deficit common to all directions remains** ([round139.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round139.txt), pre-registered; instrument [map/map.py](writeup/code/study/map/map.py) with its validation and null checks, readings applied by [map/evaluate.py](writeup/code/study/map/evaluate.py) → [r139/readings.txt](writeup/data/study/r139/readings.txt), report [r139/rep_fresh.txt](writeup/data/study/r139/rep_fresh.txt)). The forty-fourth review had placed the too-fast fall of the within part mod 5 in the four directions that mix the order-4 character with the quadratic one (−0.0083 ± 0.0021 per unit ln H per direction over two heights; −0.0089 ± 0.0017 with the instrument of this round). An instrument was built that measures the fall of every direction χ_i(p_n)·conj χ_j(p_(n+k)) for q = 3, 5, 7, 8, 9, 11, 12, 13 against the linear-response comparator (it reproduces the review's 384 numbers to 10⁻¹⁵; a first-order chain, a permutation control and lag 50 all give zero excess), and the question was registered for 24 fresh windows at 3.0·10¹⁰–3.24·10¹⁰ with bands from measurement errors only. *Result:* mod 5, k = 1, mixed directions: measured slope −0.0050 ± 0.0030, comparator −0.0048, excess −0.0003 ± 0.0030. The primary reading fails and the registered falsifier (an excess within one error of zero) fires; the contrast with the other five directions is +0.0014 ± 0.0041. So the pattern "mixed directions mod 5" was picked out of many directions on seen data and is not a property of the primes. Of the six other cells selected on seen data, four keep their sign at two errors or more (mod 11: equal, mixed and complex types, −0.0043 ± 0.0013, −0.0040 ± 0.0014, −0.0038 ± 0.0009; mod 13 complex −0.0025 ± 0.0006) and two do not (mod 8, mod 9 conjugate). What they share is not a direction type: at k = 1 the whole within part falls faster than the comparator, per direction −0.0055 ± 0.0012 for mod 7 (slope twice the comparator's), −0.0030 ± 0.0009 for mod 9, −0.0036 ± 0.0006 for mod 11, −0.0021 ± 0.0004 for mod 13 (no reading was registered on these sums), while mod 5 gives −0.0010 ± 0.0019 and k = 2 and lag 50 show nothing. The comparator is not converged in its truncation (it grows by 6–33 % per step from 11 to 13 to 17), so this common deficit may be the missing primes above 17 in the comparator; that is *open*, and an exact calculation without truncation is in progress. *Lesson:* a 5-error effect in one of many cells of a map made on seen data is a candidate, not a finding; the registered re-test decides.

**Round 136: the amplitude is 1; round 134's 0.93 was a low sample** ([round136.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round136.txt), pre-registered with thresholds set from the real errors; derivation and frozen predictions in [lowzero/amp/](writeup/code/study/lowzero/amp/) ([derivation.md](writeup/code/study/lowzero/amp/derivation.md)); [lowzero/r136z.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r136z.py), [lowzero/r136.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r136.py) → r136_C.json, r136_Dn.json, [lowzero/r136d.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/r136d.py) → r136d.json; when the registration was committed 39 of test C's 181 zero sets had been computed and no statistic). Round 134 left the off-diagonal amplitude at 0.93 ± 0.02 of the predicted one. *No formula gives that.* Worked out one by one: the ratios-conjecture two-point function for a primitive χ mod k (the Conrey–Farmer–Zirnbauer recipe, as Conrey and Snaith apply it to ζ) equals Bogomolny–Keating's term by term, bad-prime factor included, to 10⁻¹² numerically, and its gamma-factor ratio has modulus 1, so no lower-order term changes the amplitude; the exact smooth count is N̄(t) = u(t) + (2a − 1)/8 + 1/(48πt) + O(t⁻³) with u the unfolding used, so the unfolding is off by 3.5·10⁻⁶ spacings across eight spacings and the phase by ε/(24t²); the exponent of a prime dividing k does not enter; each of these moves the amplitude by at most 10⁻⁴ (*derived*, [amp/theory_out.txt](writeup/code/study/lowzero/amp/theory_out.txt)). *Looking back* (known data, no test): the same statistic on the 181 L-functions of rounds 117 and 120 at the same heights gives γ = 1.001 ± 0.019, and inside test B the part of the off-diagonal pattern that the prime indicators do not carry has slope 1.042 ± 0.024 against 0.938 ± 0.022 for the indicator part ([amp/decomp_out.txt](writeup/code/study/lowzero/amp/decomp_out.txt)). *The test:* decide between amplitude 1 and 0.932 by the pooled slope of Σ² − diagonal on full − diagonal, with the line at 0.966 (registered error rate 0.5 % each way for the two tests together). Test C, the 181 discriminants of round 120 at the new heights 600–1,000 (118,448 zeros in the band): pooled slope 0.986 ± 0.018, γ = 0.999 ± 0.027. Test Dn, all 181 fundamental discriminants with 700 < |D| ≤ 1,000 at heights 100–600 (153,467 zeros): pooled slope 0.998 ± 0.018, γ = 1.019 ± 0.029. Together 0.992 ± 0.013: 4.7 errors from 0.932 and 0.6 from 1. So on fresh data the amplitude is the predicted one; the variant with the off-diagonal term at L + 0.3 is rejected with it (against its own pattern the data give γ = 1.08 and 1.10, not 1); the conductor trend that the four earlier half-samples suggested is gone (γ = 1.018 at the largest conductors); and between and within parts agree in both tests (differences +0.008 and +0.021, 0.2 and 0.5 null errors). The level without a fitted constant is within 0.0006 (C) and 0.0004 (Dn) of the full law at every length, and the diagonal law is rejected again (level χ² 150.2 and 140.0 on 16 lengths against 20.9 and 17.6). Test B's 0.932 ± 0.024 therefore reads as a low excursion of that sample (its own p = 0.003; one of five samples now measured: 1.001, 0.932, 0.943 ± 0.104 for the complex characters, 0.999, 1.019), not as a property of the law. The exact count check added to the registration found one set with two zeros missing: a pair of L(s, χ₋₂₈₃) at 34.6596 and 34.6836 that PARI skipped at its default subdivision, outside the band; it was added and nothing changed. *Corrected after a stricter completeness check* (the count against θ_χ/π at 200 heights and the largest gap, as registered for round 138; [lowzero/repair138.py](writeup/code/study/lowzero/repair138.py)): two more sets missed a close pair inside their band, L(s, χ₋₁₉₅) at 985.574 and 985.600 in test C and L(s, χ₋₉₃₉) at 598.516 and 598.535 in test Dn. The band mean of round 136's own check could not see a pair missing near the top of a band. All numbers above are after the repair; before it test C read 1.000 ± 0.024 (γ = 1.031 ± 0.039) and the two tests together 0.998 ± 0.014, so one missing pair among 118,448 zeros moved a pooled slope by 0.014 and widened its error by a third. The decision is unchanged. The sets of rounds 117, 120 and 134 pass the stricter check (in round 117 the pair of χ₋₂₈₃ below the band was added). Test Bp (test B's own L-functions at the new heights 600–1,000, run later with the zeros of round 138's test Bp): pooled slope 0.981 ± 0.028, γ = 0.986 ± 0.046, and the part carried by the prime indicators, which was 0.938 in test B, is 0.980 ± 0.039 here: the low value belonged to that stretch of zeros, not to those L-functions. *Not yet reviewed.*

**Prime-producing polynomials: searches beyond Euler** (not pre-registered; [polys/](writeup/code/study/polys/): qs.c, qs2.c with the Python twin qs_twin.py, cs.c, comp.py, biq_fields.py, biq2.py). Target: more than 45 distinct prime values |f(0)|, …, |f(N − 1)| from a quadratic (Fung–Ruby's 36n² − 810n + 2753). Every quadratic is A y² + B y + C after a shift (0 < B < A; B = 0 or A is symmetric and repeats values); a run longer than 46 needs that no odd p ≤ 23 divides a value, so the discriminant must be a non-residue mod every such p not dividing A, and p | A needs p | B (Ruby's A = 36 removes 2 and 3; its 259668 = 6²·7213 is a non-residue mod every p from 5 to 53). Searched with that filter (p ≤ 31 for A > 60), runs in y ∈ [−80, 80]: A ≤ 60 with |C| ≤ 10⁸, A ≤ 300 with |C| ≤ 10⁶, A ≤ 1750 with |C| ≤ 3000A. Found: 45 (Fung–Ruby), 43 (47n² − 1701n + 10181) and 43 (103n² − 4707n + 50383, Speiser), nothing longer, and nothing of 42 or more for 300 < A ≤ 1750, where the values grow too large. Three other routes failed: a prime-rich quadratic of an integer-valued quadratic (quartics; best run 37); norms N(n − α) from real biquadratic fields in which no prime below 157 splits (best run 22, because 2 is a common index divisor, so f has a root mod 2 whatever α is, and α must be filtered per prime); monic cubics with |c| ≤ 1500, |d| ≤ 40,000 (no run of 38). So within these ranges the quadratic record of 45 stands (computed, under the stated filter).

**The cubic record explained, and a wider cubic search** ([polys/cs2.c](writeup/code/study/polys/cs2.c)). Kazmenko–Trofimov's −66n³ + 3845n² − 60897n + 251831 (46 distinct primes) is a cubic analogue of Euler's and Ruby's polynomials: modulo every prime 5, 7, 13, 17, …, 43 it is irreducible (the prime is inert in its cubic field), and 2, 3, 11 have one degree-1 prime each whose root sits at infinity because they divide the leading coefficient 66; the first prime that can divide a value is 47. Two facts shrink the search: a run longer than 46 needs a cubic with no root mod every p ≤ 43 (up to values ±p), and an integer-valued cubic never divisible by 2 or 3 has integer coefficients (checked over all residues: 2 | d₁, d₂, d₃ and 3 | d₃ in the Newton basis), so the denominators used by the quintic and sextic records cannot help a cubic. Searched with d enumerated in residue classes mod 30030 (after the shift 0 ≤ b < 3a): a ≤ 122 with c ∈ a·[−300, 50], |d| ≤ 1500a; a ≤ 31 with c ∈ a·[−1500, 300], |d| ≤ 20000a; 31 smooth values of a ≤ 78 with c ∈ a·[−600, 100], |d| ≤ 4000a. The only run above 42 is the record itself (and its mirror); next are 42 (a = 2) and 41 (a = 16, 25, 70); for 91 ≤ a ≤ 122 nothing reaches 41, so larger leading coefficients lose more to the size of the values than they gain in freedom.

All the record polynomials share this structure: no small prime can divide any value, up to a first prime P (computed over full periods, denominators included): n² + n + 41, 40 primes, P = 41; 36n² − 810n + 2753, 45, P = 59; the cubic, 46, P = 47; Beyleveld's quartic, 49, P = 67; Wroblewski–Meyrignac's monic quintic, 47, P = 59; the quintic of Dress–Landreau and Gupta (with /4), 57, P = 79; the sextic (with /36), 55, P = 89. Every value below P² is then automatically prime, and the rest are prime with probability about e^γ ln P / ln |f(n)|, so a record is a trade between a late first prime P and small values.

**Quartic and sextic searches** ([polys/](writeup/code/study/polys/): qq2.c, sx.c, shapeopt.py, shapegen.py, distinct.py, distinct6.py, tail.py; computed, not pre-registered). Which leading coefficients can a polynomial have whose values are never divisible by 2, 3 or 5? In the Newton basis F = Σ d_k C(y, k), Lucas's theorem and an exhaustive check over residues give: degree 3 integer, degree 4 and 5 a multiple of 1/4, degree 6 a multiple of 1/72 (d₆ ≡ 0 mod 10, any residue mod 3). The quintic record uses the minimal 1/4; the sextic record uses 1/36, twice the minimum. Quartics: about 5·10⁶ monic candidates with no root mod every p ≤ 53 or 61 (shift-normalised, |F| ≤ 1.5–3·10⁵ on |y| ≤ 18) give Beyleveld's 49 as the only run above 42; leading coefficient 1/2 gives at best 46 distinct primes ((y⁴ − 733y² + 6708y − 1754)/2); with 1/4 the congruences mod 8 force near-symmetric polynomials whose values repeat. Sextics with 1/72: random root sets give five times more runs of 30 or more than the same search with 1/36; shapes optimised for a heuristic chance (three close pairs of real roots) and searched exhaustively in their two lowest coefficients gave, over about 6·10⁵ tested candidates, at best 43 distinct primes (exactly symmetric polynomials excluded; they reach 50 consecutive values but only 24 distinct). The run-length tail falls by about a factor 0.5 per extra prime, so a 56 is out of reach of this search; the heuristic per-candidate chance (e^γ ln 67/ln|F| per value) overestimates the tail by about three orders of magnitude, which remains unexplained. No record was improved.

**Round 103: runs of prime values of Euler's polynomial** ([round103.txt](writeup/data/study/preregister/round103.txt), pre-registered; [euler41/runs_law.py](writeup/code/study/euler41/runs_law.py) → runs_law.json, [runs_data.py](writeup/code/study/euler41/runs_data.py) → runs_data.json, check [runs_check.py](writeup/code/study/euler41/runs_check.py) with sympy). The run f(0), …, f(39) of f(n) = n² + n + 41 is algebra (−163 is a non-residue mod every p < 41 and f(n) < 41² for n ≤ 39; Rabinowitsch). Beyond it the question is statistical: how many n have f(n), …, f(n + r − 1) all prime? Bateman–Horn for the pattern {0, …, r − 1} gives S_r ∫dn/∏ ln f(n + i), S_r = ∏_p (1 − ν_p/p)/(1 − 1/p)^r with ν_p the number of n mod p at which p divides one of the r values; ν_p = 0 for every p < 41, so S_r grows by a factor 6.72, 6.68, 6.77, 6.73, 6.83, 6.78, 6.88, 6.84, 6.92 per extra value, close to ∏_(p<41) p/(p − 1) = 6.72 (the primes below 41 never divide a value, so each value is a 41-rough number). Observed / expected in n ∈ [10³, 10⁷): r = 1: 2207616 / 2207109 · r = 2: 495994 / 496283 · r = 3: 111742 / 111657 · r = 4: 25609 / 25723 · r = 5: 5843 / 5959 · r = 6: 1393 / 1424 · r = 7: 324 / 346 · r = 8: 75 / 88; all eight pass the rule (3√E + 1 %). The ratio drifts down with r (1.000 to 0.856; the counts share their runs, so the drift is one fluctuation or a finite-size term, not eight). Post hoc, r = 9 and 10: 14 against 23.0 and 1 against 6.4. The longest run in the range is 10 values, from n = 179,856. Bateman–Horn for this pattern is standard; the run counts and their check are ours.

## Validation

**How to reproduce.** Use the repository's virtualenv and run from `writeup/code/study/` under the memory cap of CLAUDE.md, e.g. `(ulimit -v 1500000; timeout 590 nice ../../../.venv/bin/python script.py)`. Several scripts keep pattern caches outside the repository, named by environment variables: `STUDY_CACHE3` (theory_r3.py, also read by r6, r8, r24–r30), `STUDY_CACHE7` (r7.py, r13*), `STUDY_CACHE8` (r8.py), `STUDY_CACHE4`, `STUDY_CACHE5`, `STUDY_CACHE14`, `STUDY_CACHE18`, `STUDY_CACHE26`, `STUDY_CACHE29`, `STUDY_CACHE32` (twins, n² + 1, Sophie Germain, n² + n + 41, rounds 26, 29, 32); each is rebuilt by the script's `build` step in chunks of under ten minutes. Order: `engine.py` (round 1) and `report.py`; `theory_r2.py build/values`, `theory_geo.py`, `report2.py`; `stats_r3.py`, `theory_r3.py build/values`, `report3.py`, `bias_r3.py`; then each round's script as linked in its section, `report_generic.py` for the block tests, `tables.py` for the tables and `validate.py` last. The data needed from outside are the zeros in `writeup/data/riemann/zeros_hp2419.txt` and the two JSON extracts of Oliveira e Silva's table in `writeup/data/study/`.

- [engine.py](writeup/code/study/engine.py) and [stats_r3.py](writeup/code/study/stats_r3.py) compute the statistics of the primes from an exact segmented sieve of each window (checked: π(10⁶) = 78,498; the train count 5,096,876 = π(10⁸) − π(10⁷)).
- [theory_r2.py](writeup/code/study/theory_r2.py), [theory_r3.py](writeup/code/study/theory_r3.py) and [theory_geo.py](writeup/code/study/theory_geo.py) compute every prediction from the singular series alone; [report2.py](writeup/code/study/report2.py), [report3.py](writeup/code/study/report3.py) and [bias_r3.py](writeup/code/study/bias_r3.py) make the verdicts; [tables.py](writeup/code/study/tables.py) prints the tables above.
- [validate.py](writeup/code/study/validate.py) checks that the 18 tables printed by tables.py from the data files appear in this file exactly (the other tables are typed and were checked by the reviews), that 37 numbers quoted in the text match the data (14 of them from rounds 45–62: K, I_∞, J_∞, K_tw, the twin-gap χ² and its baseline, the largest residual below 4·10¹⁸, round 51's mean U, the band counts of rounds 48, 54 and 55, round 50's largest difference), and three identities (S(0, 2) = 2C₂; the residue-conditioned series average to S; S_f({0}) against the Bateman–Horn constant of n² + 1).
- An independent review with its own code (nothing imported from the study scripts; [review/](writeup/code/study/review/), outputs in [data/study/review/](writeup/data/study/review/)) recomputed: the block statistics of window A and C from primes listed by gmpy2.next_prime (all 32 statistics agree exactly), the singular series, the grid variance (0.70556 / 0.77451 / 0.81207), the pair constants of the quadratics (C₁/C² = 0.51751 for 4n² + 1; χ²/curve 0.407 at train), the exact inclusion–exclusion against the Bahadur orders (section 3), the twin-gap shares, the counts of n² + 1 primes and the residue identity. Its corrections are in the text: the discriminant b² − 16c, the error of order 2 at gap 6, the description of version 1, the truncation error, the steps added after seeing data, the Markov-gain misses, the block misses of the n² + 1 round, the wording of section 9, the duplicate curves and the literature.
- A second independent review ([review2/](writeup/code/study/review2/), outputs in [data/study/review2/](writeup/data/study/review2/)) recomputed sections 10–13 and found that the symmetric two-dimensional boxes counted every point twice (corrected in section 11: the Gaussian misses disappear), that the split of the round-12 pair excess by distance is not resolved (only the total with its error is now given), a post hoc probability, overstated wording on rounds 13 and 15, and that the repository cannot by itself prove the pre-registrations preceded their computations (section 1). It confirmed the third cumulant (0.31187 at H = 50 against the study's 0.3119), the long-interval variances, the Gaussian series and exact sums, the Sophie Germain series and exact sums, and the data counts.
- A third independent review ([review3/](writeup/code/study/review3/), outputs in [data/study/review3/](writeup/data/study/review3/)) recomputed rounds 17–22 and the scoreboard: the 1,436,154 admissible sets for H = 60 and the sums T_j (to 2·10⁻⁸), the data side of rounds 21 and 22 with its own sieve, the 10¹⁸ window (48,427 primes, all 18 statistics), the counts and the series of n² + n + 41, and the jumping-champion counts at 10⁸. Its corrections are in the text: the ledger after round 21 replaces round 20, the 8 round-18 tests excluded after the run, seven rather than eight digits for the crossing, the telescoping identity behind the tail, and the convergence numbers for round 20.
- A fourth independent review ([review4/](writeup/code/study/review4/), outputs in [data/study/review4/](writeup/data/study/review4/)) recomputed the polymer correction (its sign and factor, checked on a synthetic hard-core gas where the error falls to third order), all 35 laws of round 23 (to 2·10⁻¹⁰), the U_g of rounds 24–25 and the numbers of rounds 26–27. Its corrections are in the text: the large-gap result is a scale error of about 27 % with an exponential shape, not a shape error; dependence between first occurrences is excluded; round 27 does not locate the discrepancy; "within √N" became "of the size of √N"; and the ledger counts the repeated large-gap test once.
- A fifth independent review ([review5/](writeup/code/study/review5/), outputs in [data/study/review5/](writeup/data/study/review5/)) recomputed rounds 28–31: the closed form of S({0, 2, d, d + 2}), the twin-count variances with its own sieve (and the bias of the sliding estimator under the law), the large-gap counts at 10⁹, the exact sums for gaps 74 and 82, and the per-class twin spread from the four-point sums. Its corrections are in the text: the ledger's open points, the scope of "to their own scatter" (gaps up to 70), round 30 read as inconclusive, the order-3 caveat, the per-class rigidity computed rather than asserted.
- A sixth independent review ([review6/](writeup/code/study/review6/), outputs in [data/study/review6/](writeup/data/study/review6/)) recomputed rounds 32–35: the block counts with its own sieve, the explicit-formula predictions to 0.1 prime, the order-4 and order-5 terms, and the round-34 numbers. Its corrections are in the text: the noise of round 35 (1.3–1.8 times too large before), round 35 as a check of a theorem outside the law's scoreboard, the Montgomery–Soundararajan scale for the block swings, the large-gap wording (at least partly), the range of the extrapolation, the exact χ²₁₄ error model for the class spreads (marginal), and the round-34 wording.
- A seventh review ([review7/](writeup/code/study/review7/), outputs in [data/study/review7/](writeup/data/study/review7/)) recomputed rounds 37–39 with its own code: the order-4 and order-5 terms for g = 182 and 200, all U summaries, the round-39 correlation products (brute force over all primes to 7·10⁵) and predictions (own sieve to 9·10⁷), and the cell count of the tables (449 cells, Σ(1 − P) = 401 = dof). Its corrections are in the text: the null standard error for the mean of U (borderline, not insignificant), the extrapolated trend for the multiples of 6, "half explained" at train for round 39, the noise model of its χ², and the spread of the shortfall over the gap classes.
- An eighth review ([review8/](writeup/code/study/review8/), outputs in [data/study/review8/](writeup/data/study/review8/)) recounted the twins, the gap-6 pairs and the primes per class with its own sieve (45,086,078 primes and 2,984,194 twins in [10⁸, 10⁹)), derived the twin shares from its own Hardy–Littlewood product, confirmed that the law's shares are not fitted to the data (so calibration errors can only add to u²) and checked the class-≥ 40 arithmetic. Its corrections are in the text: the null error of the pooled u² (±0.055, not the scatter over gaps), the calibration-free comparison that makes the two long windows alike, the twins as the firmest piece (also at 10¹⁰), the multinomial scale for the long-gap rows, and the independence of rows assumed in every error of rounds 40–41.
- A ninth review ([review9/](writeup/code/study/review9/), outputs in [data/study/review9/](writeup/data/study/review9/)) recounted all twins per class mod 210, 2310 and 30030 in the six windows with its own sieve (all 18 spreads to 4 decimals), recomputed c, ρ and v (to 6·10⁻⁵ in the short windows), checked the expected spread (the neglected total term is +0.35 %) and the noise (cross-class correlations widen it by about 1 %; 90 short windows scatter as predicted). Its corrections are in the text: the decomposition of the mod-30030 statistic and the split by level, the mod-210 secondary in [10⁹, 10¹⁰) that the run had left out, and "unresolved at about 2σ" instead of "weak".
- A tenth review ([review10/](writeup/code/study/review10/), [count5.out](writeup/data/study/review10/count5.out)) recounted the twins of [5·10⁹, 6·10⁹) per class (equal to 7 digits), checked round 44's level decomposition (9 subclasses per class) and read the summary layer against the round texts. Its corrections are in the text: the number of reviews and which tables are typed, the rounds left out of the scoreboard, the extra scatter between windows of 10⁹, the split of [10⁹, 10¹⁰) into within- and cross-window parts (recomputed in r44b.py), and stale or overstated sentences in the In short, the seven-questions table and the synthesis.
- An eleventh review ([review11/](writeup/code/study/review11/), outputs in [data/study/review11/](writeup/data/study/review11/)) checked rounds 45–48 with its own code: the conditioned identity is exact (to 10⁻¹⁴ against the full inclusion–exclusion for small gaps), the order-3 and overlap formulas and r47's numbers reproduce, all 90 gap counts of [10⁹, 2·10⁹) and round 48's expected counts agree, and 18 values of round 45 were recomputed. Its corrections are in the text: rounds 47–48 had not re-run the registered large-gap test (round 51 does; with rounds 54–55 the point is read as accounted for, with a 1.9σ remainder), "settled" and "closed" were too strong, the u-only form of the predictability law is not the 0.001-accurate one and the law is a heuristic product, round 38's order 5 had not over-corrected, the scope of the 0.04 % and 0.1 % figures, and the rounds left out of the scoreboard.
- A twelfth review ([review12/](writeup/code/study/review12/), outputs in [data/study/review12/](writeup/data/study/review12/)) built its own conditioned law and matched r53's order-3 corrections to 10⁻¹⁰ and r51's to 10⁻⁹, confirmed the integration against Oliveira e Silva's counts, that primes above 400 cannot collide for g ≤ 800, K to 8 digits and the measured entropies. Its corrections are in the text: a leftover coarse-grid table, the remaining numerical bias of the λ grid and the order-3 interpolation (now direct quadrature, r53g.py), the same bias in round 49 (r49g.py) and round 47, the registered run's 18 of 20, "about 40 %" instead of "half", and how round 52's reading was applied.
- A thirteenth review ([review13/](writeup/code/study/review13/), outputs in [data/study/review13/](writeup/data/study/review13/)) recounted all gaps ≥ 200 in [10¹⁰, 2·10¹⁰) and [10⁸, 10⁹) with its own sieve (equal to rounds 54 and 55), confirmed the integrations, I_∞ to 4·10⁻¹⁷ against enumeration and the measured mutual information. Its corrections are in the text: the largest round-53 residual (0.063 % at g = 640), the scoreboard sentence about the extra rounds, the estimator bias at C and the discrete-law origin of round 56's 1/λ term, and "explained, with a 1.9σ remainder" instead of "closed" for the large gaps.
- A fourteenth review ([review14/](writeup/code/study/review14/), outputs in [data/study/review14/](writeup/data/study/review14/)) recomputed J_∞ two ways and the round-58 data exactly, and checked round 57's asymptote and truncation. Its corrections are in the text: how round 57's difference approaches the asymptote, the discrete law's truncation in round 58, the estimator bias (a conditional shuffle control), the c/p³ tail, the timing of round 58's pre-registration, and the summary bullets.
- A fifteenth review ([review15/](writeup/code/study/review15/)) rebuilt round 59's law (to 5·10⁻⁷) and checked it against the exact inclusion–exclusion with the full singular series, recounted the twins (2,963,535) and the bands. Its corrections are in the text: the measured order-2 truncation behind the tail miss, the trend beyond 600, the earlier literature on twin gaps, the fairer pair-factor baseline, and the band choices made after the pre-registration.
- A sixteenth review ([review16/](writeup/code/study/review16/), outputs in [data/study/review16/](writeup/data/study/review16/)) checked round 61's formula against brute-force enumeration of the local model (to 10⁻¹⁴), recomputed its table and round 62's model and data on a sub-range, and round 60's constant and entropy.
